MAGAHEADS: THREE OFFICIALS, ONE CANADA MESSAGE

Navarro, Bessent and Lutnick used different venues and registers on Sept. 2, but repeated the same public account: Ottawa walked away from a good deal for political reasons and will return on American terms.

MAGAHEADS: THREE OFFICIALS, ONE CANADA MESSAGE Current page September 3, 2026 Navarro, Bessent and Lutnick used different venues and registers on Sept. 2, but repeated the same public account: Ottawa walked away from a good deal for political reasons and will return on American terms. MAGAHEADS · September 2 · Daily briefing MAGAHEADS: THREE OFFICIALS, ONE CANADA MESSAGE Across C-SPAN, Fox News, CNBC and Bloomberg on Sept. 2, senior Trump administration figures offered a remarkably consistent account: Ottawa rejected a good deal for political reasons, Washington can wait, and Canada will eventually return on American terms. Updated Sept. 2, 2026 · 10:41 p.m. ET The image above is an AI-generated editorial illustration. Times below are Eastern. 7:00 a.m. — Peter Navarro, C-SPAN’s Washington Journal . C-SPAN’s schedule lists the White House trade adviser’s 35-minute appearance in the 7 a.m. hour. Navarro said the collapse of the Canada talks had one cause: “politics across our border.” He called Prime Minister Mark Carney “the worst thing that happened to Canada” and said the prime minister was leading the country “into the toilet.” The Canadian Press reported the remarks from the program. 8:45 a.m. — Scott Bessent, Fox News’s Fox & Friends . The Associated Press’s live coverage logged the Treasury secretary’s Canada comments at 8:45 a.m. Bessent said the Canadian tariff dispute had “virtually no impact on American prices,” blamed Carney for stopping a deal that had been on the “1-yard line,” and said the prime minister “will have to come back.” He suggested a later American offer would not be as favourable. The morning sweep first verified those two Canada-specific televised appearances. The times are reported with the level of precision the public record supports: Navarro’s program began at 7:00 a.m.; the AP’s contemporaneous live entry time-stamped Bessent’s Canada remarks at 8:45 a.m. No exact minute is asserted where the originating broadcaster has not published one. Then Lutnick put the same claim in a third register Howard Lutnick — CNBC’s Squawk Box and Bloomberg’s Balance of Power . CNBC released excerpts from Lutnick’s Sept. 2 interview. He said Canadian negotiators had begun “adding crazy ideas to the mix” late on the Friday the talks failed; he said he asked one minister whether Canada was trying to blow up the agreement and concluded that it had done so “for political reasons only.” Bloomberg Television later summarized his Canada remark in the same terms: Canada had walked away from a trade deal at the last minute for political reasons. The Bloomberg interview was published as a 14-minute conversation with Joe Mathieu from the G20 Innovation Ministerial in Chapel Hill, North Carolina. Its Canada segment concerned the state of the negotiations; the publisher’s public description does not supply a wall-clock time for the live exchange. Howard Lutnick, Jan. 17, 2025. U.S. Department of Commerce photograph by Daniel Torok; public domain. The sequence is significant for what it does and does not establish. Navarro used personal and political language. Bessent used the language of prices, leverage and a near-completed deal. Lutnick supplied the clearest account of alleged Canadian intent. The three statements repeat the same basic proposition: Canada rejected something reasonable for politics; the United States can carry the dispute more easily; and time will force Ottawa back to the table on Washington’s terms. That repeated framing is the day’s observable communications pattern. The public record does not reveal whether the appearances were centrally coordinated, and it would be wrong to claim that it does. It does show senior officials, in different venues, reinforcing a single explanation of the collapse while offering no released negotiating text and no new public route back to an agreement. That proposition does not turn on a released negotiating text. None of the officials made one public. It is a public account of what happened, and it leaves out the central Canadian objection: that the late-stage terms would have weakened Canadian industries, constrained Canada’s ability to make future trade arrangements and made Canadian firms more dependent on American counterparts. Carney gave that account after the talks collapsed. He said Canada was not prepared to accept terms that would turn Canadian industries into subsidiaries of U.S. companies or leave them to be gradually wound down in Canada. The government’s Aug. 21 statement set out the same basic distinction: a deal is not automatically acceptable simply because a deadline is near. What the morning message leaves out Bessent’s assertion about the national U.S. price index is narrower than a claim that tariffs impose no costs. A tariff bill is paid first by importers. Firms can absorb it, pass some of it to buyers, renegotiate contracts or find another supplier. The effect differs by product, region and supply chain. A small national price effect can coexist with a material disruption for particular companies, workers and consumers. The claim that Canada has more to lose is also a statement of leverage, not a complete account of outcome. Canada is more exposed to the United States market; the relationship is also deeply integrated in autos, metals, energy, food and manufacturing. The policy question is not whether pressure exists. It is whether the terms on offer justify the economic and sovereign constraints Canada says they carried. The White House made the political framing explicit in a statement to Axios, calling Canada a “bad neighbor” while defending the president’s actions. That statement was not a television appearance, but it fits the day’s public line: the conflict is framed not as a disagreement over particular sectoral rules, but as evidence that Canada chose confrontation over a supposedly generous American offer. The day also supplied the operating context for that line. Canada’s announced retaliatory tariffs are due to take effect Sept. 8, while Lutnick met Mexico’s economy minister at the G20 gathering over auto and steel tariffs. Those are connected North American disputes, but they are not proof that the Bloomberg appearance was a response to that meeting. No direct Canadian government response specifically to the Bloomberg remarks was located by publication time. The important reporting distinction is between the fact of the repeated statements and the propositions inside them. The broadcasts establish a shared public account of the impasse being delivered by senior officials. They do not, by themselves, prove that account is correct. MAGAHEADS is updated as additional verified Canada-related appearances are identified. Sources C-SPAN — Peter Navarro on Trump administration trade policy C-SPAN schedule — Sept. 2 Washington Journal listing Associated Press — live coverage of Bessent’s Fox & Friends Canada remarks Canadian Press report, republished by Castanet — Navarro’s Canada remarks CNBC — Sept. 2 excerpts from Howard Lutnick’s Squawk Box interview Bloomberg Talks — Howard Lutnick on AI and U.S.–Canada trade Bloomberg Television — Sept. 2 summary of Lutnick’s Canada remark Reuters — Mexico’s meeting with Lutnick on auto and steel tariffs Prime Minister’s Office — Aug. 21 statement on Canada–U.S. trade negotiations Axios — White House statement and U.S. polling on Canada Wikimedia Commons — Department of Commerce public-domain Lutnick portrait

A SASKATCHEWAN COPPER-ZINC MINE IS NOW PART OF CANADA’S NATIONAL-SECURITY STRATEGY

McIlvenna Bay is ramping toward commercial production after major federal backing and an Eldorado acquisition. Ottawa explicitly links its copper and zinc to national security, advanced manufacturing and supply-chain resilience.

A SASKATCHEWAN COPPER-ZINC MINE IS NOW PART OF CANADA’S NATIONAL-SECURITY STRATEGY Current page September 3, 2026 McIlvenna Bay is ramping toward commercial production after major federal backing and an Eldorado acquisition. Ottawa explicitly links its copper and zinc to national security, advanced manufacturing and supply-chain resilience. Copper ore. 787IYO / Wikimedia Commons, CC0. Critical minerals · Saskatchewan · Industrial policy · National security A SASKATCHEWAN COPPER-ZINC MINE IS NOW PART OF CANADA’S NATIONAL-SECURITY STRATEGY McIlvenna Bay is ramping toward commercial production after major federal backing and an Eldorado acquisition. Ottawa explicitly links its copper and zinc to national security, advanced manufacturing and supply-chain resilience. Published Sept. 2, 2026 · 9:23 p.m. ET Ottawa is describing a new Saskatchewan copper-zinc mine in terms that would once have sounded more at home in a defence briefing than a mining announcement. Natural Resources Canada said Wednesday that production is under way at the McIlvenna Bay mine in the Flin Flon Greenstone Belt and explicitly tied the project to national security, advanced manufacturing, clean-energy technologies and stronger supply chains. The mine is not suddenly beginning from zero on Sept. 2. Eldorado Gold, which acquired Foran Mining earlier this year, announced first copper concentrate in June and zinc and pyrite concentrate followed in July. The federal announcement marks the project’s continuing ramp-up toward expected commercial production in the third quarter, with more than 400,000 tonnes of material already available for processing. That distinction matters because the larger story is not a ribbon-cutting date. It is the way the federal government now talks about domestic mineral production. Copper and zinc are being treated as strategic inputs whose availability affects power grids, vehicles, defence systems, communications equipment and advanced manufacturing. In that framework, a mine in Saskatchewan is part of Canada’s ability to withstand disruptions abroad and supply allies without relying entirely on foreign sources. McIlvenna Bay is expected to operate for more than 18 years. Eldorado has said 2026 production could include five million to 10 million pounds of copper, 3,000 to 6,000 tonnes of zinc, 5,000 to 10,000 ounces of gold and 100,000 to 200,000 ounces of silver as the operation ramps up. The company is also studying an expansion from roughly 4,900 tonnes of ore per day to about 7,000 tonnes, along with a possible silver-lead circuit. The project has become a showcase for a more interventionist Canadian industrial policy. The federal government committed $20 million through the Critical Minerals Infrastructure Fund in December 2024, another $41 million through the Strategic Innovation Fund in January 2025 and, through the Canada Growth Fund, approximately $156 million in May 2025. The project was also referred to the Major Projects Office in September 2025. Those numbers help explain why Ottawa is presenting the mine as more than a private commercial venture. Governments are increasingly deciding that access to certain minerals is too important to leave entirely to the short-term economics of commodity cycles. Canada has large reserves, but deposits do not create strategic independence unless mines, processing capacity, transport links and customers actually exist. The labour dimension is substantial as well. Natural Resources Canada says McIlvenna Bay currently employs about 380 full-time workers and is expected to employ about 450 when commercial production is reached. Roughly 34 per cent of the workforce is Indigenous. The federal release describes the project as being developed with Indigenous partners, an important part of how Ottawa is trying to connect critical-minerals policy with regional employment and participation. Eldorado’s acquisition of Foran in April also changes the ownership context. The Vancouver-headquartered gold producer bought the company that had developed McIlvenna Bay, adding the Saskatchewan copper-zinc asset to a larger international mining portfolio. That gives the project greater corporate scale, but it also reinforces the need to distinguish where a mine is physically located from who controls capital, processing decisions and downstream relationships. For Canada, the sovereignty question is therefore broader than whether ore comes out of Canadian ground. The strategic value depends on whether Canada can process enough of it, move it through resilient infrastructure, retain skilled workers, build domestic manufacturing around it and diversify the customers who buy it. A mine can reduce dependence only if the rest of the chain does not recreate the same vulnerability somewhere else. McIlvenna Bay is an early example of the direction Ottawa is taking. The language of critical minerals has moved decisively from economic development into economic security. When the federal government calls copper and zinc part of national security, it is acknowledging that the ability to produce ordinary industrial materials at home can become a form of strategic power. Sources Natural Resources Canada — McIlvenna Bay production update Eldorado Gold — acquisition of Foran Mining Eldorado Gold — first concentrate produced at McIlvenna Bay Eldorado Gold — Q2 2026 operational update
Current page · September 3, 2026

CANADA AND EUROPE ARE BUNDLING DEFENCE, DIGITAL TRADE AND CRITICAL MINERALS

Anita Anand and Kaja Kallas have put defence cooperation, SAFE, digital trade, critical minerals and economic security into the same Canada–EU strategic package ahead of an October summit in Canada.

CANADA AND EUROPE ARE BUNDLING DEFENCE, DIGITAL TRADE AND CRITICAL MINERALS Current page September 3, 2026 Anita Anand and Kaja Kallas have put defence cooperation, SAFE, digital trade, critical minerals and economic security into the same Canada–EU strategic package ahead of an October summit in Canada. Anita Anand and Kaja Kallas at the 2025 G7 foreign ministers meeting in Canada. European Commission Audiovisual Service / Wikimedia Commons, CC BY 4.0. Canada–EU · Defence · Digital trade · Critical minerals CANADA AND EUROPE ARE BUNDLING DEFENCE, DIGITAL TRADE AND CRITICAL MINERALS The latest Anand–Kallas readout shows something larger than another diplomatic meeting: Ottawa and Brussels are assembling security, industry, technology and trade into a single strategic relationship. Published Sept. 2, 2026 · 9:23 p.m. ET Canada and the European Union are moving several relationships that once sat in separate policy boxes into the same strategic package. In a readout released Wednesday, Foreign Affairs Minister Anita Anand and EU foreign-policy chief Kaja Kallas pointed to progress on the Canada–EU Security and Defence Partnership, Canadian participation in Europe’s SAFE defence-financing framework, negotiations toward a digital trade agreement, critical-minerals cooperation and broader economic security. The list is important because of what it combines. Defence procurement is no longer being discussed in isolation from industrial capacity. Digital trade is no longer simply an extension of conventional commerce. Critical minerals are being treated as security assets as well as export commodities. Canada and the EU are increasingly talking about all of them as parts of the same problem: how democratic partners can become more resilient when supply chains, technology platforms and strategic industries are exposed to political pressure. Anand met Kallas during the informal meeting of EU foreign ministers, known as the Gymnich, in Wicklow, Ireland. Global Affairs Canada had already described Anand’s participation as historic: it was the first time a Canadian foreign minister had taken part in a Gymnich meeting. The Sept. 2 readout now gives a clearer picture of what Canada wants to build from that access. The most immediate defence component is SAFE, the European Union’s Security Action for Europe financial instrument. Canada and the EU are working on closer defence-industrial cooperation through the program. For Canada, that matters because access to European defence procurement and financing could give Canadian companies another large market and another set of industrial relationships at a moment when Ottawa is trying to reduce strategic dependence on the United States. The digital component may prove just as important. Canada and the EU are negotiating a digital trade agreement intended to complement CETA. A previous Canada–EU Digital Partnership Council statement said the two sides want to modernize digital trade rules while also cooperating on digital credentials, standards, online-platform governance and foreign information manipulation. That makes the eventual agreement potentially relevant not only to trade volumes but to Canada’s ability to shape rules for data, platforms and emerging technologies with partners other than Washington. Critical minerals sit between the defence and digital files. Copper, nickel, lithium, rare earths and other strategic materials are necessary for military systems, grids, batteries, semiconductors and advanced manufacturing. Canada has reserves and mining expertise; Europe has large industrial demand and an explicit interest in reducing vulnerable dependencies. The Anand–Kallas readout says cooperation on critical minerals and economic security has already intensified. The political language is also notable. The ministers described Canada and the EU as “trusted partners” and emphasized resilience on both sides of the Atlantic. That is standard diplomatic language, but the policy content underneath it is concrete. Canada is looking for durable alternatives and supplements to a continental relationship in which access to the U.S. market, technology and defence system can also become leverage. Europe is looking for reliable suppliers, defence partners and technology relationships of its own. The relationship will get another test soon. Anand and Kallas said preparations are under way for a Canada–EU summit in Canada on Oct. 29 and 30. That gives Ottawa and Brussels less than two months to convert broad strategic alignment into specific agreements, procurement access, financing rules and timelines. For Canada, the central question is whether Europe becomes a genuine second strategic pillar or remains primarily a diplomatic counterweight. The newest readout suggests Ottawa is trying to make the relationship operational. Defence finance, digital trade, critical minerals and economic security are now being discussed together because the same sovereignty question runs through all of them: how many essential Canadian capabilities can be diversified away from dependence on a single foreign system. Sources Global Affairs Canada — Anand–Kallas meeting readout Global Affairs Canada — Gymnich participation announcement Canada–EU Digital Partnership Council joint statement
Current page · September 3, 2026

REPUBLICANS PRESSED GREER ON CANADA. IN PUBLIC, THEY BLAMED CANADA.

House Republicans questioned U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer behind closed doors about the Canada trade war, then publicly backed the administration line that Ottawa is responsible for the impasse.

REPUBLICANS PRESSED GREER ON CANADA. IN PUBLIC, THEY BLAMED CANADA. Current page September 3, 2026 House Republicans questioned U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer behind closed doors about the Canada trade war, then publicly backed the administration line that Ottawa is responsible for the impasse. United States · Republicans · Canada–U.S. trade REPUBLICANS PRESSED GREER ON CANADA. IN PUBLIC, THEY BLAMED CANADA. A closed-door Republican meeting exposed pressure inside Donald Trump’s party over the trade war. The public message afterward was much more disciplined: Ottawa, not Washington, is responsible for the impasse. Published Sept. 2, 2026 · 9:23 p.m. ET U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer spent part of Wednesday answering questions from House Republicans about President Donald Trump’s trade war with Canada, a sign that the dispute is no longer confined to negotiations between Ottawa and the White House. According to Agri-Pulse and Axios, Greer faced questions at a closed-door meeting of the Republican Study Committee as lawmakers raised concerns about the administration’s broader trade agenda, including the Canada confrontation and agricultural issues. The significance is not that House Republicans have suddenly broken with Trump. The public evidence does not support that conclusion. CBC reported that Republicans who spoke after the meeting generally defended the president’s approach, even as some members were privately uneasy about the political and economic consequences of a prolonged tariff fight. The more revealing development is the split between what Republicans were willing to press Greer about behind closed doors and the message senior members carried out to reporters. House Ways and Means Committee chair Jason Smith supplied the clearest version of that message. “We want to negotiate. It’s the Canadians,” he told reporters after the meeting, according to Agri-Pulse. That formulation closely tracks the line senior administration officials have been using since the collapse of the talks: Washington says it was prepared to make a deal, Canada walked away and any decision to return belongs to Ottawa. Jamieson Greer. Official USTR portrait, U.S. federal government public domain. Jason Smith. Official U.S. government portrait, public domain. That matters because the same argument has now moved beyond the administration’s television appearances and negotiating team into congressional Republican messaging. Earlier Wednesday, senior Trump officials were already presenting Canada as the party that rejected a favourable agreement and would eventually have to return. Smith’s comment extends that frame into the House at the very moment Republican lawmakers are asking Greer harder questions about the costs and politics of the trade agenda. There are reasons for Republicans to pay attention. Canada’s retaliatory tariffs are scheduled to take effect Sept. 8, and members representing agricultural, manufacturing and border-state constituencies have to account for the consequences at home. Axios described mounting dissatisfaction among House Republicans with parts of Trump’s trade policy. The Canada dispute was discussed alongside the administration’s decision to permit additional tariff-free beef imports, another issue with direct consequences for politically sensitive constituencies. The meeting therefore presents two realities at once. The first is that Republican lawmakers are not simply ignoring the Canada conflict. They are questioning the administration’s trade representative about it in a private party forum. The second is that the public Republican position remains substantially aligned with the White House: Canada is being portrayed as the side that chose the rupture and must make the first move to end it. For Canada, that distinction is important. Ottawa cannot assume congressional unease will automatically translate into political pressure for a softer American position. Concern about tariffs can coexist with partisan support for the president and with a public narrative that assigns responsibility to Canada. If the administration succeeds in making that narrative the default Republican account of the dispute, the political room for congressional Republicans to challenge Trump may remain narrow even when they dislike parts of the policy. It also gives Canada at War a useful marker for following Washington’s daily message. The administration’s claim that Ottawa rejected a good deal is no longer being delivered only by cabinet officials and trade advisers. It is appearing in the language of congressional leadership. Whether that remains disciplined as retaliation begins and economic effects accumulate will be one of the clearest tests of how durable the White House’s Canada message really is. Sources Agri-Pulse — Greer faces trade questions from Republicans Axios — Inside House Republicans’ beef with Trump’s trade agenda CBC report republished by Yahoo — Republicans back Trump in his trade war with Canada
Current page · September 2, 2026

CANADA OPENS A TRADE INQUIRY INTO CHINESE TRUCK AND BUS TIRES

Canada’s trade tribunal has begun a preliminary injury inquiry into allegations that truck and bus tires imported from China were dumped and subsidized. No tariff decision has been made.

CANADA OPENS A TRADE INQUIRY INTO CHINESE TRUCK AND BUS TIRES Current page September 2, 2026 Canada’s trade tribunal has begun a preliminary injury inquiry into allegations that truck and bus tires imported from China were dumped and subsidized. No tariff decision has been made. Trade remedies · China · Truck and bus tires CANADA OPENS A TRADE INQUIRY INTO CHINESE TRUCK AND BUS TIRES A complaint from Canadian retread manufacturers and Michelin Canada has started a formal anti-dumping and subsidy process. It is an inquiry—not yet a finding or a tariff decision. Published Sept. 2, 2026 · 6:23 p.m. ET Canada’s trade-remedy system has opened a new case involving truck and bus tires imported from China. The Canadian International Trade Tribunal says it has begun a preliminary injury inquiry after the Canada Border Services Agency initiated investigations into alleged dumping and subsidizing. The complaint was brought by the Canadian Retread Manufacturers Association of Vancouver and Michelin North America (Canada) Inc. of New Glasgow, Nova Scotia. They allege that the domestic industry has suffered injury because of the imported tires. The crucial point is procedural. The Tribunal has not found that dumping or subsidizing occurred, and the government notice does not announce a new tariff, a fine or a penalty against a named company. The inquiry is the first injury test in the process set out by the Special Import Measures Act. By October 30, the Tribunal is to decide whether there is a reasonable indication that the alleged dumping or subsidizing has caused injury or retardation, or threatens to cause injury. If it finds that threshold met, the CBSA will continue its investigations and is scheduled to make preliminary determinations by November 30. Those later determinations are where possible trade remedies become concrete. If the CBSA makes preliminary dumping or subsidy determinations, the Tribunal will open a final injury inquiry at the same time. Until then, the allegation remains unproven and the case remains preliminary. That distinction matters because trade cases are often described as though a government has already imposed punishment. Here, Canada has begun examining a complaint about a class of imports from China. The process can lead to anti-dumping or countervailing duties if the statutory tests are met, but this notice is not the final decision. The Tribunal is an independent quasi-judicial body that reports to Parliament through the Minister of Finance. Its notice says interested persons, associations and governments can apply to participate in the inquiry. Source Canadian International Trade Tribunal — Tribunal Initiates Inquiry—Truck and Bus Tires from China
Current page · September 2, 2026

THE CHINA RISK IS REAL. SO IS THE AMERICAN ONE.

An Enverus warning about Chinese technical-support dependencies points to a larger Canadian sovereignty problem: Washington also has legal power over technology Canada may depend on.

THE CHINA RISK IS REAL. SO IS THE AMERICAN ONE. Current page September 2, 2026 An Enverus warning about Chinese technical-support dependencies points to a larger Canadian sovereignty problem: Washington also has legal power over technology Canada may depend on. Technology sovereignty · China · United States · Energy security THE CHINA RISK IS REAL. SO IS THE AMERICAN ONE. An Enverus warning about Chinese technical-support dependencies points to a larger Canadian sovereignty problem: Washington also has legal power over technology Canada may depend on. Published Sept. 2, 2026 · 5:44 p.m. ET The image above is an AI-generated editorial illustration. A new warning from Enverus Intelligence Research says China’s latest rules governing overseas investment could expose a weak point in North American power systems. The risk, Enverus argues, is not necessarily that Chinese-made batteries or solar inverters stop crossing the border. It is that equipment already installed may depend on engineering, software, diagnostics, training or remote technical support that remains tied to Chinese suppliers — and Beijing has strengthened the legal framework governing some of those relationships. That is a legitimate concern. China’s State Council Order No. 837 took effect July 1 and explicitly places national sovereignty, security and development interests inside the framework for managing overseas investment. Enverus says the rules could add delay risk for U.S. projects that depend on Chinese-linked engineering, training and remote technical guidance, with battery storage and solar inverters among the areas with the greatest additional exposure. The company does not claim that China has already shut down North American projects, and its public summary acknowledges that existing American restrictions remain larger barriers to Chinese participation in the U.S. power market. The important insight is broader than China. Modern infrastructure is not merely a collection of machines that arrive at a site and operate independently for decades. A battery system can depend on proprietary software, firmware, remote monitoring, replacement parts and engineers who understand its control architecture. An inverter can require updates and manufacturer support long after installation. Buying the box does not necessarily mean acquiring the independent capacity to keep the capability operating. Once that principle is accepted, however, there is no logical reason to apply it only to Chinese technology. The United States also maintains extensive controls over strategic technology, software, technical data and expertise. Under the U.S. Export Administration Regulations, U.S.-origin items can remain subject to American export-control rules after they leave the country, and certain foreign-made products can also fall within those rules because of controlled U.S. content or technology. Washington therefore possesses legal mechanisms that can affect foreign access to American technology well beyond the original sale. Canada is treated very differently from an American adversary. It benefits from a dense alliance relationship, regulatory exemptions and privileged access in many sectors. Those advantages are real and materially reduce risk. But an exemption granted under American law is still a permission created under American law. It is not the same thing as Canadian control over the underlying technology. A striking example appeared this summer in artificial intelligence. Anthropic said the U.S. government imposed export controls on its Claude Fable 5 and Mythos 5 models on June 12, requiring it to restrict access by foreign nationals whether they were inside or outside the United States. Anthropic said it did not have a reliable real-time mechanism to determine nationality and temporarily suspended the affected models. The controls were later lifted for Fable, which returned to global availability. Anthropic’s current Fable 5.1 is available internationally, so it would be wrong to say that the company’s latest general-purpose Claude model cannot be used outside the United States. But Anthropic’s more restricted Mythos 5.1, released Sept. 1, provides the sharper example. The company says it is presently available only to vetted organizations and, for now, only to a set of U.S. organizations while Anthropic works to expand access. Whatever one thinks of the policy rationale, the episode demonstrates something important for Canadian procurement: access to a private American technology product can change rapidly because of a decision made by the U.S. government. The same structure has existed for decades in other strategic sectors. The U.S. Department of Energy’s Part 810 system governs transfers of certain unclassified nuclear technology and assistance to foreign atomic-energy activities. The rules reach beyond physical equipment to knowledge and expertise. Canada benefits from favourable treatment and general authorizations in many circumstances, but the system reflects the same underlying principle Beijing invokes in its own way: technical knowledge can be considered a national strategic asset rather than an ordinary commercial service. Defence makes the dependence even easier to see. Canada will own and operate its CF-35A fighters, and the Department of National Defence says Canada controls how its aircraft are used. DND also says partner countries participate in decisions on software updates and that Canada works with Australia and Britain in a reprogramming laboratory that develops mission data. Those facts matter, and public claims that Washington can simply press a secret “kill switch” on Canadian aircraft should not be treated as established fact. At the same time, the F-35 operates inside an international sustainment system anchored in the United States. DND says the U.S. government owns and manages the global spare-parts pool until components are installed, while hardware and software upgrades are coordinated through the F-35 Joint Program Office in consultation with participating countries. American export law separately restricts defence articles, technical data and defence services. Canada receives important exemptions and special treatment under the International Traffic in Arms Regulations, but those arrangements demonstrate that the underlying technology remains controlled. This does not make China and the United States politically identical, nor does it mean that the probability of interruption is the same. Canada’s treaty relationships, defence integration, legal ties and decades of institutional cooperation with Washington create a very different risk environment from its relationship with Beijing. Those differences belong in any serious assessment. The narrower point is that the structural sovereignty question does not disappear because the supplier is an ally. The provenance of the Enverus warning also deserves scrutiny. Enverus is not a Chinese-policy watchdog or a government agency. It is a large commercial energy-data and analytics company serving thousands of customers in dozens of countries. Blackstone announced an agreement to acquire it in 2025, describing a company used by more than 8,000 customers and deeply embedded in the U.S. energy industry. Its customers pay for intelligence about project, regulatory, supply-chain and market risk. That gives Enverus a commercial reason to identify vulnerabilities before they become expensive. Operating inside the North American energy system inevitably shapes the questions Enverus asks. American policy increasingly describes Chinese batteries, inverters, communications equipment and other strategic technologies through the language of national security, reshoring and supply-chain resilience. An energy-risk firm working in that environment will naturally spend substantial effort examining Chinese exposure. That context should be understood rather than ignored. But the available record does not support dismissing Enverus as an anti-China propaganda operation. Its own work has sometimes emphasized the costs of American restrictions. When the Biden administration raised tariffs on Chinese clean-energy products in 2024, Enverus noted that the measures would add inflationary pressure and underscored continuing U.S. dependence on Chinese manufacturing. Other Enverus analysis has credited Chinese oil buying with supporting global crude markets and has described Washington’s China hawkishness as a market factor rather than simply repeating it as an unquestioned truth. Its research should be read as commercially oriented analysis from within the North American energy-security environment — a lens with interests and assumptions, but not evidence by itself of fabrication. For Canada, the question raised by the report is therefore not simply whether China can be trusted. It is how much essential Canadian infrastructure can continue operating, be repaired, updated and maintained if access to any foreign supplier is interrupted. Chinese battery and inverter manufacturers have a commercial presence in Canada, but market presence alone does not prove that a particular Canadian grid project is vulnerable to Order No. 837. That depends on contracts, supplier architecture, remote access, software, data, replacement parts and the ability of Canadian operators to work independently. The same test should be applied to American systems. Who owns the source code? Who controls software updates? Where are the servers and data? Who holds encryption keys? Can Canadian engineers repair the system without foreign permission? Does a contract guarantee access to technical documentation and replacement parts? Could the supplier’s home government legally prevent it from providing support? Can the equipment operate safely if a foreign cloud connection disappears? How long could Canada keep an essential system running if relations with the supplier’s country deteriorated? Those questions do not imply that Canada should replace American technology with Chinese technology. Simply changing the country on which Canada depends would not produce technological sovereignty. The practical objective is resilience: diversified suppliers where possible, independent maintenance capability, contractual access to documentation and software, domestic engineering depth, stockpiles of critical components and procurement rules that account for political dependency alongside price and performance. Order No. 837 is therefore worth taking seriously. Enverus is reasonable to examine how Chinese controls over technology, services and technical personnel could affect projects that assume support will always be available. But the warning becomes much more useful to Canada when the same analytical standard is applied consistently. China has legal tools to control strategic technology in the name of Chinese national interests. The United States has legal tools to control strategic technology in the name of American national interests. The alliance relationship makes those dependencies different in degree, context and probability, but it does not erase the underlying fact that another government may possess leverage over systems Canada considers essential. The sovereign test is not whether the foreign flag over the supplier’s headquarters belongs to a friend or a rival. It is whether Canada has a workable fallback when somebody else controls something essential. If another government can decide whether Canada is allowed to keep using a critical capability, Canada has a dependency worth measuring. Historical snapshot · September 2, 2026

The China risk is real. So is the American one.

Enverus warns about Chinese technology dependence. Canada should apply the same sovereignty test to American technology.

The China risk is real. So is the American one. Historical snapshot September 2, 2026 Enverus warns about Chinese technology dependence. Canada should apply the same sovereignty test to American technology. Analysis · Digital sovereignty · Energy security · China · United States The China risk is real. So is the American one. An American energy-intelligence firm warns that Chinese rules could leave critical infrastructure dependent on Beijing. Apply the same test to American technology and Canada confronts a much larger question about sovereignty. Published Sept. 2, 2026 · 5:45 p.m. ET ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ Editorial illustration: Canada at War. The image places the United States and China back to back and upside down to represent the same sovereignty test being applied to both powers. A new warning from Enverus Intelligence Research says China’s latest overseas-investment rules could create a hidden vulnerability for North American power systems. The risk, Enverus argues, is not necessarily that Chinese-made batteries or solar inverters suddenly stop crossing the border. It is that equipment already installed may depend on Chinese engineering, software, diagnostics, training or remote technical support — and Beijing has strengthened its legal authority over some of those cross-border relationships. That deserves attention. But there is an obvious question missing from the American discussion: what happens if the technology is American? The same sovereignty problem exists. The United States already controls the export of strategically important technology, software, technical data and expertise. It can restrict who receives them, require licences, prevent foreigners from gaining access to particular capabilities and impose new restrictions that alter services companies and governments expected to use. That does not make the Chinese risk imaginary. It means Canada should stop treating technological dependence on China as a unique category of sovereignty problem while assuming technological dependence on the United States is sovereignty-neutral. China’s State Council Order No. 837 took effect July 1. The regulation establishes a more formal framework governing Chinese outbound investment and places national sovereignty, security and development interests among the objectives of the system. Enverus examined the framework from the perspective of American power developers and said Chinese-linked engineering, training and remote technical support could become subject to additional approvals, creating potential delays for projects that depend on continuing relationships with Chinese companies. Enverus considers battery storage and solar inverters particularly exposed. There is nothing inherently implausible about that conclusion. Modern infrastructure is not simply a collection of physical machines. A battery system can depend on proprietary software, firmware, remote monitoring, replacement components and engineers who understand its control architecture. A solar inverter can require updates and manufacturer support years after installation. Ownership of the box does not necessarily give its purchaser complete control over the capability. That is the important insight in the Enverus report. Once that principle is accepted, however, it cannot logically stop at China. A Canadian dependence on technology whose continued operation ultimately requires another government’s permission is a sovereignty dependency regardless of the flag on the supplier’s headquarters. The United States operates one of the most extensive technology-control systems in the world. Under the Export Administration Regulations, American controls can follow U.S.-origin technology and software after it leaves the country, and in some circumstances can reach foreign-made products incorporating controlled American technology. American law also regulates the release of controlled technology or source code to foreign persons. Canada benefits from exemptions, licences and unusually favourable arrangements, but those are permissions created under American law. They are not the same thing as sovereign Canadian control. Artificial intelligence has provided a particularly vivid demonstration of the principle. This year U.S. controls affected access to advanced Anthropic models and forced the company to distinguish between capabilities available internationally and more restricted systems available only to vetted American organizations. The exact restrictions and model availability have changed, but the sovereignty lesson is durable: access to a privately developed American technology can change because of decisions made within the American national-security system. The United States has long asserted similar authority in other domains. The Department of Energy regulates transfers of certain unclassified nuclear technology and assistance abroad. American defence technology is subject to its own export-control system. Canada receives privileged treatment in both areas, but privileged access is still access governed by another country’s rules. Canada’s F-35 program illustrates the distinction. Canada will own its aircraft and retains operational command. Claims that Washington can simply press a secret “kill switch” on Canadian F-35s should not be presented as established fact. At the same time, the aircraft exists inside an international sustainment, software, mission-data and upgrade architecture anchored by the United States. Canada has substantial operational sovereignty within that system. It does not have complete technological independence from the country that designed the aircraft and anchors its support network. None of this requires pretending Washington and Beijing are politically identical. They are not. The narrower structural reality is enough: both governments regulate strategic technology in the name of national security, both possess legal tools to restrict technology transfers, and both treat technical expertise as an instrument of national power. For Canada, the policy question raised by Enverus is therefore not simply whether China poses a risk. It is how much critical infrastructure Canada can operate, maintain, repair and sustain if access to any foreign supplier’s technology is interrupted. Chinese-made batteries and inverters clearly have a commercial presence in Canada, but presence does not itself demonstrate vulnerability. Exposure depends on procurement and support agreements, which technologies are embedded, where data resides, how updates are managed, whether proprietary components are required and whether Canadian operators can function independently if a relationship breaks down. The same audit should be applied to American equipment and services. Framed that way, the Enverus warning is useful but incomplete. Enverus is a commercial North American energy-intelligence company whose customers pay it to identify risk. It operates inside an American energy-policy environment that increasingly understands China through the language of supply-chain security, reshoring and strategic competition. That perspective deserves scrutiny, but the public record does not justify dismissing the firm as an anti-China propaganda operation. Enverus has also identified costs and inflationary effects arising from American tariffs and has acknowledged continuing American dependence on Chinese manufacturing. The more defensible conclusion is that Enverus looks at China through a North American commercial and strategic lens. Canada should neither discard that work nor import its assumptions wholesale. Instead, Ottawa should take the analytical tool Enverus is using on China and turn it on every major foreign supplier. That means asking who owns source code, who controls software updates, where servers and data reside, who holds encryption keys, whether Canadians can repair a system without the manufacturer, whether technical documentation is available, whether a foreign government could legally prohibit the manufacturer from assisting Canada, whether replacement components have alternative sources and how long an essential system could continue operating if the political relationship with the supplier’s home country deteriorated. The problem is not China. The problem is dependency without sovereign fallback. China’s Order No. 837 may indeed increase risk for companies dependent on Chinese technical support. Enverus is reasonable to investigate it. But the regulation’s greatest lesson for Canada may be something the firm did not set out to demonstrate. China controls technology and overseas economic activity in the name of Chinese national interests. The United States controls technology, nuclear expertise, defence information and advanced computing in the name of American national interests. Strategic technology is not merely something companies sell. It is an instrument of national power. For decades Canada could afford to regard American technological integration differently because the political relationship was unusually stable and the two countries operated within an extraordinarily integrated defence and economic system. The present Canada–U.S. confrontation raises the question of whether that assumption should still be embedded automatically in Canadian procurement decisions. That does not mean Canada should replace American technology with Chinese technology. Changing which foreign country Canada depends upon does not produce technological sovereignty. Building the capacity to withstand interruption does. That may require diversified suppliers, contractual rights to technical information, Canadian data residency, independent maintenance capability, stockpiles of critical components, software escrow, interoperability requirements, domestic engineering expertise and procurement rules that measure foreign political dependency alongside price and performance. The Enverus warning therefore deserves to be taken seriously — just not selectively. If dependence on Beijing is a security vulnerability because Beijing controls Chinese companies and technology, dependence on Washington must also be measured against Washington’s demonstrated ability to control American companies and technology. Canada does not need to decide that the two powers are equivalent to understand the structural issue. It needs to ask a simpler question: if somebody else can decide whether Canada is allowed to keep using something essential, how sovereign is Canada really? Primary sources and further reading: Enverus Intelligence Research ; China State Council Order No. 837 ; U.S. Bureau of Industry and Security, Export Administration Regulations ; U.S. Department of Energy, Part 810 .
Current page · September 2, 2026

WASHINGTON BACKS OPENAI. CANADIAN CREATORS ARE GOING TO COURT.

The Trump administration is pressing for a permissive approach to AI training while SOCAN sues Suno in Canada and Mark Carney says laissez-faire is not the answer to AI-era safety.

WASHINGTON BACKS OPENAI. CANADIAN CREATORS ARE GOING TO COURT. Current page September 2, 2026 The Trump administration is pressing for a permissive approach to AI training while SOCAN sues Suno in Canada and Mark Carney says laissez-faire is not the answer to AI-era safety. Artificial intelligence · Copyright · Digital sovereignty · Canada–U.S. WASHINGTON BACKS OPENAI. CANADIAN CREATORS ARE GOING TO COURT. The Trump administration is urging G20 governments to give AI companies room to train on copyrighted work. SOCAN has sued Suno in Canada, while Mark Carney has already said “laissez-faire” is not the answer to AI-era safety. Published Sept. 2, 2026 · 3:28 p.m. ET Art: OpenAI wordmark via Wikimedia Commons. Commons lists the wordmark as public domain because it consists of simple text/geometric material; OpenAI trademark rights remain applicable. Two developments on opposite sides of the border have turned an abstract argument about artificial intelligence into a concrete Canadian sovereignty question: who writes the rules for the technology, and who gets paid when machines learn from human work? In Washington, the Trump administration has formally entered one of the central American copyright fights over generative AI. In a brief filed in Manhattan federal court, the U.S. government backed OpenAI’s argument that using copyrighted material to train artificial-intelligence systems can qualify as fair use. Reuters and The Associated Press reported that the administration described AI training as highly transformative and warned that an overly restrictive interpretation of copyright could impede innovation, national security and U.S. economic competitiveness. On the same day, Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick carried that philosophy into an international forum. At the G20 Innovation Ministerial in North Carolina, the United States urged governments to develop rules that leave room for AI companies to train models on creators’ work while also protecting creators’ rights. Lutnick invoked the American doctrine of fair use. The proposal did not set out a detailed global copyright regime, and American fair use is not Canadian law. That distinction matters because Canada has just acquired its own live test case. SOCAN, the Society of Composers, Authors and Music Publishers of Canada, announced Wednesday that it has sued Suno Inc. in Federal Court. SOCAN alleges that Suno’s generative-music platform produces and publicly streams outputs that replicate songs in SOCAN’s repertoire without consent or compensation and thereby infringes performing rights in the underlying musical works. SOCAN says its claim identifies a sample of 150 publicly available Suno outputs. Its case is an allegation, not a finding by the court. The Canadian proceeding should also not be collapsed into the U.S. newspaper litigation: the legal claims, rights asserted and factual records differ. But the two disputes expose the same underlying policy collision. AI companies want access to enormous quantities of human-created material; creators and publishers are asking where permission, compensation and enforceable rights fit into that model. The timing makes remarks Mark Carney made in Ireland before the June G7 summit look more consequential. Asked about artificial intelligence, child safety and the role of the United States in a changing international order, the prime minister said Canada would take child safety seriously and that “laissez-faire is not the answer to child safety.” He added that Canada would act even when not everyone around the G7 table agreed. Carney was speaking specifically about AI-era safety and children, not deciding the copyright question now before the courts. It would be wrong to turn that quotation into a Canadian legal position on Suno or OpenAI. What it does reveal is a broader regulatory philosophy: Ottawa does not accept that the default response to fast-moving digital technology should be to leave the market alone. That philosophy is also written into the government’s AI for All strategy. In launching it in June, Carney said that in the AI era, prosperity and sovereignty would belong to countries able to develop, adopt and govern the technology on their own terms. The strategy promises updated privacy and online-safety rules, protections for children, stronger AI-safety capacity and international partnerships designed to reduce technological dependence. Europe provides the clearest contrast with Washington’s lighter-touch push. The European Union’s AI Act already requires providers of general-purpose AI models to maintain a policy for complying with EU copyright law and to publish a sufficiently detailed summary of training content. Providers of the most powerful models face additional safety and cybersecurity obligations. Canada has not simply copied the European model, but Ottawa and European governments increasingly occupy the same side of the argument that AI requires public rules as well as private innovation. The G7 itself moved partway in that direction in June. Leaders, including the United States, backed a call for safer digital spaces for minors and acknowledged risks associated with conversational AI. That is important nuance: this is not a clean split in which Washington opposes every AI safeguard and Canada and Europe favour every regulation. The disagreement is over how far governments should go, how quickly, and where copyright, safety and sovereignty should constrain commercial deployment. SOCAN’s lawsuit now gives Canada a domestic arena in which those questions will be tested. The U.S. government’s OpenAI brief and Lutnick’s G20 campaign show that Washington is simultaneously trying to shape the international answer. For Canada, the issue is larger than a single music platform or newspaper lawsuit. It is whether the rules governing a foundational technology will be imported from the United States, borrowed from Europe, or written in Canada around Canadian law and Canadian interests. Sources Reuters — U.S. urges G20 countries to allow AI training on creators’ work Reuters — U.S. government backs OpenAI in New York Times copyright case Associated Press — Trump administration backs OpenAI in copyright case SOCAN — legal action against Suno Canadian Press via CityNews — Carney says laissez-faire is not the answer to child safety Prime Minister of Canada — AI for All strategy Prime Minister of Canada — G7 call for a safer digital space for minors European Commission — general-purpose AI obligations under the AI Act Wikimedia Commons — OpenAI wordmark licensing

THE TARIFF WAR IS STARTING TO CHANGE WHAT CANADIAN COMPANIES BUY

A $14.1-million Nova Scotia package shows Canadian tariff relief turning into machinery purchases, defence capacity and efforts to win customers in Europe and the United Kingdom.

THE TARIFF WAR IS STARTING TO CHANGE WHAT CANADIAN COMPANIES BUY Current page September 2, 2026 A $14.1-million Nova Scotia package shows Canadian tariff relief turning into machinery purchases, defence capacity and efforts to win customers in Europe and the United Kingdom. Tariff response · Industrial policy · Nova Scotia · Diversification THE TARIFF WAR IS STARTING TO CHANGE WHAT CANADIAN COMPANIES BUY Ottawa’s $14.1-million Nova Scotia package is more than a grant announcement. One manufacturer says it removed U.S. suppliers from consideration; others are using federal money to pursue Europe, defence work and a U.K. trade mission. Published Sept. 2, 2026 · 3:28 p.m. ET Photo: Cnccp5/Wikimedia Commons, CC0 1.0 public-domain dedication. Used as a representative image of CNC milling; it does not depict Ace Machining’s newly purchased machine. The federal government’s tariff response is beginning to show up in machine shops, export plans and purchasing decisions. A new $14.1-million package for Nova Scotia businesses contains one especially revealing detail: a Dartmouth manufacturer says U.S. tariffs caused it to take American suppliers out of the running when it bought new equipment. Atlantic Canada Opportunities Agency Minister Sean Fraser announced the package Wednesday at Ace Machining Ltd. in Dartmouth. Ace received a $442,000 non-repayable contribution to purchase and install an automated CNC milling station intended to improve productivity and help it compete for more work in Canada and abroad. Company president and co-owner Ron Wallace said the first round of U.S. tariffs changed the procurement decision. Ace removed American suppliers from consideration and selected a Matsuura MX-420 distributed by Elliott Matsuura Canada. Matsuura is a Japanese machine-tool manufacturer. Wallace said one purchase would not determine the trade dispute, but that similar decisions by enough Canadian companies could send a message. That makes the announcement more interesting than a conventional regional-development package. The tariff conflict is not only changing the price of goods crossing the border. It is beginning to influence which countries Canadian companies look to for machinery, where they seek customers and which supply chains governments are willing to subsidize. The other recipients show the same pattern. Halifax-based QRA Consulting is receiving $400,000 in non-repayable support for business development and engineering work aimed at becoming ready for European markets, identifying prospective customers and expanding exports. MacKenzie Atlantic Tool & Die/Machining in Musquodoboit Harbour is receiving $956,820 for advanced CNC equipment that Ottawa says will strengthen its ability to supply Canadian and international defence and industrial programs. SeaChange Biochemistry in Meteghan is receiving $163,700 for automated freeze-drying and packaging equipment, staff expansion, branding and participation in a United Kingdom trade mission. Other projects in the 27-recipient package include automation, cybersecurity, export certification and processing upgrades. The money comes through the Regional Tariff Response Initiative, which Ottawa says now totals $3.45 billion nationally. The government added $1.5 billion to the program in August as part of a larger $7.5-billion package for workers and businesses affected by U.S. tariffs. There are two different claims embedded in those numbers and they should not be confused. The government can document that money has been approved and equipment is being purchased. It cannot yet demonstrate that a European sales campaign will produce customers, that a U.K. mission will produce contracts or that new machinery will permanently replace U.S.-linked supply chains. Those are intended outcomes, not established results. But the Ace decision is already real. A Canadian manufacturer says tariffs changed its purchasing criteria and eliminated American suppliers from consideration. At the same time, federal money is being directed toward European market entry, British trade development and defence-oriented production capacity. That is what trade diversification looks like before the big export statistics move. It is granular: one machine, one supplier list, one engineering team, one trade mission and one potential defence contract at a time. If the Canada–U.S. dispute persists, these small procurement decisions will be worth watching because they can eventually harden into new commercial relationships that are much harder to reverse than a tariff schedule. Sources Atlantic Canada Opportunities Agency — $14.1-million Nova Scotia tariff-response announcement Atlantic Canada Opportunities Agency — project-by-project backgrounder Wikimedia Commons — CNC Milling image and CC0 licence
