THE NORDICS HAVE PUT ESSENTIAL SUPPLIES IN THEIR SECURITY PLAN

A new Nordic declaration aims to keep critical goods and services moving in crises and war. Canada is not a signatory, but its March agreement with Nordic leaders makes the new framework worth watching.

THE NORDICS HAVE PUT ESSENTIAL SUPPLIES IN THEIR SECURITY PLAN Current page September 2, 2026 A new Nordic declaration aims to keep critical goods and services moving in crises and war. Canada is not a signatory, but its March agreement with Nordic leaders makes the new framework worth watching. Nordics · Economic security · Supply chains THE NORDICS HAVE PUT ESSENTIAL SUPPLIES IN THEIR SECURITY PLAN A new Nordic declaration aims to keep critical goods and services moving in crises and war. Canada is not a signatory, but its March agreement with Nordic leaders makes the new framework worth watching. Published Sept. 2, 2026 · 11:43 a.m. ET The image above is an AI-generated editorial illustration. The Nordic countries have signed a new declaration that treats the movement of essential goods and services as a security question, not simply a trade question. The agreement, signed Sept. 2 at the Nordic Supplies Summit, is intended to build a shared ability to manage disruptions in production, logistics, distribution and supply during natural emergencies, disrupted trade flows, security crises and war. The participants are Sweden, Denmark, Finland and Åland, the Faroe Islands, Greenland, Iceland and Norway. Their declaration says the purpose is to keep critical flows running for society and the population, and also to support the reinforcement and sustainment of NATO's external border in a deteriorating European security environment. The practical menu is broad. It includes coordinating agreements so goods and services continue to flow in a crisis; planning for product conversion and joint production capacity; public-private cooperation; sectoral discussion of material preparedness and joint stockpiles; and secure access to critical raw materials and other strategic resources. This is not a treaty, a mutual-aid guarantee or a finished supply-sharing system. The declaration expressly says it is not legally binding and creates no rights or obligations under international, European Union or national law. Its next formal step is a feasibility study, due to the Nordic Security of Supply Network in 2027, on concrete areas where cooperation could deepen. That limitation is important, but so is the opening it creates. The text says cooperation can be extended to other countries in specific cases, while requiring the Nordic participants to account for existing European cooperation and NATO planning. Canada is not among the signatories, and no Canadian participation, invitation or negotiation under this declaration has been announced. Still, Ottawa already has a relevant political bridge. On March 15, the prime ministers of Canada and the Nordic countries committed to deeper economic-security cooperation, reliable global supply chains, critical-mineral access and secure, complementary energy supply chains. The new declaration puts an operational civilian-resilience framework beside that earlier political promise. For Canada, the reporting question is now specific. Can the March partnership become arrangements that keep essential goods moving when trade is disrupted or a security crisis narrows normal commercial options? Canada has geographic reach, energy, minerals, food capacity and an Arctic relationship with Nordic partners. The declaration, however, does not yet assign Canada a role or name a Canadian project. That is the distinction to hold onto. This is not evidence that Canada has joined a new Nordic bloc. It is evidence that a close group of Canadian partners is building the kind of practical preparedness architecture Canada has said it values—and has left a limited door open to cooperation beyond the region. Sources Government of Sweden — Joint Nordic Declaration on Security of Supply Government of Sweden — declaration signing announcement Government of Åland — published declaration text Nordic prime ministers and Canada — March 15 joint statement
Current page · September 2, 2026

THE CANADA TARIFF HAS BECOME A SENATE-RACE PROBLEM

A new five-state Abacus Data poll finds that supporting tariffs on Canada is a stated electoral liability for U.S. Senate candidates in every state tested.

THE CANADA TARIFF HAS BECOME A SENATE-RACE PROBLEM Current page September 2, 2026 A new five-state Abacus Data poll finds that supporting tariffs on Canada is a stated electoral liability for U.S. Senate candidates in every state tested. United States · Senate elections · Canada–U.S. trade THE CANADA TARIFF HAS BECOME A SENATE-RACE PROBLEM A new five-state Abacus Data poll finds that supporting tariffs on Canada is a stated electoral liability for U.S. Senate candidates in every state tested. Published Sept. 2, 2026 · 11:43 a.m. ET There is a new measure of the domestic political cost of Washington's tariff campaign against Canada, and it is not national. Abacus Data surveyed registered voters in five states with consequential U.S. Senate races—Iowa, Michigan, Ohio, Maine and South Carolina—and found a net negative response in every state when voters were asked whether a candidate's support for tariffs on Canada would make them more or less likely to support that candidate. The study was conducted online from Aug. 26 to 28 among 2,500 registered voters, with 500 respondents in each state. Abacus says each state sample was weighted independently for age, gender, region, education, race and ethnicity, and partisanship. The company says its objective was to measure how the trade dispute may shape the five Senate contests. The strongest numbers are among independents. In accompanying analysis, Abacus chief executive David Coletto reported a net 40-point penalty in Michigan and a net 42-point penalty in Maine when independents were asked about a Senate candidate supporting the tariffs. Those are stated reactions to a survey question, not a prediction that tariffs will decide either election. But they are a direct warning against the proposition that pressure on Canada comes without a political price at home. The economic geography is part of the point. Abacus chose Iowa for agriculture and potash, Michigan for auto manufacturing, Ohio for steel and parts, Maine for its deeply integrated border economy and South Carolina for export-oriented manufacturing. The poll was fielded after the collapse of Canada–U.S. trade talks and the new 50-per-cent tariffs on roughly $20 billion in Canadian goods, and after the announcement of higher tariffs on Canadian-made vehicles, trucks and auto parts beginning in 2027. Across the five states, the pollster says voters were more negative about the administration's handling of trade with Canada than they were about Donald Trump overall. Most respondents expected tariffs to hurt the American economy, workers, farmers and their own states. Opposition to the proposed 50-per-cent tariff on Canadian-made vehicles exceeded support in every state, according to Coletto's analysis, from 51 per cent opposed in South Carolina to 67 per cent in Maine. The result also undercuts the idea that the dispute has made Canada itself unpopular. Abacus found that positive views of Canada remained high in all five states and that between 55 and 72 per cent preferred the bilateral relationship that existed before Trump took office to the current one. Fewer than one in five preferred the current relationship, the pollster said. There are limits to what this can establish. It is pollster-produced research, paid for by Abacus Data, based on online state samples of 500 registered voters each. The public release describes the weighting but does not make the full tables and every question wording freely visible without the pollster's report-download process. State-level estimates are necessarily more sensitive to wording, weighting and sampling choices than a large national survey. It is therefore not proof that tariffs will determine November's Senate results, or that a voter who reports a penalty today will vote on trade alone. It is nevertheless a more precise political test than a national approval poll. In states connected to Canada through cars, commodities, border commerce and manufacturing supply chains, voters say a candidate's support for the tariff pressure campaign would cost that candidate support. For Canada, the finding does not change the immediate commercial damage from tariffs. It does identify a potential pressure point in the American political system: the administration's strategy is being tested not only against Canadian endurance, but against voters in states whose economies know the cross-border relationship at close range. Sources Abacus Data — release and methodology David Coletto — detailed accompanying analysis
Current page · September 2, 2026

ON INGRAHAM, CANADA BECOMES A WARNING LABEL

Lorrie Goldstein's post moved through Laura Ingraham's social feed while Élie Cantin-Nantel appeared on her Fox program. The routes were different, but the Canada story was strikingly similar.

ON INGRAHAM, CANADA BECOMES A WARNING LABEL Current page September 2, 2026 Lorrie Goldstein's post moved through Laura Ingraham's social feed while Élie Cantin-Nantel appeared on her Fox program. The routes were different, but the Canada story was strikingly similar. Media Watch · Fox News · Canada–U.S. relations ON INGRAHAM, CANADA BECOMES A WARNING LABEL Lorrie Goldstein's post moved through Laura Ingraham's social feed; Canadian commentator Élie Cantin-Nantel appeared on her program. The routes were different, but the story about Canada was strikingly similar. Published Sept. 2, 2026 · 11:43 a.m. ET Laura Ingraham did not interview Lorrie Goldstein in the material reviewed for this report. The connection was different: Ingraham's public X feed amplified Goldstein's Sept. 1 post promoting his column on Canadian living standards, presenting it as evidence of the kind of country American Democrats want to emulate. Goldstein's post and Ingraham's framing were public political commentary, not a news report or a finding by an independent American institution. The following day, the same Fox program took a more direct route. The Ingraham Angle interviewed Canadian commentator Élie Cantin-Nantel under the on-screen premise that Canada had become an “unviable place” to build businesses. Cantin-Nantel said about 10,000 Canadians were relocating to the United States each month. That number is the point at which the television argument outran the available evidence. Statistics Canada recorded 120,640 emigrants from Canada in 2025—about 10,053 a month. That is an all-destinations count. It does not show that 10,000 people a month moved to the United States, and it does not establish why each person left. The distinction is not semantic: a total-emigration figure cannot, by itself, demonstrate a U.S.-bound monthly flight. Canada's economic problems are real. Low productivity, affordability pressures, interprovincial barriers and a large annual emigration figure are legitimate subjects for hard reporting and argument. Goldstein's underlying post cited a Fraser Institute report; the report's conclusions can be debated on their merits. What changes when the material crosses the border is its job. A Canadian argument about performance becomes an American partisan warning label: Canada is not a complicated neighbour under pressure, but a simple preview of Democratic failure. That is a familiar frame on Ingraham's public feed and program. In a separate post, the account accused Prime Minister Mark Carney of sacrificing the Canadian economy to damage Donald Trump and Republican prospects in Maine and Michigan. In January, Ingraham posted that the two countries' economies should be compared again in six months. And in a March 2025 interview carried by the program, Donald Trump called Canada a “51st state” and one of the countries he found hardest to deal with. Those examples do not prove that Goldstein, Cantin-Nantel, Fox News and Ingraham coordinated their messages. No such evidence is offered here. They do show a repeatable editorial pattern: Canada is cast as a failed progressive experiment, an adversarial trade actor, or a subordinate country whose economic weakness confirms American political claims. The difference matters at a moment when Washington is using tariffs and political pressure against Canada. A domestic Canadian critique can be sharp, ideological and still be part of normal democratic life. Once it is repackaged for an American audience as proof that Canada deserves to be dismissed, punished or treated as a cautionary tale, it becomes part of the political atmosphere in which policy is made. There is also a practical lesson for Canadian commentators. Strong criticism survives scrutiny when its numbers are exact about what they measure. The public does not need a falsely specific U.S.-migration claim to understand that Canada has serious economic work to do. And American viewers deserve to know when a figure about people leaving Canada has been turned into a claim about people fleeing to their country. Canada at War is not alleging disloyalty or unlawful conduct by Goldstein or Cantin-Nantel. This is a documented account of a public chain of framing and amplification. It will publish substantive responses from either commentator, Fox News or Laura Ingraham in full or in relevant part. Sources Fox News — Élie Cantin-Nantel segment on The Ingraham Angle Statistics Canada — annual international-migration estimates Lorrie Goldstein — Sept. 1 public post Laura Ingraham — public X account Laura Ingraham — Carney/Maine/Michigan post Fox News — Trump interview on Canada as a “51st state”
Current page · September 2, 2026

A SMILING CARNEY IN A HARSHER WORLD: CANADA’S MIDDLE-POWER TEST BEGINS

As Canada confronts U.S. tariffs, Mark Carney’s middle-power strategy is beginning to be tested at the G20 and across the Atlantic.

A SMILING CARNEY IN A HARSHER WORLD: CANADA’S MIDDLE-POWER TEST BEGINS Current page September 2, 2026 As Canada confronts U.S. tariffs, Mark Carney’s middle-power strategy is beginning to be tested at the G20 and across the Atlantic. G20 · Trade diversification · Middle powers A SMILING CARNEY IN A HARSHER WORLD: CANADA’S MIDDLE-POWER TEST BEGINS Canada’s answer to U.S. coercion is taking shape: build practical ties with countries that still value trust, rules and reliable supply chains. Published Sept. 2, 2026 · 12:28 a.m. ET The photograph is almost too neat for the moment. Mark Carney is smiling, composed and formally dressed at the White House in May 2025. Behind the image is a relationship that has since become far harsher: a trade fight, tariffs, sovereignty taunts and a Canadian government openly accepting that the old dependence on Washington is no longer safe. Canada’s response cannot consist only of retaliation. Retaliation is necessary when a country is targeted, but it does not create a future. The larger Carney strategy is to make Canada harder to isolate and less vulnerable to economic coercion by building practical alliances with countries that still value reliable rules, trusted supply chains and democratic institutions. That is the meaning of the middle-powers idea now being tested around the G20. Finance Minister François-Philippe Champagne has said Canada is being viewed as a preferred transatlantic partner at a time of global uncertainty. It is a claim that deserves scrutiny, but it is also a serious opening. Canada has assets that partners need: energy, critical minerals, food, advanced manufacturing capacity, public institutions and an economy linked to both Europe and the United States. The question is whether Ottawa can convert the new interest into signed projects, investment, transport links and dependable markets. A middle-power strategy is not a slogan. It succeeds only when a country can offer something tangible and follows through quickly enough that companies and governments make decisions before the next shock. The current rupture with the United States gives that work urgency. Washington’s 50 per cent tariffs on a major group of Canadian goods and Canada’s announced countermeasures have shown businesses that market access can be used as political leverage. Canada cannot replace the U.S. market overnight. It can, however, stop treating that market as the only strategic horizon. Champagne’s transatlantic language points toward a specific kind of diversification. It is not an argument for turning away from the United States or pretending integration does not matter. It is an argument for giving Canada more than one serious option. A country with alternative buyers, investors and supply partners negotiates differently from a country that has nowhere else to go. Europe is the natural first place to test that proposition. Canada has a trade agreement with the European Union, growing security relationships and a profile as a stable supplier in a world preoccupied with energy security and critical minerals. But the challenge is operational. Good speeches about shared values will not build a processing plant, a port link or a supply chain. Canadian governments will need to make it easier for partners to invest and easier for Canadian firms to sell. The G20 is also a reminder that the United States is not Canada’s only difficult economic partner. The meeting has been dominated by disputes over Chinese exports, industrial capacity and critical inputs. Canada cannot simply choose between Washington and Beijing. It must develop the ability to cooperate with democracies and other reliable partners while keeping the capacity to make its own decisions about its economic future. That is the tougher world behind the smiling Carney image. The old model assumed that economic integration with the United States made Canada safer and richer. It did make Canada richer. It no longer guarantees political safety. A neighbour can be indispensable and still use that indispensability against you. Carney’s middle-power approach begins from that recognition. It asks Canada to become useful in several directions at once: a reliable energy and minerals partner for Europe, a serious security partner in the Atlantic and Arctic, a place where allied manufacturers can invest, and a country willing to work with other middle powers when larger states treat trade and finance as instruments of pressure. There is no easy version of this plan. Diversification costs money, requires infrastructure, and takes years. It will sometimes collide with domestic arguments about regulation, projects and foreign investment. But the alternative is increasingly clear. If Canada does not build more economic and diplomatic options, every future dispute with Washington begins with the same question: how much can Canada afford to refuse? The smiling photograph should not be read as nostalgia for a relationship that has vanished. It is better read as contrast. Canada can be confident without being naive, open to negotiation without accepting humiliation, and allied with the United States without organizing its entire future around U.S. goodwill. The test now is whether the government can turn that posture into a durable network of partners before the next crisis arrives. Sources Reuters: Canada is transatlantic partner of choice amid uncertainty, Canadian minister says Finance Canada: targeted countermeasures and support for workers and businesses White House portrait of Mark Carney, public domain via Wikimedia Commons
Current page · September 2, 2026

MEXICO WATCHES CANADA’S TRADE WAR AND SEES ITS OWN WARNING

Mexican writers and political leaders see Canada’s trade rupture with Washington as a warning for Mexico’s own CUSMA negotiations.

MEXICO WATCHES CANADA’S TRADE WAR AND SEES ITS OWN WARNING Current page September 2, 2026 Mexican writers and political leaders see Canada’s trade rupture with Washington as a warning for Mexico’s own CUSMA negotiations. Mexico · Canada–U.S. trade · CUSMA · Analysis MEXICO WATCHES CANADA’S TRADE WAR AND SEES ITS OWN WARNING Mexican economists, writers and ministers are reading Ottawa’s rupture with Washington as a warning: formal trade rules are no longer the same thing as certainty. Published Sept. 2, 2026 · 12:28 a.m. ET Mexico is not speaking with Canada’s volume. It is not matching Ottawa’s defiance or turning its own talks with Washington into a public confrontation. But the Mexican voices now writing about Canada’s tariff crisis are not relaxed. They are reading it as a warning. The collapse of Canada’s talks with the United States did more than produce another dispute between close trading partners. It exposed a negotiating method: tariffs first, pressure second, concessions demanded behind closed doors, and the terms of a supposed agreement capable of changing at the last moment. For Mexico, whose next round of negotiations with Washington is approaching, that is a live lesson. Valeria Moy, the Mexican economist and El País México columnist, wrote after the Canada–U.S. talks broke down that the failure was not surprising. The warning signs had been present for weeks. Her account describes a sequence that Canada knows well: threat, apparent progress, optimism, a new demand, collapse and retaliation. Canada had been told a deal was within reach. Then Washington’s terms changed. The result was 50 per cent tariffs on a broad range of Canadian goods and Ottawa’s decision to retaliate dollar for dollar. Moy’s larger point is the one Mexico must now absorb: a country cannot assume an agreement is safe simply because it is written down, because meetings have been productive, or because both sides would plainly benefit from avoiding damage. In the present American approach, a treaty can be treated as a temporary instrument rather than a binding framework. Moy argues that Mexico should see Canada’s experience as a preview of the behaviour it may have to confront. Tariff threats can move a negotiation beyond ordinary trade questions and onto sovereignty, cultural policy, foreign relationships and domestic political choices. That is why the Canadian fight matters well beyond the goods on the tariff lists. Canada did not simply refuse a technical adjustment. It rejected conditions its government described as unfair and uneconomic. The reported demands reached beyond immediate tariff relief into the country’s ability to make its own decisions. The details are contested; the pattern is not. Market access is being used as leverage over political ground. Sonia Corona, reporting from Mexico for El País , describes a country watching the Washington–Ottawa clash with uncertainty while trying to protect its own position. Mexico is not a party to the immediate rupture, but it is very much a party to the larger question: whether CUSMA can still provide a stable basis for continental commerce. President Claudia Sheinbaum has avoided a public escalation. That restraint is strategy, not indifference. She has sent Economy Secretary Marcelo Ebrard to Washington to assess the mood of the American administration and pursue relief from U.S. tariffs on Mexican steel, aluminum, vehicles and auto parts. Mexico’s public line is continued engagement and the hope that an agreement is still possible. Its actions show it understands the risk. Mexico is already being asked to negotiate in an environment where the United States has preferred bilateral bargaining, even though the treaty is formally trilateral. Washington can identify each partner’s separate vulnerabilities, pursue different concessions, and use apparent progress with one country as leverage against the other. Canada and Mexico are left watching developments next door, calculating what precedent may appear at their own table. Eyanir Chinea, also writing for El País México , reported that Mexico regarded any Canada–U.S. agreement as important information for its own CUSMA strategy. That was a cautious formulation before the talks collapsed. It now looks more urgent. Canada has supplied Mexico with information of a harsher kind: even after apparent progress, Washington may use timing and tariffs to demand more. Ebrard has maintained a public posture of optimism, saying Mexico has made substantial proposals and expects serious agreements. Mexico has compelling reasons to avoid an immediate trade war. Its factories, auto-parts producers and export industries are tightly connected to the U.S. market. But optimism cannot by itself solve a structure in which one side is willing to turn trade access into a rolling political test. Angélica Moreno Flores, a Mexican political scientist writing in El Universal , says attention has moved from the M to the C of T-MEC. For months the focus was Mexico’s tariff exposure and its own negotiations. Canada’s crisis is now the clearest picture of the continent’s new trade reality. Moreno’s argument is not merely that tariffs are economically harmful. It is that tariffs have become instruments of political power. A tariff can protect an industry or raise revenue; it can also alter supply chains, force a bargaining concession and make an example of a country that refuses a demand. The question is not only who pays. It is what the threat is intended to obtain. That is the sovereignty issue at the heart of the Canadian fight, and increasingly at the heart of Mexico’s anxiety. Mexico has faced American pressure over trade, migration, border enforcement, Chinese investment, industrial policy and security. The boundaries between those subjects are becoming less clear. A country seeking stable market access may be asked to demonstrate compliance in areas far removed from the product crossing the border. Mexico and Canada do not face identical circumstances. Mexico’s economic integration with the United States is deeper in several sectors, and its government has used a less publicly confrontational style. Those differences may give Mexico room to manoeuvre. They do not give Mexico immunity. A more integrated relationship can make a country more valuable to the United States; it can also make it more vulnerable to coercion. Moreno notes that Mexico has preserved tariff-free access for much of its exports to the United States. That is a real advantage. It should not be confused with certainty. Canada’s experience demonstrates how a country can remain inside the treaty framework yet face punitive tariffs, or be told that protection depends on accepting conditions outside its expected scope. The annual-review system makes that distinction especially dangerous. The treaty remains in force, but every review creates another occasion for Washington to reopen pressure, demand more or threaten disruption. The old promise of continental trade was that known rules would replace arbitrary political interference. The Mexican writers now watching Canada see a different reality: the rules can still exist while political leverage dominates the result. Sheinbaum and Ebrard are trying to manage a difficult balance. Mexico must defend its interests without supplying Trump with a public quarrel he can exploit. It must negotiate hard enough to protect its industries while keeping enough calm to maintain the talks. Canada’s refusal to accept a bad agreement may prove that Washington cannot automatically dictate terms to every partner. It may also increase the temptation to secure concessions elsewhere and declare victory. The quiet from Mexico should therefore not be read as complacency. It is the quiet of a government studying a live demonstration of what may await it. Mexican writers are saying what official language cannot always say plainly: Canada’s experience is part of a recognizable pattern of negotiation by threat; tariffs have become a means of testing sovereignty; and Mexico is watching urgently because it knows its own turn is coming. Mexico may not be on Canada’s side of the barricade today. But it is sitting very close to it. The question is not whether it can avoid every conflict with Washington. It is how much autonomy it can preserve when access to its central export market is made conditional on a continually changing political bargain. Canada has learned that lesson in public. Mexico is learning it in real time. Sources Valeria Moy, Otra conversación fallida — El País México Angélica Moreno Flores, Soberanía y aranceles — El Universal Sonia Corona, México mira con incertidumbre — El País México Eyanir Chinea, México confía en tener una mejor posición comercial — El País México

CANADA IS IN EUROPE’S FOREIGN-POLICY ROOM. THE MEETING IS UNDER WAY.

Anita Anand joins the Sept. 1–2 EU Gymnich in Ireland; the meeting is under way and Canada is participating in parts of the talks.

CANADA IS IN EUROPE’S FOREIGN-POLICY ROOM. THE MEETING IS UNDER WAY. Current page September 1, 2026 Anita Anand joins the Sept. 1–2 EU Gymnich in Ireland; the meeting is under way and Canada is participating in parts of the talks. Europe · Foreign policy · Gymnich · Developing CANADA IS IN EUROPE’S FOREIGN-POLICY ROOM. THE MEETING IS UNDER WAY. Anita Anand’s historic Gymnich participation is happening now. The two-day meeting continues Wednesday, so Canada at War is replacing the stale preview without pretending a final outcome already exists. Published Sept. 1, 2026 · 4:52 p.m. ET WICKLOW, Ireland — Canada’s first participation by a foreign minister in an EU Gymnich is no longer a coming event. The two-day meeting opened Tuesday in Ireland, with Anita Anand among the non-EU foreign ministers invited into parts of the informal talks alongside counterparts from the United Kingdom, Iceland, Norway, Switzerland and Ukraine. The important correction to the earlier preview is timing: the Gymnich runs Sept. 1 and 2. It has not concluded. Any account presenting Tuesday afternoon as the final outcome would be premature. What is now established is that the Irish EU presidency has convened the foreign ministers around three broad subjects: Russia’s war against Ukraine, the Middle East and the effectiveness of the European Union’s common foreign and security policy. Sweden and Greece both publicly identified Canada among the invited partner countries participating in parts of the meeting. That makes Anand’s presence more than a bilateral visit to Ireland. Canada is being included in an informal European foreign-policy forum designed for political exchange outside the formal decision-making machinery of the Foreign Affairs Council. Canada does not get an EU vote and has not joined the bloc’s institutions. The significance is access. Global Affairs Canada says Anand’s participation is intended to advance the New Canada-EU Strategic Partnership of the Future and deepen work on economic security, trade diversification, defence and security. Ottawa has also tied the visit directly to the Canada-EU Leaders’ Summit scheduled for Oct. 29 and 30 in Canada. On Ukraine, Canada says Anand’s priorities include military assistance, energy security, reconstruction and accountability. Those subjects fit directly with the meeting’s first major foreign-policy theme and with Canada’s existing role as a NATO ally and major supporter of Kyiv. The wider Canadian story is strategic diversification. Ottawa is trying to reduce the number of areas in which a single external partner can exert overwhelming leverage. Europe cannot replace the United States as Canada’s economic neighbour, but deeper European ties can create additional markets, defence-industrial opportunities, technology partnerships and political options. The Gymnich invitation is evidence that this relationship is becoming more institutional. In 2025, Canada and the EU launched the Strategic Partnership of the Future and signed a Security and Defence Partnership. In 2026, the two sides have continued work on defence procurement, digital trade, critical minerals, industrial policy and energy security. The October leaders’ summit is expected to be the next major political checkpoint. Anand is also scheduled to meet Irish Foreign Minister Helen McEntee while in Ireland. The Irish presidency and EU foreign-policy chief Kaja Kallas are co-hosting the Gymnich. What Tuesday does not provide is a final communiqué or a negotiated Canada-EU agreement. Gymnich meetings are informal by design, and this one continues Wednesday. The accurate headline today is therefore that Canada has entered the room and the talks are under way — not that the meeting is over. Status: This report replaces the earlier meeting preview. The Sept. 1–2 Gymnich was still in progress when this story was published. Canada at War will treat any later formal readout as a subsequent development rather than inventing an outcome before one exists. “When the Americans stop doing memes, stop throwing shade, stop trying to be tough and start being serious about having those discussions, we can have those discussions”

Mark Carney says Canada-U.S. trade talks can resume when Washington stops the posturing and returns to serious negotiations.

“When the Americans stop doing memes, stop throwing shade, stop trying to be tough and start being serious about having those discussions, we can have those discussions” Current page September 1, 2026 Mark Carney says Canada-U.S. trade talks can resume when Washington stops the posturing and returns to serious negotiations. Canada–U.S. trade · Prime Minister · Sovereignty “When the Americans stop doing memes, stop throwing shade, stop trying to be tough and start being serious about having those discussions, we can have those discussions” Carney leaves the negotiating door open — but says Washington has to stop performing and start negotiating. Published Sept. 1, 2026 · 4:52 p.m. ET Prime Minister Mark Carney at the White House, May 6, 2025. Daniel Torok / The White House. Public domain. OTTAWA — Mark Carney has set a blunt condition for restarting Canada-U.S. trade talks: Washington must stop treating a high-stakes economic dispute as a performance. The prime minister told reporters Tuesday that negotiations could resume when the American side stops the memes, the taunts and the effort to project toughness and begins approaching the dispute seriously. It was unusually undiplomatic language from a leader who has often described the changed Canada-U.S. relationship in controlled institutional terms. But it was also an offer. Carney did not say talks were impossible. He said a mutually beneficial agreement remained possible if it respected Canadian sovereignty and if Washington returned with terms Canada could regard as durable. The immediate backdrop is the collapse of negotiations on Aug. 21. Canada said late changes to the U.S. terms were unacceptable and suspended the process rather than accept an agreement Ottawa believed would damage strategic industries and constrain Canadian decision-making. Canada’s countermeasures are scheduled to take effect Sept. 8. The published list uses different rates — 15, 25 and 50 per cent — across targeted American products, matching U.S. measures on $27.6 billion of Canadian goods. That makes Carney’s reference to memes more than a complaint about tone. Since the talks broke down, Trump has escalated the symbolic side of the dispute, including the “Lake America” order and repeated public attacks on Canada and its leadership. Those gestures generate political attention. They do not solve the questions that ended the negotiations: market access, integrated auto production and the extent to which Canada can make industrial and trade choices without an American veto. Carney argued Tuesday that the proposed U.S. terms went to the heart of that last question. His government’s position is that trade can be negotiated, but the survival of Canadian industries and the country’s capacity to make sovereign policy choices are not bargaining chips. Washington disputes that account. U.S. officials say Canada walked away from a favourable offer and argue that the economic pressure is greater on Canada than on the United States. That remains the central factual and political divide: Washington depicts the impasse as a Canadian choice; Ottawa says an agreement that subordinates key sectors or sovereignty is not an acceptable agreement. Carney’s message is therefore both rebuke and test. The rebuke is aimed at the public theatre surrounding a dispute with real consequences for workers, prices and continental supply chains. The test is whether the United States can put a reliable and economically credible proposal back on the table. For now, there is no announced date for renewed talks. The door is open, but Carney has attached a condition to it: stop performing and start negotiating. GERMANY HAS A NAME FOR AMERICA’S NEW PLACE ON THE MAP. IT IS NOT DIPLOMATIC.

Germany

GERMANY HAS A NAME FOR AMERICA’S NEW PLACE ON THE MAP. IT IS NOT DIPLOMATIC. Current page September 1, 2026 Germany Canada at War illustration inspired by Der Postillon’s satirical premise. Humour · Media Watch · Germany GERMANY HAS A NAME FOR AMERICA’S NEW PLACE ON THE MAP. IT IS NOT DIPLOMATIC. Der Postillon looked at North America, considered the current map-renaming mood and decided the United States needed a new label. Published Sept. 1, 2026 · 4:52 p.m. ET There are diplomatic communiqués. There are state visits. There are strongly worded statements. And then there is Germany’s Der Postillon , which has looked at the map of North America, considered the current mood, and decided that the United States needs a new name: “Ass of Canada.” Before anyone rings Global Affairs, this is not a dispatch from Ottawa. It is satire—very deliberately and very efficiently deployed. In a piece published Aug. 28, Der Postillon imagines Prime Minister Mark Carney using Donald Trump’s enthusiasm for renaming bodies of water as legal precedent. If a president can relabel the Gulf of Mexico the “Gulf of America,” the joke goes, why should a Canadian prime minister not look south and make a cartographic intervention of his own? The publication’s imaginary Carney offers the map-based rationale: the country currently called the United States is, quite literally, south of Canada. From there the joke proceeds with the ruthless logic of a good newsroom parody: a new acronym, a parade of countries supposedly prepared to adopt it, and a proposed flag redesign too rude to be mistaken for a Department of Canadian Heritage initiative. For Canadians arriving late to the joke, Der Postillon is Germany’s well-established satirical news outlet. Founded in 2008 by Stefan Sichermann, it publishes deadpan mock news reports in the formal voice of a newspaper and cheerfully claims a far older heritage—“honest news, independent, fast, since 1845.” That is part of the act. Its Carney piece does not pretend to report a real Canadian law, a real renaming, or a real press conference. The comedy lies in treating a surreal escalation as if it were normal government business: a leader at a podium, a map, a neighbouring country suddenly recategorized with the tidy confidence of an administrative change. There is also something useful in the fact that the line came from Germany. Canada’s relationship with the United States is usually narrated at home in the language of trade agreements, border rules, defence procurement and presidential threats. From a distance, the basic visual is more immediate: Canada, immense and north; the United States, directly below; and a global audience beginning to notice how strange the old assumptions have become. The joke is coarse, but the craftsmanship is not. Der Postillon identifies the unnerving part of this period: once political leaders start treating names, maps and national symbols as disposable props, the rest of the world is free to answer in the same register. Canada did not rename its neighbour. But Germany’s satirists have now put the thought on the map. Satire note: The Canadian actions described in Der Postillon’s source article are fictional. This story reports on and analyzes the satire; it does not present the fictional renaming as a real government action. Source Der Postillon — “Carney benennt USA in ‘Ass of Canada’ um” (German)

WASHINGTON MOCKED CANADA. THE NATIONAL POST MADE THE HUMILIATION THE HEADLINE.

Canada at War examines how the National Post framed Scott Bessent

WASHINGTON MOCKED CANADA. THE NATIONAL POST MADE THE HUMILIATION THE HEADLINE. Current page September 1, 2026 Canada at War examines how the National Post framed Scott Bessent Media Watch · National Post · Canada–U.S. trade WASHINGTON MOCKED CANADA. THE NATIONAL POST MADE THE HUMILIATION THE HEADLINE. Scott Bessent's insult was news. The editorial choice was how to package it. Canada at War believes that choice deserves scrutiny when U.S. officials are openly trying to portray Canada as weak, small and unable to resist. Published Sept. 1, 2026 · 3:04 p.m. ET The National Post promoted U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent's ridicule of Canada's military capabilities in this Aug. 31 post on X. Screenshot reproduced for the purpose of reporting on and criticizing the presentation of the story. There it was Monday evening, delivered by one of Canada's largest newspapers: “U.S. Treasury secretary taunts Canadian navy's lack of firepower: ‘Two submarines from the Edmonton mall.’” The words belonged to U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent. The headline belonged to the National Post . Those are different facts, and the distinction matters. Bessent really did mock Canada in his CNBC interview. The CNBC transcript records him saying that Canada could not engage in a tit-for-tat fight with a country 13 times larger, dismissing Ottawa's claim that it was weathering the dispute, blaming Prime Minister Mark Carney for a political shouting match and making the Edmonton Mall submarine joke. He also accused Carney of acting in his own and his party's interests and claimed Canada had walked away from the best trade deal offered to any country. The National Post did not invent those remarks. It was entitled to report them. Other Canadian outlets reported them too. Global News, for example, framed its story around Bessent's statement that the countries were “not at war” , while Edmonton coverage naturally focused on his bizarre use of the mall's former submarine attraction as a punchline. Canada at War's concern is narrower and more specific: what happens when an American official's attempt to humiliate Canada becomes the principal promotional hook used by a Canadian news organization? Journalism is not only the collection of facts. It is also selection, hierarchy and framing. A newsroom chooses what goes in the headline, what gets pushed on social media, what becomes the photograph, and which part of a long interview becomes the reason to click. In this case, the National Post chose the humiliation itself: a Trump administration official taunting the Canadian navy's “lack of firepower.” That does not establish that the newspaper agrees with Bessent. It does not establish that its editors intended to help the Trump administration. Canada at War is making neither allegation. What it does establish is visible on the screen: a senior American official tried to portray Canada as militarily insignificant and incapable of standing up to the United States, and the National Post marketed that taunt to Canadians as the centrepiece of the story. Carney answers the memes and taunts Hours later, Carney addressed the wider style of American pressure. The Guardian reported that he said serious trade discussions could resume when Americans “stop doing memes, stop throwing shade, stop trying to be tough” and begin treating the negotiations seriously. His point was that theatrical provocation is now occurring alongside a dispute with enormous economic consequences. Carney also described the behaviour as “beneath their office” and said, “It's not constructive, but that's their democracy.” The complaint reaches beyond one Bessent joke: the Canadian government is increasingly treating mockery, memes and public belittlement as part of the environment surrounding stalled trade negotiations, rather than as harmless background noise. Why the framing matters The Trump administration's pressure campaign against Canada is not conducted only through tariff schedules and negotiating texts. Its public rhetoric repeatedly carries a second message: Canada is smaller; Canada is weaker; Canada is dependent; resistance is pointless. Bessent's “13 times larger” line made that theory explicit. The submarine joke turned it into entertainment. That creates an uncomfortable editorial problem for Canadian journalism. Ignoring remarks by the U.S. Treasury secretary would be irresponsible. Canadians need to know what powerful American officials are saying about their country. But reporting an attempt at humiliation is not identical to packaging the humiliation itself as the attraction. Different outlets can legitimately make different choices. The point is not that there is a forbidden headline. The point is that those choices are worthy of criticism and comparison — particularly during a confrontation in which the larger country is openly trying to convince the smaller country that resistance cannot work. The larger Postmedia context: Canada at War has separately examined Postmedia's financial history, U.S. hedge-fund ownership links and the limits of what those facts prove. That investigation explicitly does not claim that owners direct individual headlines. Read the rewritten Postmedia investigation → A case study, not proof of a conspiracy This National Post headline should not be asked to prove more than it can prove. It is not evidence that a New Jersey hedge fund ordered an editor to write a particular line. It is not evidence that every Postmedia journalist shares one political view. It is not evidence of coordination with Washington. It is a case study in amplification. The United States Treasury secretary supplied the belittling message. A Canadian newspaper chose the belittling message as the headline. Readers can see both facts and decide what they think of the editorial judgment. There is even a final absurdity. Bessent's shopping-mall intelligence appears to have been off. West Edmonton Mall's attraction operated four submarines, not two . That factual miss is trivial compared with the trade confrontation. But it is an apt ending to a performance built around the idea that Canada should accept its own insignificance. Washington supplied the insult. The National Post supplied the headline. Canada at War believes Canadians should pay attention to both. Current page · September 1, 2026

POSTMEDIA'S FINANCIAL PUZZLE: MONEY, OWNERSHIP AND MEDIA POWER

A careful examination of Postmedia

POSTMEDIA'S FINANCIAL PUZZLE: MONEY, OWNERSHIP AND MEDIA POWER Current page September 1, 2026 A careful examination of Postmedia Analysis · Media ownership · Postmedia POSTMEDIA'S FINANCIAL PUZZLE: MONEY, OWNERSHIP AND MEDIA POWER The public record shows years of losses, debt restructuring and a major U.S. hedge-fund role in one of Canada's most influential newspaper systems. It does not show that an owner ordered a particular headline. Both facts matter. Published Sept. 1, 2026 · 3:04 p.m. ET · Rewritten and expanded for legal and factual precision What this story is — and is not — saying: Canada at War is examining documented ownership, financing, political connections and editorial power. We are not alleging that Chatham Asset Management or any other owner dictates individual Postmedia stories, that every Postmedia journalist shares one political agenda, or that Postmedia is secretly acting on instructions from Donald Trump or the U.S. government. The public evidence reviewed here does not establish those claims. Postmedia is one of the most powerful media institutions in Canada. Its network includes the National Post , major metropolitan dailies, Sun newspapers and a large collection of local and digital brands. That reach gives the company an unusual ability to influence which issues are elevated, which arguments receive repeated exposure and what political language becomes familiar across much of the country. That would make its ownership and financial structure worth examining in ordinary times. It matters even more during an intense Canada–United States confrontation in which trade, sovereignty and the political relationship with Washington have become central national questions. The starting point is not ideology. It is the balance sheet. A newspaper empire that has repeatedly lost money Canada at War reviewed Postmedia's annual reports through fiscal 2024. Since the company's creation in 2010, the record is dominated by losses rather than a conventional story of investors buying a newspaper chain, restoring profitability and collecting growing returns from the operating business. The striking exception was fiscal 2021, when Postmedia reported a $33.7-million net profit. But that result included a $63.1-million non-cash pension-settlement gain. In fiscal 2022 the company reported a net loss of $74.7 million; in 2023, $72.6 million; and in 2024, $49.7 million. At Aug. 31, 2024, Postmedia's accumulated deficit stood at approximately $1.162 billion. Those figures do not mean investors necessarily received no economic benefit. Creditors can collect interest. Debt can be bought below face value. Refinancings, exchanges, asset sales and restructurings can preserve or create value in ways that do not resemble an ordinary shareholder dividend. The complete private return of a creditor or hedge fund cannot be reconstructed from public filings alone. What the filings do establish is narrower: Postmedia has not been a steadily profitable newspaper business. The company was born from a collapse Postmedia itself was created in 2010 from the newspaper assets of Canwest after Canwest's financial collapse. Many of the best-known newspaper properties in that group had entered Canwest a decade earlier, when it acquired substantially all of Hollinger's Canadian newspaper and internet assets in 2000. That history matters because the institution Canadians now know as Postmedia did not emerge from a greenfield media startup. It inherited a national newspaper infrastructure — mastheads, newsrooms, printing operations, distribution systems and political influence — that had accumulated over generations. The assets survived even as ownership structures and financing changed dramatically. Chatham arrived through debt One of the most important financial actors in Postmedia's later history is Chatham Asset Management, a New Jersey investment firm founded by Anthony Melchiorre. In October 2016, Chatham disclosed that it had exchanged about US$178.9 million in Postmedia debt for 61.2 million variable-voting shares, representing roughly 65 per cent of that class at the time. The transaction was part of a recapitalization intended to reduce Postmedia's debt burden. The distinction between economic ownership and legal voting control is important in Canada. Canadian ownership rules and Postmedia's share structure limit how voting rights can be exercised. It would therefore be inaccurate to describe the company simply as an American corporation or to collapse the share classes into one uncomplicated claim of foreign control. It is accurate to say that Chatham became a central financial force in Postmedia through distressed debt and a debt-for-equity exchange. Why the American political context is relevant Ownership becomes politically interesting because Chatham's media and political history extends beyond Canada. Public reporting cited by Canada at War shows that Melchiorre and his wife donated more than US$100,000 to Republican candidates and party committees over a period beginning in 2010. That fact should be stated carefully: donations to Republican candidates and organizations are not, by themselves, evidence that Melchiorre personally directed Postmedia coverage or that Postmedia publications received instructions from Republican politicians. Chatham also controlled American Media Inc., the publisher of the National Enquirer , during the 2016 U.S. election. The U.S. Department of Justice later described American Media's admission that it made a US$150,000 payment in concert with a presidential campaign and principally to prevent damaging allegations from influencing the election. Reporting by the Los Angeles Times also placed Melchiorre at a White House dinner with Donald Trump and American Media chief executive David Pecker after Trump became president. Those facts make the political network relevant. They still do not prove that a Postmedia headline in Canada was ordered by Chatham or that Canadian newsrooms are puppets of a U.S. owner. The distinction is essential. Influence does not require a secret memo The strongest reason to examine a national newspaper chain is not the fantasy of a proprietor telephoning an editor every morning with a list of approved headlines. Institutions shape political life more subtly than that. They decide where resources go. They hire senior editors and executives. They determine how many reporters remain in a newsroom. They choose which commentary is syndicated. They establish priorities, cost structures, product strategies and the boundaries within which local publications operate. Over time, those decisions can affect the range and repetition of ideas reaching the public even when individual reporters work independently and conscientiously. Postmedia newsrooms contain journalists who do serious reporting. Local mastheads have their own histories and professional cultures. A story about ownership should not erase those people or pretend every article has one source of political motivation. But the opposite error would be to pretend ownership and institutional structure are irrelevant simply because no one can produce a memo saying, “write this headline.” Media power is often structural. The business of consolidation Postmedia's survival has repeatedly involved cost-cutting, newsroom reductions, publication closures, reduced print schedules, centralized functions and consolidation. Its 2015 acquisition of Sun Media's English-language newspaper operations dramatically expanded the chain and left the same company operating titles that had once competed more independently in several major Canadian markets. There is a defensible argument that consolidation kept some publications alive that might otherwise have disappeared. There is also a cost: fewer separately financed newsrooms mean fewer genuinely independent institutional centres making decisions about what to investigate, emphasize and challenge. The public-interest question is therefore more complicated than “Postmedia good” or “Postmedia bad.” It is this: what happens when a financially fragile but politically important newspaper system becomes concentrated under a small number of financial interests? A platform can have value beyond annual profit A national newspaper chain has assets that do not appear neatly as a line called “political influence” on an income statement. It reaches voters, politicians, corporate executives, public servants and opinion leaders. It can help set agendas. It can make certain arguments seem mainstream through repetition. It can decide which disputes receive sustained attention and which are treated as marginal. That does not mean Postmedia's owners necessarily maintain the company because of political influence. Canada at War has not found public evidence that proves such a motive, and we do not state it as fact. It does mean the value of a national media platform cannot be understood solely from its annual net income. Today's National Post headline is a useful example — not proof On Aug. 31, U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent went on CNBC and mocked Canada's ability to resist the United States, saying the American economy was 13 times larger and joking about Canada using “two submarines from the Edmonton Mall.” The National Post promoted the story with the headline: “U.S. Treasury secretary taunts Canadian navy's lack of firepower: ‘Two submarines from the Edmonton mall.’” Canada at War's separate Media Watch analysis argues that the framing deserves scrutiny because the humiliation itself became the promotional hook. But that headline is not evidence that Chatham ordered anything. It does not prove coordination with Washington. It should not be used as a shortcut around the burden of evidence. It is useful for a different reason: it demonstrates why editorial choices matter. Bessent supplied the insult. The newspaper chose which part of his remarks to elevate. Readers are entitled to examine that choice in the larger context of a company with enormous reach, a complicated financial history and significant U.S. ownership ties. The question Canada should keep asking For more than two decades, the newspaper infrastructure first assembled under Canwest and then carried through Postmedia has survived debt crises, collapsing print advertising, restructuring and repeated losses. The mastheads remain influential. That endurance deserves serious examination without conspiracy theories and without euphemism. The careful conclusion is not that every Postmedia story is propaganda. The evidence does not support such a statement. The careful conclusion is that ownership, financing, consolidation and political networks matter when evaluating any powerful media institution — especially one helping frame Canada's relationship with a much larger country that is currently applying economic and political pressure. Canadians do not need to assume hidden orders to ask who owns their newspapers, how those newspapers survive, what interests surround their owners and how editorial choices shape the political atmosphere. Those are ordinary democratic questions. During a sovereignty crisis, they become necessary ones. NATO’S HALIFAX LAB PUTS CANADIAN DEFENCE TECH NEAR THE FRONT OF THE QUEUE

NATO

NATO’S HALIFAX LAB PUTS CANADIAN DEFENCE TECH NEAR THE FRONT OF THE QUEUE Current page September 1, 2026 NATO Canadian maritime autonomy in action: a DRDC unmanned survey vehicle during Unmanned Warrior 2016. John F. Williams / Office of Naval Research, via Wikimedia Commons, CC BY 2.0. Representative technology; not identified by NATO as a GLOW 2026 participant. Defence · NATO · Halifax · Canadian industry NATO’S HALIFAX LAB PUTS CANADIAN DEFENCE TECH NEAR THE FRONT OF THE QUEUE GLOW is not an arms show. It is the integration stage where NATO tries to find out whether emerging systems can actually work together — while Ottawa is simultaneously opening a faster procurement route for Canadian drone and autonomous-system companies. Published Sept. 1, 2026 · 11:21 a.m. ET · Updated 1:32 p.m. ET NATO’s three-day Innovation Continuum GLOW event opened Tuesday at COVE in Dartmouth, Nova Scotia, turning Halifax harbour into part of the Alliance’s pipeline for moving experimental technology toward military use. The event runs Sept. 1 to 3 and is led by NATO Allied Command Transformation. NATO describes GLOW as the integration phase of its 2026 Innovation Continuum: systems are brought together, physically integrated and put through dry runs before the live experimentation and demonstration phase, SHINE, returns to Halifax in October. The 2026 technology priorities are unusually relevant to Canadian geography and industry: Arctic intelligence, surveillance and reconnaissance; artificial intelligence; layered counter-drone systems; next-generation targeting; smart logistics and sustainment; electronic warfare; AI-enabled command-and-control and ISR; cognitive warfare; and cyber resilience. The interesting Canadian story is not simply that NATO officials are meeting in Nova Scotia. It is that a domestic defence-tech ecosystem is growing around the same harbour where NATO is trying to shorten the distance between prototype and procurement. Canada says 22 of the 150 companies selected for NATO DIANA’s 2026 cohort are Canadian — the largest national group. COVE itself is a DIANA accelerator site, and Canada hosts 16 of the Alliance’s more than 200 DIANA test centres. The Department of National Defence says DIANA’s Rapid Adoption Service can allow Allied governments to move into research, prototype or production contracts with innovators without the usual lengthy competitive process. That matters for small Canadian firms because defence procurement has historically favoured large incumbents with the staff, capital and patience to survive long purchasing cycles. Nova Scotia already has companies working in precisely the kinds of fields NATO is emphasizing. Federal defence-industrial funding announced this year backed Sensor Technology Ltd. for AI-enabled sensing; GALAXIA Mission Systems for space and mission technology; Marine Thinking for autonomous marine systems; AML Oceanographic for ocean sensing; and Leeway Marine as it develops a new unmanned surface vessel. Other Dartmouth investments support GSTS, which works in maritime intelligence and vessel-risk systems, and MilAero Electronics Atlantic, which builds high-performance electrical assemblies for aerospace, defence and marine customers. Those firms should not be confused with a confirmed GLOW participant list. NATO’s public event material available Tuesday identifies the event, location and technology themes but does not publish a complete roster of the companies whose systems are being integrated this week. Canada at War is therefore not claiming those named Nova Scotia firms are participating in GLOW. The distinction is important — and so is the opportunity. Canada is spending heavily to build sovereign capabilities in aerospace, robotics, autonomous systems and other defence technologies while simultaneously trying to reduce reliance on American suppliers. A NATO testing and adoption network located in Canada offers domestic companies a possible route into a market much larger than the Canadian Armed Forces alone. It can also work in the other direction. Allied technology can be tested in Canadian conditions, integrated with Canadian systems and potentially adopted without every capability having to originate in the United States. A second Canadian procurement pipeline is now taking shape alongside that NATO innovation network. On Tuesday, InDro Robotics announced that it had been selected as a qualified supplier for Canada’s new Defence Drone Initiative Marketplace. The government’s own CanadaBuys record confirms InDro Robotics Inc. as one of the suppliers admitted to the arrangement. The marketplace is designed as a pre-qualified pool for uncrewed and autonomous systems, counter-drone capabilities, communications and data systems, engineering and integration, testing, training and experimentation. National Defence says the initiative is intended to strengthen domestic capacity, reduce critical supply-chain dependencies and give Canadian companies a faster route to military requirements. CanadaBuys lists 142 related award notices under the Defence Drone Initiative supply arrangement. Those notices should not be read as 142 major drone purchases: many of the initial arrangements, including InDro’s, carry a nominal value of $1. The important point is access. Once qualified, companies can compete for subsequent research, prototype, testing, demonstration and potential production work. That distinction is already becoming concrete. A separate federal solicitation for low-cost uncrewed aircraft systems was restricted to qualified suppliers in Stream 1 of the Defence Drone Initiative Marketplace, with bids due Sept. 1. In other words, admission to the marketplace can become the gate through which companies must pass before they can compete for actual military requirements. InDro provides a useful example of the kind of Canadian technology Ottawa is trying to pull through that gate. The company says it has submitted proposals involving long-range remotely piloted aircraft and ground robotics, including work to robotize an extreme-range hybrid-electric all-wheel-drive vehicle for remote operation. It also describes a long-range ISR aircraft designed for Arctic temperatures. Separately, Innovation, Science and Economic Development Canada lists InDro military technologies including Talus, an autonomous mixed-terrain electric platform designed for GPS-denied and degraded-communications environments, and the hydrogen-fuel-cell Obsidian Defense Tracker ground robot. Qualification is not a purchase order, a production contract or a guarantee that any particular InDro system will be bought by the Canadian Armed Forces. But that is precisely why the supplier pool deserves scrutiny. The question is no longer only which Canadian prototypes appear at demonstrations. It is which Canadian companies get through the procurement gate, which receive funded orders, who owns their intellectual property and supply chains, and whether successful firms remain Canadian-controlled as they scale. That makes Halifax worth watching after GLOW closes. The real measure will not be how many prototypes are demonstrated. It will be how many Canadian technologies survive integration, reach SHINE in October, win prototype or production contracts and remain Canadian-owned as they scale. Sources NATO Allied Command Transformation — Innovation Continuum 2026 NATO ACT Innovation Branch — GLOW, Sept. 1–3 National Defence — Canada and NATO DIANA ACOA — Nova Scotia defence-technology investments National Defence — Defence Drone Initiative CanadaBuys — InDro Robotics Defence Drone Initiative supply arrangement InDro Robotics — qualified-supplier announcement and technology details ISED — Canadian defence innovations eligible for additional sales Wikimedia Commons — image licence and provenance

AMERICANS TELL TRUMP: COMPROMISE WITH CANADA

A Reuters/Ipsos poll finds Americans oppose additional tariffs on Canada and overwhelmingly prefer compromise to a hard-line trade stance.

AMERICANS TELL TRUMP: COMPROMISE WITH CANADA Current page September 1, 2026 A Reuters/Ipsos poll finds Americans oppose additional tariffs on Canada and overwhelmingly prefer compromise to a hard-line trade stance. United States · Canada–U.S. trade · Reuters/Ipsos poll AMERICANS TELL TRUMP: COMPROMISE WITH CANADA The striking number in a new U.S. poll is not simply that Americans dislike the tariffs. It is that they reject the negotiating theory behind them. Published Sept. 1, 2026 · 11:21 a.m. ET 68% want trade-offs. 25% want the hard line. Donald Trump has presented the confrontation with Canada as an exercise in leverage: use the size of the American market, tariffs and the threat of still more economic pain to force Ottawa to accept Washington’s terms. A new Reuters/Ipsos poll suggests most Americans do not want their government to negotiate with Canada that way. The national survey found 68 per cent of U.S. adults believe the United States should be willing to make trade-offs with Canada even if Washington does not get most of what it wants. Only 25 per cent said the United States should take a tough stance and insist on getting most of what it wants. That may be the more consequential finding than the poll’s headline tariff number. On the tariffs themselves, 57 per cent oppose additional U.S. tariffs on Canada, while 20 per cent support them. Another 21 per cent said they did not know. The poll was conducted by Ipsos from Aug. 28 to 30 using its probability-based KnowledgePanel and included 1,023 U.S. adults. Ipsos describes the sample as nationally representative. The public also assigns responsibility for the dispute very differently from the Trump administration. Forty-six per cent say the United States bears more responsibility for the current trade dispute. Twelve per cent put more responsibility on Canada, while 18 per cent blame the two countries equally. There is a pocketbook warning in the results as well. Forty per cent expect the tariffs between Canada and the United States to have a mostly negative effect on their own financial situation. Only four per cent expect a mostly positive effect; 27 per cent expect little effect and 28 per cent are unsure. For Canada, those numbers matter because Washington’s leverage does not exist in a political vacuum. The administration can impose economic costs on Canada, but the domestic political sustainability of that pressure depends partly on whether Americans believe the confrontation is worth the cost. The poll does not prove voters will punish Republicans over Canada in November. Nor does it establish that public opposition will change White House policy. Foreign policy and tariff powers can be exercised even when opinion is hostile. But it does show a large gap between the administration’s maximalist posture and the negotiating posture preferred by the public it represents. The same gap appears in Trump’s symbolic pressure campaign. Sixty-three per cent oppose his executive order renaming Lake Ontario “Lake America”; 14 per cent support it. Opposition includes 84 per cent of Democrats and 65 per cent of independents. Even Republicans are more likely to oppose the renaming than support it, 43 per cent to 33 per cent. That makes the poll more than another approval-rating story. Canada’s central strategic problem is how much economic and political pressure Washington can sustain while Ottawa refuses terms it considers unacceptable. Reuters and Ipsos have now measured one limit on that power: the American public is not asking Trump to squeeze Canada harder. By nearly three to one, it is asking him to compromise. Sources Ipsos — full poll release and methodology Reuters — poll report
Current page · September 1, 2026

THE SECOND GATE OPENS FOR CHINESE EVS

Canada’s second Chinese-EV quota period begins Sept. 1 with 24,500 fresh slots plus unused first-period capacity. Here are models, estimated prices and ranges to watch.

THE SECOND GATE OPENS FOR CHINESE EVS Current page September 1, 2026 Canada’s second Chinese-EV quota period begins Sept. 1 with 24,500 fresh slots plus unused first-period capacity. Here are models, estimated prices and ranges to watch. China · Electric vehicles · Trade diversification THE SECOND GATE OPENS FOR CHINESE EVS Canada’s second import period starts today with 24,500 fresh slots plus unused first-period capacity. Now the question is which automakers turn quota access into Canadian showroom cars. Published Sept. 1, 2026 · 4:25 a.m. ET At 12:00 a.m. today, Canada opened the second six-month window of its new quota for electric vehicles built in China. The immediate number is 24,500 vehicles. The more interesting number is larger: Ottawa’s rules add any quota left unused from March through August. Global Affairs Canada’s last published utilization update before the deadline showed 15,603 of the first period’s 24,500 slots used as of Aug. 28. If no large last-minute shipment changed that total, roughly 8,897 unused places would carry into the new period — potentially giving importers room for about 33,397 China-origin vehicles between Sept. 1 and Feb. 28. That does not mean 33,397 new Chinese-brand cars will suddenly appear in Canadian dealerships. The quota is based on where a vehicle is substantially manufactured, not the nationality of the badge. Shanghai-built Teslas, Chinese-built vehicles from Western brands and demonstration vehicles can all count. It also takes time to certify vehicles, build dealer networks, stock parts, train technicians and convince buyers to trust a brand they may never have seen. But the policy gate is now unmistakably open. The 100 per cent tariff is no longer the rule inside the quota Canada imposed a 100 per cent surtax on Chinese-made EVs in 2024. Under the Canada-China arrangement implemented March 1, 2026, eligible vehicles that receive a quota permit are instead assessed at the normal 6.1 per cent most-favoured-nation tariff. The annual first-year quota is 49,000 vehicles, split into two periods. Global Affairs Canada’s new Notice 1168 says the second period runs from Sept. 1 through Feb. 28, with permits issued to eligible importers on a first-come, first-served basis. That is a major change in economics. A car that was effectively priced out of Canada by a 100 per cent surtax can now compete if the manufacturer secures quota space and satisfies Canadian vehicle rules. The old story was whether Canada would allow Chinese-made EVs in meaningful numbers. The new story is which manufacturers will turn access into actual retail sales. Three models worth watching The models below are not a promise that all three will be sold in Canada. They are useful examples of the price-and-range pressure Chinese manufacturers could bring. Chery has confirmed a Canadian launch plan; the exact launch model or models were not yet settled when it announced its plans. BYD and Zeekr are more speculative model-by-model, so their figures should be read as reference points rather than Canadian MSRPs. BYD Dolphin Estimated Canadian price: about C$35,000 Range: roughly 211–427 km, depending on battery and trim AutoTrader identifies the Dolphin as the kind of sub-$40,000 electric hatchback that could fill a conspicuous hole in the Canadian market. BYD has not announced a Canadian Dolphin MSRP, so the C$35,000 figure is an estimate, not a dealer price. Chery Omoda E5 Estimated Canadian price: about C$35,000–45,000 Range: roughly 400–430 km This one matters because Chery has publicly said it aims to sell vehicles in Canada by the end of 2026, likely beginning with one or two models from one of its brands. Omoda is among the brands it controls. The E5’s 61-kWh battery and compact-SUV shape put it directly in one of Canada’s most important vehicle segments. Chery has not confirmed that the E5 itself will be the launch model. Zeekr 001 Reference price: about C$98,000 Range: up to about 620 km in long-range rear-drive form Zeekr is part of China’s Geely group, which already has deep connections to brands Canadians know, including Volvo, Polestar and Lotus. AutoTrader has highlighted the 001 as a model it believes could fit Canada. It would not be the cheap-China story: it is a premium electric shooting brake aimed at high-end European rivals. There is no announced Canadian 001 price or confirmed Canadian launch. Why Canadians have not noticed the quota yet The first import period did not produce a wave of unfamiliar Chinese badges. Drive Tesla Canada, using Global Affairs Canada’s utilization data and known shipments, reported that much of the volume was likely Shanghai-built Tesla vehicles. Lotus has also brought China-built Eletre vehicles into the country. The federal data identifies customs categories, not a public model-by-model scoreboard. That distinction is politically important. “Chinese EV imports” can mean a Chinese-owned Chinese brand, a U.S. brand building in Shanghai, or a European-linked brand whose vehicle comes off a Chinese assembly line. Ottawa’s rules focus on origin: a vehicle substantially manufactured in China falls under the quota, even if the logo on the hood is familiar. Price could be the disruptive part Canada’s new-vehicle market has an affordability problem. AutoTrader has noted how thin the selection of genuinely inexpensive battery-electric vehicles remains. That is why a Dolphin near C$35,000 or an Omoda E5 in the C$35,000–45,000 band would matter more to the mass market than another six-figure electric SUV. There are still constraints. Quota access is limited. Federal incentive rules do not automatically make every Chinese import eligible for subsidies. Canadian crash, lighting and other regulatory requirements must be met. Dealers need parts and service. Concerns about connected-vehicle data and cybersecurity will follow Chinese brands into Canada. And Washington maintains a much harder policy toward Chinese vehicle technology, creating questions for Canadians who regularly drive across the U.S. border. None of those issues erases the central change. Canada has chosen a different path from the United States: managed access rather than an effectively prohibitive tariff wall. Today is the first real test of that choice The second quota window begins with more room than the headline 24,500 suggests because unused first-period capacity rolls over. Importers can apply for shipment-specific permits up to 30 days before expected entry, and Global Affairs Canada says permits will continue to be issued first-come, first-served until further notice. That means the infrastructure is in place for a faster second half than the first. Chery is preparing a Canadian entry. BYD has been openly discussed as a likely contender. Geely’s family of brands already has pathways into the market. The question over the next six months is whether those plans become showroom inventory — and whether the arrival of lower-priced, longer-range Chinese EVs forces every automaker selling in Canada to respond. For consumers, the numbers to watch are no longer a 100 per cent surtax and a closed door. They are 6.1 per cent, a quota with unused capacity, and the price tags manufacturers decide to put in Canadian windows. Sources Global Affairs Canada: Electric vehicle imports from China Global Affairs Canada Notice 1168 — second quota period Global Affairs Canada Notice 1162 — 6.1% tariff and repeal of the 100% surtax Drive Tesla Canada: first window ends with nearly 9,000 spots unused AutoTrader: Which segments should Chinese automakers target in Canada? AutoTrader: First Chinese EV maker confirms plans to launch in Canada AutoTrader: 10 EVs from China that should be sold in Canada
