REPUBLICANS SHUT CBC OUT OF MIDTERM CONVENTION

The Republican National Committee has revoked CBC/Radio-Canada media accreditation for its Sept. 9-10 midterm convention in Dallas.

REPUBLICANS SHUT CBC OUT OF MIDTERM CONVENTION Current page September 1, 2026 The Republican National Committee has revoked CBC/Radio-Canada media accreditation for its Sept. 9-10 midterm convention in Dallas. United States · Media · Press access REPUBLICANS SHUT CBC OUT OF MIDTERM CONVENTION RNC revokes accreditation for CBC/Radio-Canada as tensions with Canada spread from trade and diplomacy into the media. Published Sept. 1, 2026 · 12:49 a.m. ET The Republican Party has revoked the press credentials of CBC/Radio-Canada for its coming midterm convention in Dallas, pushing a dispute involving Canada’s public broadcaster into the centre of an American political event. CBC head of public affairs Chuck Thompson confirmed Monday that the Republican National Committee would no longer provide accreditation for CBC/Radio-Canada journalists at the Sept. 9-10 convention. He called the decision “disappointing” and said the broadcaster hoped it would be reconsidered. CBC reported that convention organizers had not immediately responded to its request for an explanation. The decision follows days of Republican criticism of CBC over the broadcaster’s language guidance concerning the Sept. 11, 2001 attacks. A leaked internal memo drew attention to longstanding CBC guidance that generally advised journalists not to describe acts as terrorism without attribution. Republican lawmakers and other U.S. officials attacked the policy after it became public. CBC subsequently changed the guidance. Basem Boshra, the broadcaster’s senior director of journalistic standards and public trust, said the organization would make clear that journalists do not require direct attribution when describing the Sept. 11 attacks as terrorism. CBC said its previous approach was about consistent language standards, not a denial of the nature of the attacks. National Post reported that the RNC specifically cited the controversy when it withdrew CBC and Radio-Canada’s previously approved credentials. The revocation therefore came even after CBC clarified its policy. The convention itself is an unusually prominent midterm event. President Donald Trump and Vice-President JD Vance are scheduled to headline the two-day gathering, which Republicans are using to promote their candidates and national message heading into the November elections. The dispute also raises a broader press-access question. Political parties control credentials for their own conventions, and accreditation is not the same thing as a legal right to enter a private political event. But denying access to a major foreign public broadcaster because party officials object to its journalism turns that administrative power into a political sanction. CBC can still report on the Republican Party from outside the convention. What the RNC controls is direct access to speeches, delegates, officials and the activity inside the hall. Against the backdrop of the wider Canada-U.S. confrontation, that distinction matters. The two countries are already fighting over tariffs, industrial policy and sovereignty. Now a Canadian national broadcaster has become part of the conflict. Sources CBC News report (Yahoo mirror) Associated Press: CBC changes 9/11 language policy Associated Press: Republican midterm convention
Current page · September 1, 2026

HOW TO GET OFF AMERICAN TECH — AND WHAT TO USE INSTEAD

A practical Canadian guide to non-U.S. alternatives for email, search, maps, browsers, office software, cloud storage, phones and more, built from Paris Marx’s guide and independent checks.

HOW TO GET OFF AMERICAN TECH — AND WHAT TO USE INSTEAD Current page September 1, 2026 A practical Canadian guide to non-U.S. alternatives for email, search, maps, browsers, office software, cloud storage, phones and more, built from Paris Marx’s guide and independent checks. Digital sovereignty · Practical guide · Canada HOW TO GET OFF AMERICAN TECH — AND WHAT TO USE INSTEAD Paris Marx’s guide to non-U.S. technology is surging after the Lake America map fight. Here is a practical Canadian version — from email and search to maps, office software, phones, hosting, streaming and AI. Published Sept. 1, 2026 · 4:25 a.m. ET Donald Trump’s decision to rename Lake Ontario “Lake America” has turned an argument about digital sovereignty into something Canadians can see on a phone screen. Google Maps adopted the new U.S. federal name for American users. That, in turn, sent readers back to a practical guide maintained by St. John’s writer and podcaster Paris Marx: a catalogue of ways to move everyday digital life away from American technology companies. Marx, host of Tech Won’t Save Us and author of the Disconnect newsletter, began the project before the lake-name fight. His point is larger than one president or one map. Canada has allowed a remarkable amount of its communications, documents, searches, cloud storage, maps, phones and business infrastructure to sit inside systems controlled by a handful of U.S. corporations. A consumer cannot undo that architecture alone. But consumers and businesses can reduce how many points of dependency they have — and governments can make those choices easier by building alternatives. This is a practical Canada at War guide to the strongest non-U.S. options in Marx’s catalogue, checked against the companies and projects themselves. It is not a claim that every service below is perfectly independent of American infrastructure. In fact, that caveat is central to the story: a European or Canadian app can still rent servers from Amazon, Microsoft or Google. Digital sovereignty is a supply-chain problem as much as an app-store problem. Email, cloud storage and the easiest first move Proton Mail PROTON MAIL · SWITZERLAND For an ordinary household, email is probably the cleanest place to start. Marx’s own anchor is Proton, the Swiss privacy company whose suite now includes Proton Mail, Drive, Calendar, Pass, VPN, Docs and other tools. One account can therefore replace pieces of Gmail, Google Drive, Google Calendar, Google Docs and some security utilities without forcing a user to assemble six unrelated products. There are other credible choices. Tuta and Posteo are German email providers. Infomaniak is Swiss and offers a broader cloud-and-productivity suite. Zoho is an Indian company with mail, office and business tools. Mailbox.org is German. Fastmail is Australian, though users who are trying to minimize U.S. infrastructure should still examine where any service hosts the particular data they care about. The practical rule is not “find a perfect flag.” It is to ask three questions: Who owns the service? Under whose law does it operate? And whose infrastructure does it depend on underneath? Web browsers: leaving Chrome without making the web painful Vivaldi VIVALDI · NORWAY Marx has settled on Vivaldi, developed by a Norwegian company founded by former Opera executives. It is deliberately aimed at people who want a full-featured browser rather than a stripped-down privacy experiment, and it synchronizes across major desktop and mobile platforms. For many users, that makes it a more realistic replacement for Chrome, Edge or Safari than a technically demanding browser that requires constant tinkering. There is an important limitation: Vivaldi is built on Chromium, the open-source browser engine that also underpins Chrome and many competitors. Moving to Vivaldi changes the company that provides the browser and its services, but it does not completely escape an ecosystem whose technical direction is heavily influenced by Google. Firefox-based LibreWolf is a more radical alternative, but Marx notes that it is less friendly for ordinary users. The lesson repeats throughout this guide: independence comes in degrees. Search: Qwant, Mojeek and Europe’s unfinished index Qwant QWANT · FRANCE Search is harder because Google’s advantage is not merely a website; it is the enormous index underneath it. Marx currently rates France’s Qwant as his best non-U.S. choice. Germany’s Ecosia and the Netherlands’ Startpage are alternatives, while Britain’s Mojeek is notable for operating its own search index. Qwant and Ecosia began serving some results from a shared European search index in 2025. That project matters strategically because a search service that simply resells results from Microsoft Bing or Google has changed the storefront without eliminating the dependency. Marx says he now trusts Qwant with more searches than he did when he began his experiment, but still returns to Google for some difficult queries. That is useful honesty: migration does not have to be absolute to reduce dependence. Maps: the category the Lake America fight made political Organic Maps ORGANIC MAPS · ESTONIA Maps are where this suddenly stopped feeling abstract. Marx’s strongest general-purpose non-U.S. option is HERE WeGo, operated by the Netherlands-based mapping company HERE Technologies. TomTom Go, also Dutch, is strong for driving. Canada’s Transit is built around public transportation. For walking, cycling and offline navigation, the OpenStreetMap ecosystem opens another route entirely. Organic Maps, based in Estonia, packages OpenStreetMap data in a simple offline-friendly app. OsmAnd is another OpenStreetMap-based option associated with the Netherlands. Mapy is Czech. Magic Earth is Dutch. None necessarily reproduces every Google Maps feature, review database or business listing, but they prove that navigation itself does not require a Google or Apple account. For Canadians irritated that a geopolitical decision in Washington can flow through an American platform and alter what millions of people see on a map, keeping a second mapping app is a particularly easy form of redundancy. Documents and office work: the Microsoft problem is solvable LibreOffice LIBREOFFICE · EUROPE Microsoft Office and Google Workspace are deeply embedded in Canadian institutions, but basic document work has many alternatives. LibreOffice, backed by the Germany-based Document Foundation, remains the most obvious installed desktop suite. It handles word processing, spreadsheets and presentations without requiring a cloud account. For collaboration, the picture is broader. Nextcloud, developed by a German company and open-source community, can provide file storage, calendars, contacts, calls and collaborative editing when paired with office tools. Collabora is based in Britain. Proton has been adding web-based documents and spreadsheets. Zoho provides a mature Indian alternative. CryptPad, developed in France, emphasizes encrypted collaboration. Compatibility matters more here than ideology. A business that exchanges complex Excel models or heavily formatted Word files with American clients may not be able to leave Microsoft overnight. It can still move internal notes, routine documents, archives and some collaboration elsewhere. A staged migration reduces operational risk. Passwords, authentication and personal security Marx moved his password manager and two-factor authentication codes into Proton’s suite. Other non-U.S. options include KeePass, an open-source project with roots in Germany, and Aegis for Android, developed in the Netherlands. Canada’s 1Password deserves a special asterisk: it is Canadian, but Marx says the company told him it uses Amazon Web Services and did not plan to migrate away. That is exactly why nationality alone is not enough to answer the sovereignty question. Security software is one category where a rushed political switch would be foolish. Export credentials carefully, confirm the new vault works on every device, save recovery material, then delete the old account only after the migration is proven. Phones and hardware: the hardest dependency Fairphone FAIRPHONE · NETHERLANDS Hardware is more difficult because a phone is an ecosystem, not just a slab of electronics. Marx points to Fairphone, the Dutch manufacturer known for repairable devices and longer support. Fairphone can also be paired with de-Googled Android options for users who want to reduce Google’s role further. Other hardware makers outside the United States include HMD in Finland, Sony in Japan, Asus and Acer in Taiwan, Lenovo in China, and Samsung and LG in South Korea. Kobo, founded in Canada and now owned by Japan’s Rakuten, is an alternative to Amazon’s Kindle. Norway’s reMarkable competes in digital notebooks. But even a non-American handset can contain U.S.-designed chips, U.S. intellectual property, American apps or Google services. Hardware shows why the serious policy conversation has to include semiconductor design, cloud computing, operating systems and procurement — not just consumer branding. Messaging and social media: sometimes the network wins This is where Marx’s experiment becomes deliberately pragmatic. He has acknowledged continuing to use some American services because the people he needs to reach are there. Signal, WhatsApp and Discord cannot be replaced merely by installing a technically superior app if everyone in a family, workplace or community remains on the old network. Mastodon, whose decentralized network has strong European roots, offers a different model for social media. But the realistic strategy may be to add alternatives before deleting incumbents: build contacts elsewhere, move communities gradually, and make sure no single platform is the only way people can reach you. Video calls, translation, design and the long tail Norway’s Whereby offers browser-based video meetings, while Jitsi and Nextcloud provide open-source paths. For translation, Germany’s DeepL is the obvious heavyweight alternative to Google Translate; France’s Reverso and South Korea’s Papago cover other needs. For graphics, Britain’s Affinity competes with Adobe’s creative tools, Australia’s Canva has become a mass-market design platform, and GIMP remains an open-source image editor. Money transfer offers another familiar non-U.S. name: Wise is British. For hosting, Marx moved his own sites to FullHost in Canada. For cloud storage, Canada’s Sync.com, Switzerland’s pCloud and Tresorit, and Proton Drive provide alternatives. Podcast publishers can look at Britain’s Captivate; independent publishers can examine Ghost, headquartered in Singapore. France’s Dailymotion and the decentralized PeerTube project provide non-U.S. video options. Streaming and media: Canada already has part of the answer American streaming services dominate television and music, but this is one area where Canadians already have domestic choices. CBC Gem, Crave and the National Film Board sit alongside non-U.S. services such as France’s Deezer, Britain’s Mubi and BritBox. No single service reproduces Netflix, Spotify or YouTube, but the practical question is whether every entertainment subscription needs to send money and viewing data through the same national ecosystem. AI: there is no clean substitution yet Artificial intelligence is the newest and perhaps most strategically sensitive layer. Europe has AI companies such as France’s Mistral, Canada has important research institutions and firms, and open-weight models can be run through non-U.S. infrastructure. But the leading consumer AI market is intertwined with American cloud providers, chip designers, model companies and app platforms. Anyone promising a simple national substitute is skipping several layers of the stack. For governments and businesses, this is where procurement matters. Sensitive Canadian data can be required to remain under Canadian or allied jurisdiction; workloads can be diversified among providers; open standards can reduce lock-in; and contracts can include portability requirements so switching is technically possible before a crisis makes it urgent. The point is not purity. It is leverage. Marx’s guide is most useful when read as a map of dependencies rather than a shopping list. A Canadian can switch Gmail to Proton, Chrome to Vivaldi, Google Search to Qwant, Google Maps to HERE or Organic Maps, Microsoft Office to LibreOffice for some work, and a conventional Android phone to Fairphone. That can meaningfully reduce exposure to a handful of U.S. corporations. It cannot create sovereign infrastructure by itself. The deeper task belongs to Ottawa, provinces, universities and Canadian business. Public procurement can support domestic hosting and open standards. Governments can insist that critical records remain portable. Canadian cloud and cybersecurity capacity can be treated as infrastructure rather than a boutique sector. Europe can be a partner rather than merely another market. And consumers should be able to choose a non-U.S. service without discovering that it secretly sits on the same American hyperscale cloud. The Lake America episode provided a vivid demonstration of why this matters. A political decision in Washington did not remain inside a federal register. It travelled through a private American technology platform and became part of the digital reality presented to users. Digital sovereignty is the ability to decide that Canada should have another route. Logo note: Six brand/project marks from Simple Icons are used for editorial identification. Brand names and trademarks remain the property of their respective owners. The Falklands test: when an ally’s sovereignty becomes leverage.

Donald Trump says Washington is reviewing its position on the Falkland Islands. The significance for Canada is not a prediction about Canadian territory, but a question about whether an ally

The Falklands test: when an ally’s sovereignty becomes leverage. Current page September 1, 2026 Donald Trump says Washington is reviewing its position on the Falkland Islands. The significance for Canada is not a prediction about Canadian territory, but a question about whether an ally Sovereignty Watch · United States · United Kingdom · Argentina The Falklands test: when an ally’s sovereignty becomes leverage. Donald Trump says Washington is reviewing its position on the Falkland Islands. For Canada, the important question is not whether the South Atlantic dispute is identical to Canada’s own confrontation with Washington. It plainly is not. The question is what happens when the territorial position of an ally can enter the bargaining inventory of American power. Published Aug. 31, 2026 · 8:54 p.m. ET · Analysis OTTAWA — Donald Trump needed only a few words Monday to open a question that reaches far beyond a remote group of islands in the South Atlantic. Asked in the Oval Office about the sovereignty of the Falkland Islands, the British-administered territory that Argentina calls the Malvinas and claims as its own, the U.S. president confirmed that Washington’s position is under review. “I always review every position. That’s just one of many,” Trump said, according to Reuters. He did not say that the United States had changed its policy. He did not endorse Argentina’s sovereignty claim. He did not announce negotiations, sanctions or any other concrete measure. Those distinctions matter. A review is not a reversal, and speculation about where it may lead should not be reported as policy. But the fact of the review matters for another reason. Reuters reported that a possible change in the American position on the Falklands had appeared among options considered by the Pentagon earlier this year as Washington looked at ways to pressure NATO allies it believed had failed to support U.S. military operations in the war with Iran. That connection changes the character of the story. If the Falklands question were being reviewed only as part of ordinary South Atlantic diplomacy, it would primarily be a dispute among Britain, Argentina, the islanders and the international institutions in which the issue has been argued for decades. But if the American position on the territory can also be contemplated as leverage against Britain over an unrelated military disagreement, then sovereignty itself has entered the toolbox of pressure politics. For Canada, the warning is about method: an ally’s territorial position can become part of a negotiation about something else. That does not make Britain’s Falklands dispute equivalent to Canada’s current relationship with the United States. The histories, legal arguments, populations and geography are entirely different. Canada is a sovereign state. The Falklands are a self-governing British Overseas Territory claimed by Argentina. Canada and the United States share the world’s longest international border and an economic relationship of extraordinary depth. The Falklands are an archipelago thousands of kilometres from Britain over which Britain and Argentina fought a war in 1982. Those differences are precisely why the comparison should be made carefully. The relevant parallel is not the territorial claim. It is the use of territorial and sovereignty questions as instruments in wider political bargaining. What Washington has — and has not — done The United States has generally avoided taking a formal side on ultimate Falklands sovereignty since the 1982 war, while recognizing Britain’s de facto administration of the islands. Trump’s statement Monday does not itself alter that position. Argentina’s claim is longstanding and predates the current governments in both Washington and Buenos Aires. President Javier Milei has continued to assert Argentine sovereignty over the islands, even as he has cultivated an unusually close political relationship with Trump. Britain’s position is equally clear. London says it has no doubt about British sovereignty and places the islanders’ right to self-determination at the centre of its argument. In a June statement at the Organization of American States, the British government pointed to the 2013 referendum in which 99.8 per cent of participating Falkland Islanders voted to retain their status as a British Overseas Territory, on turnout of 92 per cent. The British statement went further: there can be no negotiation over sovereignty without the agreement and participation of the islanders themselves. That principle is not merely rhetorical in current British strategy. The United Kingdom’s National Security Strategy says sovereignty over its Overseas Territories must be protected against all challenges so that their inhabitants’ right of self-determination is upheld. It specifically commits Britain to maintaining a military presence in the Falklands, as well as in Gibraltar and other strategically important territories. The UK’s 2025 Strategic Defence Review likewise calls for a defensive military posture in the Falkland Islands, South Georgia and the South Sandwich Islands. In other words, the issue Trump says Washington is reviewing is not peripheral to British policy. London has formally classified protection of the islands and their inhabitants’ political choice as a national-security responsibility. The Canadian relevance Canada has spent much of 2026 learning that assumptions about the political limits of its relationship with the United States can no longer simply be treated as permanent facts. The current tariff confrontation has already moved beyond an ordinary argument over rates and market access. Trump has repeatedly spoken about Canada in territorial and political terms. American political figures have discussed Canada’s status and, more recently, Alberta’s possible separation. U.S. officials have applied economic pressure while Ottawa has been forced to reconsider defence procurement, supply chains, energy strategy, industrial policy and the degree to which Canadian prosperity can safely depend on one overwhelmingly dominant trading partner. None of that means the United States is preparing a Falklands-style policy toward Canada. There is no evidence of that, and Canada at War is not suggesting it. The Falklands episode is significant because it offers a concrete test of a broader proposition: can Washington treat its diplomatic position on an ally’s territorial sovereignty as a bargaining asset when seeking concessions on a different issue? If the answer is yes, then smaller and middle powers have to think differently about the architecture of alliances. Alliances have always contained bargaining. Governments trade support, access, intelligence, basing arrangements, procurement decisions and diplomatic backing. The postwar Western system was never free of coercion or power disparities. But it rested in part on the expectation that certain foundational commitments — territorial integrity, treaty obligations and the political independence of allies — were more durable than the transactional disputes of the day. The concern raised by the Falklands review is that the boundary between foundational commitments and negotiable leverage may be becoming less clear. Britain’s own answer: more sovereign capacity There is another reason Canadians should read Britain’s strategy closely. The British National Security Strategy is explicit that an era of greater uncertainty requires stronger sovereign capabilities. It calls for Britain to reduce vulnerabilities to coercion, rebuild its defence industrial base, protect strategic infrastructure and retain enough national capacity to make decisions independently. That language could have been written for the debate now taking place in Canada. Canada’s response to American tariffs increasingly involves the same concepts: diversified markets, domestic industrial capacity, critical minerals, energy infrastructure, defence production, European partnerships and less dependence on a single external supplier or customer. Finance Minister François-Philippe Champagne used the G20 meeting in Asheville on Monday to emphasize trade diversification, critical minerals, energy and potash. Foreign Minister Anita Anand is heading into deeper consultations with European counterparts. Ottawa is examining procurement and industrial choices through a sovereignty lens that would have seemed unusually stark only a short time ago. The Falklands development does not prove that those Canadian policies are correct in every detail. It does reinforce the strategic premise behind them: political dependence can turn into economic or diplomatic vulnerability when the larger partner decides that previously separate issues belong in the same negotiation. Self-determination and the danger of selective principles There is also an uncomfortable question of principle. Britain argues that the islanders themselves should determine their political future. Argentina rejects the way Britain applies self-determination to the dispute and maintains its sovereignty claim. Those positions have been contested internationally for generations. Washington does not need to resolve that dispute in order for the current review to have consequences. If its diplomatic position changes because American officials independently conclude that the merits of the sovereignty question demand a change, that is one thing. If its position changes because Britain is being punished over Iran, NATO burden-sharing or another unrelated disagreement, that is something very different. It would mean the principle being applied to the territory was affected by the usefulness of the territory as leverage. That distinction is important for Canada because Ottawa’s security depends heavily on systems built around rules that are supposed to survive changes in bilateral mood: NATO, NORAD, intelligence arrangements, treaty commitments and an enormous network of cross-border institutions. Canada cannot make itself independent of the United States, nor would attempting to sever the relationship make strategic or economic sense. Geography alone makes deep co-operation unavoidable. But Canada can reduce the number of areas in which disagreement with Washington leaves it without practical alternatives. That is the difference between autonomy and isolation. Watch what happens next The immediate story now has several tests. First, does the Trump administration conduct a genuine policy review, and which agencies participate? Second, does the White House or State Department articulate a new position on sovereignty or self-determination? Third, does Washington explicitly connect the Falklands issue to British behaviour in NATO, Iran or another unrelated file? Fourth, how does London respond if the review advances beyond Trump’s brief acknowledgment? And fifth, does Milei’s government treat the opening as a diplomatic opportunity and seek a formal American shift? Until those questions are answered, it would be premature to describe the United States as having abandoned its longstanding posture. But it would be equally mistaken to dismiss Trump’s comment as merely another passing remark. Reuters’ reporting supplies the context that gives the review strategic significance: a possible Falklands shift had already been considered as one way to pressure NATO allies. For Canada, that makes the story worth watching closely. Ottawa’s confrontation with Washington is ostensibly about tariffs, trade balances and market access. Yet the Canadian debate has expanded into defence, sovereignty, provincial politics, maps, energy, investment and the country’s relationship with Europe. The lesson of the past year is that issues once kept in separate diplomatic compartments can be pulled together very quickly. The Falklands are far from Canada. The principle at stake is not. When a powerful ally begins reviewing whether its position on another ally’s territory might be useful in a different dispute, the question for every smaller partner becomes unavoidable: which commitments are fixed, and which have quietly become bargaining chips? Sources and further reading Reuters, Aug. 31, 2026 — Trump says he is reviewing U.S. position on Falkland Islands UK Foreign, Commonwealth & Development Office, June 25, 2026 — Falkland Islands statement to the OAS UK Government — National Security Strategy 2025 UK Government — Strategic Defence Review 2025

Danielle Smith takes her case to American television.

Alberta Premier Danielle Smith is making a public appeal for a tariff-free Canada–U.S. relationship while declining to make Alberta oil a bargaining chip.

Danielle Smith takes her case to American television. Current page August 31, 2026 Alberta Premier Danielle Smith is making a public appeal for a tariff-free Canada–U.S. relationship while declining to make Alberta oil a bargaining chip. Alberta · Canada–U.S. trade · Public diplomacy Danielle Smith takes her case to American television. The Alberta premier is urging a return to tariff-free trade and negotiations—while holding fast to her view that oil and gas should not become Canada’s bargaining weapon. Published Aug. 31, 2026 · 7:02 p.m. ET EDMONTON — Danielle Smith’s latest intervention in the Canada–U.S. trade fight is not aimed principally at Ottawa. It is aimed at Americans. After appearing on Fox Business on Sunday, Alberta’s premier said she had used the interview to advocate for a tariff-free relationship and for the two federal governments to resume negotiations. Her public message was straightforward: tariffs and counter-tariffs raise costs, disrupt supply chains and put businesses under pressure on both sides of the border. That is a familiar Smith argument, but the audience matters. The collapse of the Canada–U.S. talks has pushed provincial leaders into a kind of public diplomacy: trying to reach American voters, companies and elected officials directly, rather than leaving every message to federal negotiators. Ontario Premier Doug Ford has taken a more combative version of that campaign to U.S. television. Smith is choosing a different register—appeal, economic interdependence and a negotiated off-ramp. The difference is not merely stylistic. Alberta’s economy is unusually exposed to the American market through energy, petrochemicals, agriculture and a web of cross-border industrial supply chains. Smith’s political instinct has been to preserve the commercial relationship while arguing that escalation threatens workers on both sides. Her pitch is that a tariff fight makes neither country stronger. There is also a clear limit to her approach. Smith has repeatedly opposed using Alberta oil and gas exports as a retaliatory lever. Supporters see that as a defence of a sector that depends on stable access to U.S. buyers. Critics argue it narrows Canada’s bargaining room at precisely the moment Washington is demonstrating a willingness to use economic power for political aims. That tension is now central to the Canadian debate. A country facing coercive tariffs wants negotiation; it also needs credible leverage. The federal government has chosen counter-tariffs scheduled to take effect Sept. 8. Smith’s position is that the pressure should not be allowed to rupture the underlying partnership—especially the energy relationship that links Alberta and the United States every day. Her American television appearance followed President Donald Trump’s order directing U.S. federal use of “Lake America” for Lake Ontario. The symbolism has sharpened an already ugly dispute. Smith’s answer has been to emphasize the long alliance, shared security and integrated economy rather than trade insults with the White House. Whether that message can move the administration is another question. Public appeals can keep attention on the costs of tariffs and give U.S. businesses a reason to press their own government. They do not substitute for a deal. But Smith is making a distinct Alberta case: Canada should be firm about sovereignty, avoid treating energy as expendable, and keep a route back to tariff-free commerce open. Current page · August 31, 2026

Champagne takes Canada’s diversification case to the G20.

François-Philippe Champagne arrives at the G20 finance ministers meeting in Asheville emphasizing shared prosperity, Canadian resources and trade diversification as he prepares to meet Scott Bessent.

Champagne takes Canada’s diversification case to the G20. Current page August 31, 2026 François-Philippe Champagne arrives at the G20 finance ministers meeting in Asheville emphasizing shared prosperity, Canadian resources and trade diversification as he prepares to meet Scott Bessent. Canada–U.S. trade · G20 Asheville Champagne takes Canada’s diversification case to the G20. Canada’s finance minister arrived in Asheville emphasizing shared prosperity, critical minerals, energy and potash — and preparing for the first senior Canada–U.S. face-to-face contact since the trade talks collapsed. Published Aug. 31, 2026 · 5:56 p.m. ET François-Philippe Champagne. Official Government of Canada portrait. ASHEVILLE, N.C. — Finance Minister François-Philippe Champagne arrived at the G20 finance ministers meeting Monday with a deliberately different message from the confrontation coming out of Washington: Canada wants collaboration, a level playing field and more trading options. Champagne’s office said his bilateral meetings in Asheville would emphasize “collaboration and shared prosperity” while advancing the trade diversification Canadian businesses are seeking amid global uncertainty. The Canadian pitch is built around things other countries need: critical minerals, energy and potash, as Ottawa tries to turn Canada’s resource base into leverage in a less predictable trading system. The message comes only 10 days after Canada–U.S. trade negotiations broke down. Washington imposed 50 per cent tariffs on roughly $28 billion of Canadian goods on Aug. 22, and Canadian counter-tariffs are scheduled to begin Sept. 8. Champagne’s spokesperson, John Fragos, reiterated Ottawa’s explanation for the rupture: Canada suspended negotiations after the United States introduced terms the government considered unfair and uneconomic. That makes Champagne’s expected meeting with U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent the most closely watched Canadian encounter in Asheville. It is believed to be the first face-to-face meeting between senior Canadian and American officials since the negotiations collapsed. Neither man is the principal trade negotiator, but both sit close enough to the economic consequences to matter. Bessent set a much sharper tone earlier Monday. He accused Prime Minister Mark Carney of turning the dispute into a “political shouting match,” argued that Canada could not sustain a true tit-for-tat confrontation with an economy roughly 13 times its size and claimed Ottawa had walked away from the best trade deal offered to any country. Canada disputes that account. Champagne, by contrast, is using the G20 to make the case that Canada has alternatives. Diversification does not mean replacing the enormous U.S. market overnight. It means reducing the vulnerability that comes from relying overwhelmingly on one customer — particularly when that customer is willing to use tariffs as political and economic leverage. The broader G20 meeting is itself tense. Bessent is pressing governments to address global trade imbalances, increase growth and intensify economic pressure on Iran. Other participants are arriving with concerns about American tariffs, high energy prices, U.S. debt and the growing flood of Chinese exports into world markets. For Canada, however, the immediate question is simpler: whether Champagne’s language of shared prosperity can produce anything concrete when he sits down with Bessent. A meeting is not a resumed negotiation. But after 10 days of escalating tariffs and rhetoric, it is at least a channel. Sources and further reading Department of Finance Canada — G20 announcements and releases The Canadian Press / CityNews — Champagne to meet Bessent at G20 Reuters — G20 diplomacy, tariffs, Iran and global imbalances
Current page · August 31, 2026

CARNEY PUTS BARTON AND GREWAL ON THE HUNT FOR $1 TRILLION

Ottawa installs Dominic Barton and Gurinder Grewal at Invest in Canada as Mark Carney makes foreign investment central to Canada

CARNEY PUTS BARTON AND GREWAL ON THE HUNT FOR $1 TRILLION Current page August 31, 2026 Ottawa installs Dominic Barton and Gurinder Grewal at Invest in Canada as Mark Carney makes foreign investment central to Canada Mark Carney is putting two people with deep connections to global capital in charge of the federal agency that is supposed to persuade global capital to choose Canada. Ottawa announced Monday that Dominic Barton, the former global managing partner of McKinsey & Company and former Canadian ambassador to China, will become chair of Invest in Canada. Gurinder Grewal, an investor whose career has ranged across energy, infrastructure, industrials and technology, will become the agency’s chief executive officer. The appointments matter because Invest in Canada is being handed a much larger job at precisely the moment Canada is trying to reduce its economic vulnerability to the United States. The Carney government says it wants to catalyse $1 trillion in total investment over five years, with Invest in Canada helping move international investors from an initial look at the country to actual projects in energy, critical minerals, artificial intelligence and infrastructure. That makes this more than a personnel announcement. It is part of the machinery behind the government’s attempt to answer the central economic problem exposed by the trade war: Canada cannot become less dependent on one enormous customer simply by announcing that it intends to diversify. Somebody has to find the capital, connect it to projects, navigate governments and regulations, and get things built. The Barton choice Barton is an unusually consequential appointment for a part-time board chair. He spent more than 30 years at McKinsey, including nine as global managing partner and six as Asia chair. He later served as Canada’s ambassador to China from 2019 to 2021 and currently chairs Rio Tinto and LeapFrog Investments. He has also chaired the federal Advisory Council on Economic Growth. That résumé gives Ottawa something it clearly wants: direct access to the international corporate and institutional-investment networks Canada is now trying to court. It also guarantees scrutiny. Barton’s long McKinsey career and his time in Beijing have made him a familiar and sometimes controversial figure in Canadian public life. In this job, however, the useful question is measurable: can the new leadership convert contacts and government ambition into projects that actually diversify Canadian economic power? Grewal will have the operational job. Ottawa says he founded MEM Growth Partners after more than a decade at Advent International, where he became a partner and co-head of industrials, infrastructure and energy. His appointment is full-time for five years; Barton’s chairmanship is a three-year, part-time term. A much bigger mandate Invest in Canada was established under federal legislation in 2018 to attract and facilitate foreign direct investment. Its own 2026–27 plan says it will concentrate on energy, advanced manufacturing, defence, life sciences, value-added agriculture and critical minerals while expanding relationships with economies that share Canada’s strategic outlook. The agency explicitly describes reducing reliance on any single trading partner as part of that work. The numbers are already substantial. Ottawa says the stock of foreign direct investment in Canada reached $1.6 trillion at the end of 2025, up 6.9 per cent in a year, while 2025 inflows reached $96.8 billion — the highest annual level since 2007. The government says roughly $280 billion in public capital investment and incentives over five years is intended to help enable more than $1 trillion in combined public, private and institutional investment. There is a tension here worth watching. Economic sovereignty does not mean replacing dependence on American capital with indiscriminate dependence on somebody else’s capital. Foreign investment can expand Canadian productive capacity, open export routes and create leverage; it can also transfer ownership and strategic control. The important question is therefore not simply how many billions Barton and Grewal can attract, but what Canada gets in return and which capabilities remain Canadian. Carney’s language makes clear that diversification is the objective. The Prime Minister said Monday that Canada is trying to build an economy that is “not reliant on any single trading partner.” That is the correct test for this appointment as well. If Invest in Canada becomes a more powerful front door for energy, minerals, AI and infrastructure investment, its success should eventually be visible in things more concrete than announcements: mines financed, power built, factories opened, ports expanded, export customers added and Canadian companies given more than one market in which they can survive. Washington has spent months demonstrating what happens when one trading relationship becomes powerful enough to be used as leverage. Ottawa’s answer now includes Barton and Grewal, a federal investment agency with a larger mandate, and a very large number — $1 trillion — attached to the door. Sources Prime Minister of Canada — appointment announcement Prime Minister of Canada — Dominic Barton biography Invest in Canada — 2026–27 Departmental Plan The Globe and Mail — Aug. 31 report
Current page · August 31, 2026

Meet Andy Harris, the congressman who wants Alberta as the 51st state

Maryland Republican Andy Harris says the United States should make Alberta the 51st state. A closer look at the House Freedom Caucus chairman, his record and what his remarks mean for Canada.

Meet Andy Harris, the congressman who wants Alberta as the 51st state Current page August 31, 2026 Maryland Republican Andy Harris says the United States should make Alberta the 51st state. A closer look at the House Freedom Caucus chairman, his record and what his remarks mean for Canada. Lead · Sovereignty Watch · Alberta · U.S. Congress Meet Andy Harris, the congressman who wants Alberta as the 51st state He is not a random television provocateur. He chairs the House Freedom Caucus, sits on Appropriations and says Donald Trump is “taking advantage” of western Canadian alienation. His record makes the Alberta remark worth taking seriously — without pretending it is U.S. government policy. Published Aug. 31, 2026 · 5:05 p.m. ET · Reporting & analysis Rep. Andy Harris, Republican of Maryland. Official U.S. House portrait / Office of the Clerk. There are congressional backbenchers, and then there is Andy Harris. On Monday morning, the Maryland Republican went on Newsmax and suggested that if Alberta leaves Canada, the United States should make it the 51st state. “Tremendous oil reserves there,” he said. He then offered a diagnosis of western Canada: “The socialism doesn’t work in Western Canada. A lot of those provinces don’t want to be associated with Ottawa. And the president’s taking advantage of it.” The first obligation is not to inflate the remark. Harris did not announce an American annexation policy. He was speaking on partisan television, and neither the White House nor Congress has announced a plan to absorb Alberta. But neither is Harris simply a man who wandered in from the parking lot. He is an eight-term member of Congress, chairman of the House Freedom Caucus and a member of the powerful House Appropriations Committee. When someone in that position describes a neighbouring country’s internal political division as something the American president is “taking advantage of,” Canadians are entitled to pay attention. Harris, 69, was born in Brooklyn to parents who fled communist Eastern Europe after the Second World War. He trained as a physician at Johns Hopkins, practised anesthesiology for nearly three decades and served 17 years in the U.S. Naval Reserve, including during Operation Desert Shield and Desert Storm. He spent 12 years in the Maryland Senate before winning a seat in Congress in 2010. In 2024, his colleagues elected him chairman of the Freedom Caucus, the hard-right Republican bloc that has repeatedly exerted leverage over House leadership. A record Canadians may find familiar Harris has spent years near the sharp edge of American conservative politics. After the 2020 presidential election, he voted to object to electoral votes from Arizona and Pennsylvania even after the Capitol had been attacked on Jan. 6, 2021. During the overnight House debate, the Washington Post reported that Harris became involved in a heated confrontation on the floor that nearly turned physical. He later opposed creating the bipartisan Jan. 6 commission and was among 21 House Republicans who voted against awarding Congressional Gold Medals to police officers who defended the Capitol. Two weeks after the attack, Harris set off a newly installed House metal detector while carrying a concealed handgun near the chamber. Capitol Police investigated. Harris’s office said he had a Maryland handgun permit, carried for self-defence because of threats, complied with screening and had never carried a firearm onto the House floor. Officers did not allow him into the chamber with the weapon. That distinction matters: the reported episode is striking enough without embellishment. Then there was COVID-19. Harris is a physician, which made his intervention unusually conspicuous. In 2021 he said he had prescribed ivermectin as a COVID treatment and complained that pharmacists would not fill such prescriptions. At the time, the U.S. Food and Drug Administration had not approved ivermectin to treat or prevent COVID-19, and major American medical and pharmacy organizations opposed its use for that purpose outside clinical trials. Harris had earlier administered COVID vaccines himself, but later opposed vaccine and mask mandates and questioned their necessity. His social-policy record is equally conservative. Harris has been a prominent opponent of abortion and was an original co-sponsor of the 2021 Life at Conception Act, legislation that sought legal protection beginning at fertilization. He later backed a federal 15-week abortion proposal containing exceptions. He also became a particular antagonist of Washington, D.C.’s marijuana legalization movement after inserting federal budget language preventing the District from spending its own funds to establish a regulated recreational cannabis market approved by voters. There has also been a smaller ethics controversy. In 2018, Maryland Democrats filed a complaint alleging Harris failed to disclose income earned by the consulting firm of the woman who became his wife. Campaign finance records showed his campaign and other Republican committees had paid her firm. Harris’s campaign said the omission was an error and that he amended the disclosure after learning of it. That is the documented fact; an allegation of deliberate concealment would go beyond the evidence available to Canada at War. The Orbán connection For Canadians trying to place Harris politically, his enthusiasm for Hungarian Prime Minister Viktor Orbán may be more revealing than any isolated scandal. Harris has repeatedly cultivated ties with Hungary’s conservative political movement and publicly praised Orbán’s example. The relationship places him inside an international current of national-conservative politics that treats immigration, liberal institutions and progressive social policy as connected fronts in a broader political struggle. That makes Monday’s language about Canada especially interesting. Harris did not merely say Albertans have grievances. He described western Canada through an American ideological template — “socialism” in Ottawa, alienated western provinces, enormous oil reserves — and then proposed changing an international border. The oil reference is difficult to miss. His hypothetical 51st state was introduced not with a meditation on Albertans’ democratic aspirations but with the observation that there are “tremendous oil reserves there.” Canada should resist the temptation to turn Harris into more than he is. One congressman talking on Newsmax cannot annex a province. Alberta’s constitutional future belongs to Albertans and Canadians, and any separation process would involve Canadian constitutional law, democratic legitimacy and negotiations far more complicated than a television sound bite. But dismissing him as a crank would be equally lazy. Harris helps lead an influential faction of the governing Republican Party. Donald Trump has repeatedly spoken of Canada as a possible 51st state. The administration is applying extraordinary economic pressure to Canada. U.S. officials are publicly mocking Canadian military and economic leverage. Alberta is simultaneously confronting a real separatist debate. Against that background, a senior congressional ally of Trump saying the United States should take Alberta — and that the president is exploiting western alienation — is news. The question is not whether Andy Harris has discovered a workable plan for continental rearrangement. He has not presented one. The question is why annexation of Canadian territory has become casual political conversation among people with actual power in Washington. That is a change Canadians would be foolish not to notice. Current page · August 31, 2026

Bessent escalates — then sits down with Canada.

Scott Bessent sharply escalates his rhetoric toward Canada at the G20 in Asheville as he prepares to meet François-Philippe Champagne. Canada at War tracks the reaction and what comes next.

Bessent escalates — then sits down with Canada. Current page August 31, 2026 Scott Bessent sharply escalates his rhetoric toward Canada at the G20 in Asheville as he prepares to meet François-Philippe Champagne. Canada at War tracks the reaction and what comes next. Lead · Canada–U.S. trade · G20 Asheville · Developing Bessent escalates — then sits down with Canada. Hours before an expected face-to-face meeting with François-Philippe Champagne, U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent mocked Canada’s ability to retaliate, blamed Mark Carney for a “political shouting match” and claimed Ottawa walked away from “the best trade deal of any country on the globe.” The G20 now becomes the first test of whether the rhetoric is theatre — or the beginning of a harder American line. Updated Aug. 31, 2026 · 3:53 p.m. ET Scott Bessent. Official U.S. Department of the Treasury portrait. Source: U.S. Treasury. François-Philippe Champagne. Government of Canada portrait. Source: Canada.ca. ASHEVILLE, N.C. — Scott Bessent did not arrive at the G20 sounding like an official preparing to cool the Canada–U.S. trade dispute. Speaking to CNBC on Monday, the U.S. Treasury secretary dismissed the idea that Canada and the United States were engaged in a conventional tit-for-tat fight because, he said, the American economy is roughly 13 times larger. He accused Prime Minister Mark Carney of transforming the dispute into a political shouting match and suggested Carney was acting in the interests of himself and the Liberal Party rather than Canadians. Then came the taunt. Bessent mocked the idea of Canada confronting the United States by asking whether it would deploy “their two submarines from the Edmonton Mall.” The remark was delivered as a joke, but in the middle of a tariff confrontation involving Canadian sovereignty, economic retaliation and increasingly personal attacks from Washington, it landed as something more than comic relief. Bessent also repeated Washington’s central account of the collapsed negotiations: that Canada had been offered what he called the best trade deal of any country in the world and chose to walk away at the last moment. The striking thing about Monday is the contradiction: Bessent is publicly belittling Canada while simultaneously confirming that he will meet the Canadian finance minister. Ottawa’s version of the collapse is different. Carney has said the United States introduced last-minute terms that Canada regarded as unfair and uneconomic and that raised questions about whether any agreement would be reliable. Canada suspended the negotiations on Aug. 22 and moved toward reciprocal tariffs scheduled to begin Sept. 8. So the argument entering the G20 is no longer simply about tariff percentages. It is about who broke the negotiation, what kind of agreement Washington was demanding and whether the two governments still agree on the basic rules under which a negotiation can take place. Champagne: “ready to discuss” There has been no comparable rhetorical response from Champagne so far. Instead, the Canadian finance minister arrived in Asheville telling reporters that he came in a good frame of mind and was “ready to discuss.” Axios also reported that Champagne told international journalists Canada’s economy was doing fine despite the escalating trade confrontation. That makes the expected Bessent–Champagne bilateral unusually important. It is the first publicly announced cabinet-level meeting between the two governments since the Aug. 22 breakdown. It is not, by itself, a resumption of trade negotiations. But it does reopen a senior political channel that had effectively gone quiet in public. What matters later today is the language the two men use when they emerge. A bland statement that they discussed the bilateral economic relationship would mean very little. A reference to further talks, instructions to negotiating teams, the Sept. 8 Canadian counter-tariffs or another scheduled meeting would be much more significant. There is also the possibility that one side simply repeats its existing position. If Bessent again says Canada must accept the deal Washington believes was already offered, or if Champagne says Canada will not return without changes to the U.S. terms, Asheville may confirm that contact has resumed without showing any narrowing of the dispute. Europe has other fights with Washington As of late Monday afternoon, Canada at War has found no substantive public European reaction specifically to Bessent’s remarks about Canada. But the atmosphere around the G20 is hardly one of uncomplicated allied solidarity. European finance ministers are openly unhappy about Russia’s return to the G20 finance table. Reuters reported that German Finance Minister Lars Klingbeil warned against any normalization of relations with Moscow while Russia’s war against Ukraine continues. European officials also kept Russian Finance Minister Anton Siluanov out of the traditional G20 family photograph. At the same time, the European Union has backed one of Bessent’s principal objectives in Asheville: additional economic pressure on Iran. The EU said Monday that it supports further measures, including the U.S.-led “Operation Economic Outcast,” while continuing to call for diplomacy and de-escalation. Bessent has been trying to build international support for sanctions strong enough to force Tehran back toward negotiations. That mixture — agreement with Washington on Iran, disagreement over Russia and anxiety over U.S. tariff policy — captures the broader mood of this G20. French Finance Minister Roland Lescure compared the world economy to Asheville’s weather: unpredictable and foggy, with everyone hoping it clears. What else is happening at the G20 The formal American agenda is built around economic growth, deregulation, global imbalances and sovereign debt. Bessent has argued that countries should “grow their way out” of rising debt burdens and has promoted the U.S. approach of lighter regulation and stronger private-sector investment. He has also been pressing the case for easing regulations on smaller banks. The gathering is occurring against a much less orderly geopolitical backdrop. Iran and energy prices are dominating security-related economic discussions. European anger over Russia’s participation has spilled into public view. The U.S. Treasury Department is also facing criticism after restricting accreditation for some journalists from major American outlets, including Bloomberg and The New York Times. That is the setting in which Canada’s dispute is being discussed: not an isolated bilateral quarrel, but one of several fractures running through a meeting that is supposed to coordinate the world’s largest economies. What we are waiting for now The next useful information should come from Bessent, Champagne or their staffs after the bilateral. Neither side has announced a precise public timetable for a readout, so comments could emerge in a hallway scrum, a formal statement or later interviews rather than at a scheduled joint news conference. There are four questions worth watching. Did they actually discuss restarting formal negotiations? Did Washington offer any modification of the terms Canada rejected? Did Canada signal any change to the Sept. 8 retaliatory tariffs? And did they agree to another meeting or establish a working channel? If the answer to all four is no, Bessent’s morning remarks may turn out to be the day’s real story: Washington using the G20 not to lower the temperature with Canada but to reinforce its argument that Ottawa is too small to win a prolonged confrontation. If there is movement, however, the same day could end very differently from the way it began. The finance ministers’ meeting offers both governments a way to resume contact without either side admitting it has backed down. For now, the door is open — but Bessent has made clear that Washington intends to walk through it from a position of overwhelming economic power. Bessent and Champagne reopen the door. Whether anything comes through it is now the question. Canada at War

Must Reads from Other Sources Current page August 31, 2026 Canada at War The Walrus · Aug. 31, 2026 Canada’s Trade War Is a Fight for the Free World Michael Ondaatje The Walrus frames Canada’s tariff confrontation with the United States as more than a dispute over duties and market access: it is a test of whether a smaller democracy can resist economic coercion from a much larger ally. The piece argues that Mark Carney’s decision to stand up to Donald Trump offers other countries a model for defending sovereignty when Washington uses trade leverage to force political concessions. Its larger claim is that Canada’s fight matters beyond Canada because the future of the free world depends on countries being able to say no to power without surrendering the rules that are supposed to bind allies. Current page · August 31, 2026

The boycott is becoming a habit.

Buy Canadian behaviour, U.S. travel cancellations and provincial alcohol bans are persisting as the trade confrontation deepens.

The boycott is becoming a habit. Current page August 31, 2026 Buy Canadian behaviour, U.S. travel cancellations and provincial alcohol bans are persisting as the trade confrontation deepens. Canada–U.S. trade · Consumer boycott · Buy Canadian The boycott is becoming a habit. Canadians are still checking labels, avoiding U.S. travel and keeping American alcohol off provincial shelves. What began as a burst of anger is showing signs of becoming ordinary consumer behaviour. Published Aug. 31, 2026 · 2:00 p.m. ET A consumer boycott matters differently from a tariff. A tariff is written into law, applied at the border and measured by customs data. A boycott is decentralized. It exists in millions of small decisions: which pasta sauce goes into a shopping cart, whether a family books Florida or Halifax, whether a provincial liquor board puts American bourbon back on a shelf. That makes it harder to measure — and potentially harder to reverse. Reporting Monday from Buffalo Toronto Public Media says the renewed Buy Canadian push is continuing more than a week after the collapse of the trade talks. Shoppers are checking country-of-origin labels more carefully, some are returning U.S. goods bought by mistake, provincial leaders are maintaining bans on American alcohol, and Canadians are again cancelling or avoiding travel to the United States. Business Insider separately reported on Canadians trying to build an almost “America-free” pattern of consumption. The experiment exposes both the reach of U.S. products into everyday Canadian life and the limits of substitution: technology, winter produce and some branded goods are difficult or expensive to replace. The important shift is psychological. A boycott becomes more durable when the decision stops feeling like a protest and starts feeling like routine. Canada has seen waves of Buy Canadian sentiment before. What is unusual now is the breadth of the trigger. The dispute is not confined to one commodity or one trade ruling. It has become connected to tariffs, sovereignty, language and culture, industrial policy, Trump’s attacks on Canadian leaders and the attempt to rename Lake Ontario. That gives consumers more than one reason to keep participating. The shelves are political Grocery-store behaviour is the clearest everyday expression of the boycott. Canadian shoppers are looking more closely at labels and origin claims, and retailers have responded by making Canadian provenance more visible. But origin is complicated. A familiar Canadian brand may be owned by an American company. A product assembled in Canada may rely on U.S. ingredients. A Canadian-owned company may manufacture abroad. Business Insider highlighted the problem of “maple washing,” where branding suggests a stronger Canadian connection than the product actually has. That means the boycott is forcing consumers to decide what “Canadian” means: ownership, manufacturing location, ingredients, employment or simply where the economic benefit lands. There is no single correct answer. The important point is that consumers are asking the question at all. Alcohol is still symbolic leverage American alcohol remains one of the most visible targets because provincial governments control major parts of the retail system. Removing U.S. wine and spirits from government-controlled shelves turned a consumer preference into an institutional boycott. During the trade negotiations, Washington wanted those restrictions lifted. Ottawa indicated it would encourage provinces to restore U.S. alcohol in exchange for a satisfactory deal. The talks collapsed instead. Now premiers are signalling that American products will not automatically return. That matters economically to producers on both sides of the border, but it matters even more politically: putting the bottles back would be read as a visible normalization of the relationship. Travel may be the deeper wound Travel decisions are harder to reverse than a grocery purchase. A consumer can buy a Canadian cereal this week and an American one next week. A family that replaces an annual U.S. vacation with a Canadian or European trip may discover a new habit, new loyalty points and new expectations. Border communities, airlines, hotels and tourist destinations therefore have reason to worry about persistence rather than just the initial decline. Some Canadians quoted in current reporting say they do not know when they will return to the United States. That is not the same as a permanent national shift, and anecdotal reports should not be mistaken for comprehensive travel data. But it shows how political resentment can become a long-term economic preference. The boycott has limits An America-free life is not realistic for most Canadians. The two economies are too integrated. Canadian hospitals, farms, factories, software systems, stores and households depend on U.S. goods and services that cannot be replaced quickly or cheaply. There is also an affordability problem. Buying Canadian can cost more, especially when domestic production is smaller or seasonal. A boycott that becomes a test of household sacrifice will be harder for lower-income consumers to sustain. That does not make the movement irrelevant. Boycotts do not have to eliminate every American purchase to change incentives. A modest but persistent shift in discretionary spending can affect brands, tourism and political lobbying. American companies that lose Canadian sales have a reason to tell Washington that the trade confrontation is costing them. Canadian companies that gain customers have a reason to invest in capacity. Retailers have a reason to source differently. From reaction to structure The strategic question is whether governments and businesses treat the boycott as temporary anger or as evidence of a more durable reorientation. If Canadian demand for domestic goods lasts, companies may respond with new manufacturing, packaging, distribution and marketing. If U.S. travel remains weak, Canadian destinations may capture spending that previously left the country. If provincial alcohol bans persist, importers and restaurants may build new supplier relationships. Those adjustments make the original behaviour harder to reverse because they create alternatives. That is why the boycott deserves to be tracked alongside tariffs and formal negotiations. Trade policy changes prices by law. Consumer nationalism changes markets by preference. Canada’s confrontation with the United States is now doing both. Sources Buffalo Toronto Public Media — continued Buy Canadian sentiment, U.S. travel avoidance and provincial alcohol bans, Aug. 31, 2026 Business Insider — Canadians trying to live an “America-free” lifestyle, Aug. 31, 2026 Prime Minister of Canada — trade-negotiation terms involving provincial U.S. alcohol restrictions, Aug. 22, 2026
