The 5 per cent problem is a small-business crisis.

The new U.S. tariffs hit only a slice of Canadian exports, but that slice contains thousands of smaller companies that cannot absorb a 50 per cent border tax.

The 5 per cent problem is a small-business crisis. Current page August 31, 2026 The new U.S. tariffs hit only a slice of Canadian exports, but that slice contains thousands of smaller companies that cannot absorb a 50 per cent border tax. Trade war · Small business · Export shock The 5 per cent problem is a small-business crisis. Washington’s newest 50 per cent tariffs reach only a minority of Canada’s exports to the United States. But the companies inside that minority are often too small to reroute production, bargain down margins or wait out a trade war. Published Aug. 31, 2026 · 2:00 p.m. ET The headline number can make the new U.S. tariffs sound contained: roughly five per cent of Canadian exports to the United States are directly caught by the latest measures. For the businesses inside that five per cent, there is nothing small about it. The Wall Street Journal reported Monday that smaller Canadian exporters of products including honey, artwork, wool, cosmetics and flowers are among the companies most exposed to the new 50 per cent U.S. duties. Nearly 80 per cent of affected exporters surveyed expect revenue losses, according to the Journal, and more than a third expect losses of at least 50 per cent. That is the difference between a macroeconomic statistic and a company’s survival. A multinational can move production, finance inventory and absorb a bad quarter. A family exporter selling one specialized product into one nearby market may have none of those options. The trade war has been dominated by autos, steel, aluminum and national industrial strategy. Those sectors deserve the attention: they employ large workforces and anchor entire regions. But the new tariff list reaches far beyond factories with thousands of workers. It catches companies whose entire U.S. strategy may consist of a warehouse, a distributor and a customer base built over years. Their advantage was geography and predictable access to the world’s largest consumer market. A 50 per cent border tax can erase that advantage overnight. There is no easy place to put the tariff Every tariff forces the same practical question: who pays? A Canadian exporter can try to raise its U.S. price and pass the duty to the buyer. It can cut its own price and absorb part of the tariff in its margin. The U.S. importer can absorb some of the cost. Or the transaction can simply stop. For a differentiated product with strong demand, the burden may be shared. For a small company selling into a competitive market, a 50 per cent increase can be fatal to the sale. Dan Kelly, president of the Canadian Federation of Independent Business, told Buffalo Toronto Public Media that he is hearing from owners whose American customers will not accept a 50 per cent premium and whose Canadian companies cannot swallow the cost themselves. That leaves them directly in the middle of the confrontation. This is why the tariff’s relatively narrow share of total exports can be misleading. Exposure is not evenly distributed across the Canadian economy. Small firms have less room to manoeuvre A large manufacturer can sometimes redesign a supply chain. It can shift a product between plants, negotiate long-term freight contracts, hedge currencies, finance a temporary inventory build or lobby for an exclusion. Small firms are less likely to have those tools. Diversifying to Europe or Asia may require new distributors, certification, packaging, language support and years of relationship-building. Selling more inside Canada can help, but a domestic market of roughly 40 million people cannot automatically replace access to a U.S. market more than eight times larger. Even finding a new buyer does not solve the logistics problem. A southern Ontario company can put goods on a truck and reach millions of U.S. consumers in hours. Shipping the same product across an ocean changes freight cost, delivery time, working-capital needs and risk. That is why “diversify” can be a good national strategy and still be a brutal short-term instruction to an individual business. The employment effect comes later The first response to a tariff shock is often not a plant closure. Companies cancel hiring, postpone equipment purchases, cut shifts, reduce advertising and hold more cash. Those decisions are harder to see than a factory gate closing, but together they weaken the economy. The Journal cited forecasts that unemployment could rise to seven per cent and that tens of thousands of jobs could be lost if the tariffs persist. Those estimates remain forecasts, not outcomes. What is already visible is the uncertainty that causes firms to delay decisions. That uncertainty can do damage even if a deal eventually removes the tariffs. A company that was planning an expansion this autumn may wait. An American customer may qualify a supplier in another country rather than risk another tariff shock. A Canadian owner nearing retirement may decide not to invest in another five years of growth. The policy problem Ottawa has announced billions of dollars in support for affected workers and businesses. That is necessary, but support programs face a difficult design problem: a tariff can destroy revenue faster than government assistance can be approved and delivered. Loans help a viable company bridge a temporary disruption. They are much less useful if the tariff permanently removes the market that made the company viable in the first place. The best support therefore has to do more than replace lost cash. It has to help companies find customers, certify products for new markets, finance inventory and survive the transition long enough for diversification to become real. There is also a political lesson. Canada’s response cannot be judged only by whether GDP remains positive or whether the largest exporters survive. A trade war can be nationally manageable and locally devastating at the same time. That is what the five per cent figure hides. The biggest Canadian companies will dominate the negotiations because they dominate trade. But for thousands of smaller exporters, the question is simpler and more immediate: can the company still make a sale after a 50 per cent tariff is added at the border? For many of them, the answer is no. Sources The Wall Street Journal — “Canadian Small Businesses to Bear Brunt of New U.S. Tariffs,” Aug. 31, 2026 Buffalo Toronto Public Media — Canadian small-business exposure and CFIB comments, Aug. 31, 2026 Department of Finance Canada — tariff response and business support measures, Aug. 25, 2026
Current page · August 31, 2026

Canada may not have been negotiating with one Washington.

New reporting from The Guardian and Washington Post describes a divided Trump trade team, with Jamieson Greer, Howard Lutnick and Peter Navarro pulling in different directions during the Canada talks.

Canada may not have been negotiating with one Washington. Current page August 31, 2026 New reporting from The Guardian and Washington Post describes a divided Trump trade team, with Jamieson Greer, Howard Lutnick and Peter Navarro pulling in different directions during the Canada talks. Analysis · Washington · Trade-team divisions Canada may not have been negotiating with one Washington. New reporting describes a Trump trade team divided over what to offer Canada and who had authority to offer it. If that account is right, the last-minute changes that helped sink the deal were not simply bargaining tactics — they were the product of a U.S. government arguing with itself. Published Aug. 31, 2026 · 2:00 p.m. ET The most important new question about the collapse of the Canada–U.S. trade talks may not be what Canada rejected. It may be whether the United States ever had a single, stable offer to make. The Guardian reported Monday on the competing centres of power inside Donald Trump’s trade team: U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer, Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick and White House trade adviser Peter Navarro. The Washington Post had already reported a specific internal fight over Greer’s willingness to reduce a 50 per cent tariff on aluminum derivatives to 25 per cent in return for Canadian concessions. According to the Post, Lutnick and Navarro objected strongly to Greer’s approach in a White House meeting because they believed the higher tariff was necessary to force more manufacturing into the United States. The Guardian’s account places that dispute inside a broader picture of a negotiating structure in which several powerful officials had different instincts and portfolios but Trump remained the ultimate authority. Canada’s negotiators may have been bargaining not only across the table, but against a moving argument inside the U.S. government. That possibility would help explain one of the central disputes since the talks collapsed on Aug. 22. Prime Minister Mark Carney says the United States introduced new terms late in the process that reduced the value and reliability of the agreement. Greer has publicly denied that Washington made last-minute demands and has said Canada was responsible for walking away. Those two claims have been treated as mutually exclusive. But internal divisions in Washington create a third possibility: Greer may have believed he had authority to negotiate one set of terms, only to have other officials with control over key tariffs insist on harder conditions before the deal could be finalized. The problem of divided authority Greer’s office is the natural centre of a trade negotiation. He helped negotiate the original United States–Mexico–Canada Agreement during Trump’s first administration and has the formal trade-policy role. But several of the tariffs Canada most urgently wanted reduced — including measures affecting steel, aluminum and vehicles — involve authorities administered or heavily influenced by the Commerce Department. That gives Lutnick enormous practical power. A trade representative can negotiate a concession, but if the department controlling the tariff does not support it, the concession may not survive. Navarro adds another layer. He has long advocated aggressive tariffs and reshoring, and has publicly portrayed Canadian retaliation as evidence that Ottawa is behaving more like an adversary than an ally. His influence is not the same as Greer’s formal negotiating authority or Lutnick’s departmental control, but his views closely match Trump’s preference for tariffs as leverage. The result is a structure in which Canada could hear one thing from a negotiator, another from a cabinet secretary and still face intervention from the president. The aluminum fight The Washington Post’s reporting offers the clearest concrete example. Greer was reportedly prepared to cut the tariff on aluminum derivatives from 50 to 25 per cent as part of a broader exchange. Lutnick and Navarro opposed that reduction, arguing that the higher tariff was important to U.S. manufacturing policy. This was not a minor technical detail. Canadian officials have said the commercial value of the proposed deal depended on whether the tariff relief actually covered the products and sectors Canada needed protected. A headline tariff reduction could look significant while exclusions, product definitions or content rules stripped much of the benefit away. Late in the negotiations, the United States also sought to exclude some heavy trucks produced in Ontario from tariff reductions, according to the Post. Canadian reporting has similarly described a dispute over medium- and heavy-duty trucks and over the fine print governing automotive relief. That is precisely the kind of change that can transform a deal from politically difficult to economically unacceptable. The French-language dispute The same institutional confusion appears around culture and French-language protections. Carney has said U.S. demands reached into areas Canada considers sovereign and non-negotiable, including French-language and cultural policy. Greer has dismissed that account as false or exaggerated. But the dispute is broader than whether one negotiator used a particular phrase. The underlying American concerns include Canadian rules affecting streaming services, online content and market access. Those issues can be framed in Washington as barriers to American companies even when Ottawa regards them as cultural policy. When several U.S. officials are shaping the package, a Canadian negotiator needs to know not only what is being requested but whether any assurance survives the next internal review. Reliability is itself a negotiating term This is why the internal U.S. divisions matter beyond palace intrigue. Carney said the late changes called into question the reliability of the deal. Reliability is not an abstract diplomatic virtue. For a company deciding where to build a factory, or a government deciding whether to dismantle retaliatory tariffs, the durability of an agreement has economic value. If Canada gives up leverage in exchange for tariff relief, it needs confidence that the relief will still exist after another White House meeting. The U.S. system under Trump makes that confidence difficult. Trump deliberately centralizes decisions and frequently uses uncertainty as leverage. Officials may compete to interpret his preferences. Industries lobby different departments. Tariff authorities are scattered across statutes and agencies. A concession offered on Monday can become politically vulnerable by Friday. That does not mean Canada’s negotiators were passive or faultless. Ottawa was seeking major concessions of its own, and the U.S. side says Canada expanded its demands late in the process. The point is narrower: the American account of a stable offer rejected by Canada is harder to sustain if the terms were still being fought over inside Washington. What Canada should learn If talks resume, Canada will need to negotiate differently. Every major concession should be tested against the official who actually controls the relevant tariff. Every verbal understanding should be translated into precise text early. And any final package should be judged not only by its tariff rates but by how difficult it would be for Washington to reopen or reinterpret it. The lesson is not that Greer, Lutnick or Navarro is the “real” negotiator. The lesson may be that all of them are — until Trump decides otherwise. That is an unusually difficult counterpart for any country. For Canada, whose factories and supply chains are built around predictable access to the U.S. market, it is a strategic problem. The collapse of the talks therefore looks less like a single disagreement over one final offer and more like a collision between two kinds of sovereignty. Canada refused terms it believed reached too deeply into domestic policy. Inside Washington, competing officials were simultaneously fighting over how much economic pressure the United States should surrender. That is the story behind the story: Ottawa was negotiating with the United States, but the United States was still negotiating with itself. Sources The Guardian — “As US-Canada tensions flare, who’s calling the shots in Trump’s trade team?”, Aug. 31, 2026 The Washington Post — internal U.S. divisions over Canadian tariff concessions, Aug. 23, 2026 The Canadian Press/CityNews — Greer disputes Canada’s account of last-minute U.S. demands, Aug. 26, 2026 Prime Minister of Canada — Carney’s explanation for suspending negotiations, Aug. 22, 2026
Current page · August 31, 2026

FIRST THE MICROPHONE. NOW THE ROOM.

Anita Anand will become the first Canadian foreign minister to attend an EU Gymnich meeting, another concrete step in the Canada-Europe strategic turn ahead of Carney’s Strasbourg visit and the October summit.

FIRST THE MICROPHONE. NOW THE ROOM. Current page August 31, 2026 Anita Anand will become the first Canadian foreign minister to attend an EU Gymnich meeting, another concrete step in the Canada-Europe strategic turn ahead of Carney’s Strasbourg visit and the October summit. Aug. 26 Carney’s coming Sept. 16–17 European Parliament visit becomes public: first the political stage. Sept. 1–2 Anand joins the EU foreign ministers’ informal Gymnich meeting in Ireland: now the policy room. Sept. 16–17 Carney attends Ursula von der Leyen’s State of the Union and addresses the European Parliament. Oct. 29–30 Canada and the EU hold their leaders’ summit in Canada. Canada at War has already written about the extraordinary symbolism of Mark Carney being invited to Europe’s parliamentary stage. Monday’s announcement is not the same story. Foreign Affairs Minister Anita Anand will travel to Ireland for the Sept. 1–2 informal meeting of European Union foreign affairs ministers known as the Gymnich. Global Affairs Canada says it will be the first time a Canadian foreign minister has participated in one. The distinction matters. Carney’s European Parliament appearance is public political diplomacy: a Canadian prime minister speaking directly to Europe’s elected legislators at a moment when Canada is looking for strategic alternatives to overwhelming U.S. dependence. Anand’s invitation is closer to the machinery. Why this is new: the Carney story was about Canada being given a highly visible European platform. The Anand story is about Canada being brought into an informal meeting where EU foreign ministers exchange views on live geopolitical and security questions. What a Gymnich is—and what it is not Gymnich meetings are informal gatherings of EU foreign ministers. They are not formal Council meetings that adopt legislation or binding decisions. Their value is precisely that ministers can have broader strategic conversations away from the normal formal agenda. The Sept. 1–2 meeting in Wicklow will be co-hosted by Irish Foreign Minister Helen McEntee and Kaja Kallas, the EU’s high representative for foreign affairs and security policy. Global Affairs says discussions will include the Middle East, Ukraine and shared geopolitical priorities. Canada is not joining the European Union. Anand does not acquire a vote in EU institutions. But participation gives Canada access to a conversation it has not previously entered at this level. Ottawa is explicitly connecting this to the new partnership The Government of Canada is not presenting Anand’s attendance as a ceremonial courtesy. Global Affairs says the visit will help implement the New Canada-EU Strategic Partnership of the Future and deepen co-operation on economic security, trade diversification, defence and security. It also says the meeting will help prepare for the Canada-EU Leaders’ Summit scheduled for Oct. 29–30 in Canada. That makes the sequence more important than any single meeting. Canada and the EU launched the new strategic partnership at their June 2025 summit and signed a security and defence partnership covering areas that include defence-industry collaboration, military mobility, cybersecurity, hybrid threats and emerging technologies. Canada has since become the first non-European country to participate in the EU’s SAFE defence procurement framework. Anand and Kallas already co-chair the Canada-EU Joint Ministerial Committee, the principal formal forum for strategic foreign-policy dialogue between the two sides. Where Carney fits without repeating the old story On Aug. 26, Canada at War published Why Mark Carney’s European Parliament address matters . The argument was that Europe was giving Carney an unusually significant political microphone just before the October summit. That analysis still stands. What Monday adds is evidence that the relationship is advancing on more than one track. Carney will attend European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen’s State of the Union address on Sept. 16 and speak to the European Parliament the next day. That gives Canada a public audience with legislators from across the EU’s 27 member states and political families. Anand’s Gymnich appearance comes two weeks earlier and operates differently. It gives the foreign minister a seat in an informal strategic discussion among the people running European foreign policy. One is persuasion in public. The other is access to policy conversation. The October leaders’ summit is where both tracks can potentially become agreements. The trade war is accelerating a relationship that predates it It would be a mistake to describe Canada’s European relationship as something invented after the latest collapse in Canada-U.S. trade talks. The security partnership, CETA, defence work and strategic-partnership framework were already in place. What has changed is the urgency. Carney has argued that Canada needs to reduce its dependence on the United States and expand non-U.S. exports. The sharp confrontation with Washington gives every existing European mechanism a new strategic value. Europe cannot replace the United States as Canada’s dominant economic partner in the near term. Geography and decades of integrated supply chains make that unrealistic. But diversification does not require replacement. It requires enough alternative markets, capital, defence relationships and political partners that one country has less capacity to dictate Canada’s choices. Seen that way, the Anand invitation matters because it is another small piece of institutional density. What to watch in October The test remains concrete outcomes. Watch for new economic-security commitments, measures to diversify trade, defence-industrial agreements, procurement access, critical-mineral arrangements, digital co-operation and any roadmap that makes the strategic partnership more durable than a political moment. Canada being welcomed into an informal EU foreign ministers’ meeting does not by itself change the balance of power in North America. But paired with Carney’s Strasbourg invitation and the October summit, it shows something more consequential than diplomatic warmth: Canada and Europe are steadily adding rooms, forums and institutions in which they can act together. Current page · August 31, 2026

Toyota and Honda built Canada into their North American system. Trump’s 50% tariff could force them to unwind it.

Toyota and Honda make more than three-quarters of Canada’s vehicles. Trump’s proposed 50% tariff on Canadian car imports could force a major restructuring of their Canadian production.

Toyota and Honda built Canada into their North American system. Trump’s 50% tariff could force them to unwind it. Current page August 31, 2026 Toyota and Honda make more than three-quarters of Canada’s vehicles. Trump’s proposed 50% tariff on Canadian car imports could force a major restructuring of their Canadian production. >75% Toyota and Honda’s combined share of vehicles made in Canada, according to Reuters. 17% Share of Toyota’s U.S. sales last year that were Canadian-built, Barclays analysts told Reuters. ~25% Share of Honda’s U.S. sales last year that were Canadian-built, according to the same analysis. Donald Trump’s proposed 50% tariff on Canadian-built cars is usually described as a weapon against Canada’s auto industry. That is true, but incomplete. It is also a direct shock to two of Japan’s largest industrial companies. Reuters reported Monday that Toyota and Honda account for more than three-quarters of all cars manufactured in Canada. Analysts warned that if the 50% tariff takes effect Jan. 1 as proposed, both companies could face pressure to close some Canadian assembly lines. Neither company told Reuters it planned to do that. Both declined to comment on the report. The point is therefore not that plant closures have been decided. It is that the economics of decades of integrated North American production could be deliberately made much worse. The strategic contradiction: Washington says it is taxing Canadian production. Much of the production it is making more expensive belongs to foreign companies—especially Japanese companies—that built plants in Canada precisely because North America was supposed to function as an integrated market. Canada is not a side operation for either company Toyota Motor Manufacturing Canada has three Ontario assembly plants, two in Cambridge and one in Woodstock. Toyota says its Canadian operation can produce more than half a million vehicles a year. Woodstock builds the RAV4 and RAV4 Hybrid; Cambridge plants make Lexus vehicles and other Toyota production. Honda’s Canadian manufacturing base is in Alliston, Ontario. Honda’s own manufacturing information lists two auto plants producing the Civic family and CR-V, including hybrid versions. The operation dates to 1986 and has been part of Honda’s North American manufacturing network for four decades. These plants are not simply making vehicles for Canadians. They are deeply tied to the American market. Reuters, citing Barclays analysts, said Canadian-built vehicles accounted for 17% of Toyota’s U.S. sales last year and almost one-quarter of Honda’s. Toyota exports Canadian-built RAV4s to the United States, while Honda exports CR-Vs. A tariff can redraw a supply chain without moving a border A 50% import tariff does not physically prevent a vehicle from crossing into the United States. It changes the price calculation so severely that a company has to ask whether the existing production map still makes sense. Toyota and Honda would have several theoretical options: absorb some of the tariff, raise prices, redirect Canadian production to other markets, shift U.S.-bound production to American factories, or reduce Canadian output. None is simple. Vehicles are configured for specific markets. Factories already have capacity constraints. Suppliers have built their own networks around existing plants. Moving a model is not the same as moving a spreadsheet entry. That is why the Jan. 1 date matters even though a negotiated settlement remains possible. Industrial planning takes years, while tariff decisions can alter the economics in months. The pressure reaches Tokyo There is another layer to the story. Toyota and Honda are already trying to navigate a global car market transformed by Chinese electric-vehicle competition, high investment costs and shifting demand. Reuters reported that Toyota has committed to invest up to US$10 billion over five years to expand U.S. operations. Honda, meanwhile, has warned that uncertainty over the North American trade framework could affect whether it builds another assembly plant on the continent. That gives Washington leverage—but it also creates friction with companies headquartered in a major U.S. ally. A Canadian tariff story can therefore become a Japanese industrial-policy story. If a Japanese company is forced to rethink a profitable Canadian plant because Washington has made cross-border sales uneconomic, the cost of the Canada-U.S. confrontation does not stop at the Canadian border. What the tariff is really testing For decades, the central premise of the North American auto industry was that a vehicle could be designed, sourced and assembled across borders within a common production system. Canadian, American, Mexican, Japanese, Korean and European companies all invested around that premise. Trump’s proposed tariff tests whether that premise still exists. If it goes into effect at 50%, Toyota and Honda would face an unusually stark decision: preserve Canadian production and accept a huge penalty on access to their largest market, or reorganize production around a political border that their investment decisions were designed to make less important. For Canada, that is the danger. The auto war is not merely about losing exports tomorrow. It is about whether the next generation of capital spending concludes that Canada is still safely inside the North American manufacturing system. Sources: Reuters, Aug. 31, 2026 ; Toyota Motor Manufacturing Canada ; Honda Canada Manufacturing . ← Back to Canada at War
Current page · August 31, 2026

TRUMP CALLED APPLE

U.S. Interior Secretary Doug Burgum says Donald Trump personally contacted Apple about changing Lake Ontario to Lake America on its maps. Apple has not announced a change.

TRUMP CALLED APPLE Current page August 31, 2026 U.S. Interior Secretary Doug Burgum says Donald Trump personally contacted Apple about changing Lake Ontario to Lake America on its maps. Apple has not announced a change. WHITE HOUSE → APPLE private platform → THE MAP millions see Until Monday morning, the most important fact in the “Lake America” dispute was that Donald Trump had changed a United States federal name, while private technology companies were left to decide what to do with it. Now the White House is no longer merely changing the government database that mapping companies consult. According to Trump’s own interior secretary, the president has personally gone to Apple. Reuters reported Monday that Interior Secretary Doug Burgum said on Fox Business that Trump had reached out to Apple directly about changing “Lake Ontario” to “Lake America.” Burgum said he expected that a change could be coming soon. Apple did not immediately comment. The line that matters: Burgum says Trump contacted Apple. Apple has not announced that it will change its maps, and Canada at War is not reporting that Apple has agreed to do so. This is different from what happened at Google Google’s decision created the first version of this controversy. After the U.S. Geographic Names Information System was changed, Google applied its existing mapping policy and began showing “Lake America” to users in the United States. Canadian users continue to see Lake Ontario, while Google says users elsewhere see both names. That was a corporate choice built on a corporate rule. Trump’s Aug. 27 executive order instructed the U.S. Department of the Interior and Board on Geographic Names to update GNIS and use the new name throughout the federal government. It did not order private map companies to change their products. MapQuest demonstrated the distinction by refusing to adopt the new name. Google chose to follow the federal database for U.S. users. Apple had not made a comparable public change when the issue erupted. Monday’s disclosure therefore adds something qualitatively different: direct political pressure on a private mapping company. The legal power and the practical power are not the same thing The president has broad authority to direct how the U.S. executive branch refers to geographic features for federal purposes. The executive order tells federal agencies to use “Lake America” and tells the geographic-names machinery to remove Lake Ontario references from GNIS. That does not rename the lake in Canada. It does not alter Ontario’s naming rules, Canada’s geographic databases or international usage. And it does not turn Apple into a federal agency. But the practical power of a mapping platform can be enormous. The name that appears by default in a phone, vehicle, search result or embedded map can become the name a user encounters hundreds of times. A private company does not have sovereign naming authority, but it can have distribution power on a scale governments once monopolized through official maps and textbooks. That was the central argument of Canada at War’s Sunday analysis, Who owns the map? Google’s choice showed how a federal database can propagate through privately controlled digital infrastructure. Trump’s reported call to Apple adds a second channel: the government can also try to persuade the infrastructure owner directly. The sovereignty issue just became much easier to see There is nothing unusual about governments lobbying companies. Presidents routinely speak to corporate executives about investment, regulation, national security and public policy. The important question here is the object of the lobbying. The name concerns a lake shared by two sovereign countries. Canada and the United States have had a bilateral process since 1989 for dealing with names of shared geographic features. Trump used an executive route that made the new label official for the American federal government without requiring Canadian agreement. Now, according to Burgum, the president has asked one of the world’s most powerful technology companies to carry that federal label into a private mapping ecosystem. That does not make the request illegal. It does make the private-company choice more politically consequential. If Apple changes the label, the next question will not simply be whether it followed an automated data rule. It will be how the company responds when a president personally asks it to reflect his government’s naming decision for territory shared with another country. What to watch next The next factual threshold is simple: Apple itself. Does Apple announce a change? Does it explain a general mapping policy, as Google did? Does it display different labels by country, use dual naming, or retain Lake Ontario? Does it address the presidential contact at all? Until Apple answers, Burgum’s statement is evidence of pressure, not evidence of compliance. And that distinction is exactly why the story matters. The fight is no longer only about what a government calls a lake. It is about who gets to shape the privately owned systems through which people see the world. Sources: Reuters, Aug. 31, 2026 ; White House executive order, Aug. 27, 2026 ; Canada at War’s Google/MapQuest map-power analysis and 1989 shared-names report . ← Back to Canada at War
Current page · August 31, 2026

Washington wants the world to put up more walls against China. Canada has a choice to make.

Scott Bessent is urging G20 governments to re-examine their trade terms with China, creating a new strategic question for Canada as Ottawa seeks room to diversify away from the United States.

Washington wants the world to put up more walls against China. Canada has a choice to make. Current page August 31, 2026 Scott Bessent is urging G20 governments to re-examine their trade terms with China, creating a new strategic question for Canada as Ottawa seeks room to diversify away from the United States. World Watch · China · G20 · Canadian sovereignty Washington wants the world to put up more walls against China. Canada has a choice to make. U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent is taking a blunt message to the G20: other countries should reconsider the terms on which they trade with China. For Canada, the demand lands at an awkward moment — just as Ottawa is trying to build alternatives to overwhelming dependence on the United States. Published Aug. 31, 2026 · 2:40 a.m. ET ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ ★ Editorial illustration: the 13-star American flag, representing the original colonies. U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent is pressing G20 governments to reconsider their trade terms with China. U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent has opened another front in Washington’s attempt to reshape the world trading system — and this one could put Canada directly between the United States and China. In an interview with Reuters ahead of the G20 finance ministers and central bank governors meeting in Asheville, North Carolina, Bessent said he would encourage member countries to re-examine their terms of trade with China. His argument is that China’s export machine has become too large for the rest of the world to absorb while Chinese domestic demand remains comparatively weak. “The world cannot have a China with a $1.2 trillion trade surplus,” Bessent told Reuters. He said the United States has already insulated itself from many Chinese exports through tariffs and outright restrictions, including on automobiles, with the result that more Chinese goods are flowing into Europe, Latin America and other markets. The important Canadian question is not whether Washington has a case against Chinese industrial policy. It is whether Washington expects to decide Canada’s China policy too. Bessent’s proposed answer is international pressure. Other countries, he said, will have to give Beijing an incentive to move away from export-led growth and toward stronger domestic consumption. “The rest of the world is going to have to examine their terms of trade with China,” he said. That is more than an argument about Chinese economics. It is an attempt by Washington to organize other major economies around a U.S. diagnosis of the problem and, potentially, around additional barriers to Chinese trade. Canada’s dilemma For Canada, the timing is extraordinary. The country is already fighting a severe trade confrontation with the United States while trying to reduce the strategic vulnerability created by sending such a large share of its exports to one market. Europe, the United Kingdom and the Indo-Pacific have therefore become more important to Ottawa. So has the question of what a workable economic relationship with China might look like. That does not mean Canada has suddenly become pro-Beijing. Ottawa has its own disputes with China, including over electric vehicles, industrial subsidies, agricultural trade, national security and foreign interference. Canada can independently conclude that particular Chinese trade practices require a response. But Bessent’s remarks introduce a different issue: whether Canada’s diversification strategy will itself face pressure from Washington. If Canada expands commercial ties with China while the United States is trying to assemble a broader front against Chinese exports, Ottawa could find itself confronting American demands on two levels at once. Washington is already using tariffs to influence where companies manufacture and invest. A coordinated G20 campaign against China would raise the possibility that U.S. trade policy will increasingly seek to influence with whom its allies trade as well. That distinction matters. Canada deciding that a subsidized Chinese product threatens a Canadian industry is Canadian trade policy. Canada restricting trade because Washington requires alignment is something else: a sovereignty question. The U.S. case Bessent’s argument is not occurring in a vacuum. European governments are also worried about rising Chinese exports, particularly in industries such as automobiles and advanced manufacturing. China’s large industrial capacity, subsidies and weak domestic demand have generated concern well beyond Washington. The U.S. Treasury has made global imbalances a formal priority of its 2026 G20 presidency. In February, Treasury said the finance track would focus on what it called “excessive global imbalances.” In July, Treasury again singled out China in its report on major trading partners, criticizing the lack of transparency surrounding Chinese exchange-rate policy while keeping China on its monitoring list. There is therefore a legitimate multilateral economic debate here. The danger for Canada is allowing that debate to collapse into a binary geopolitical instruction: trade with the United States on Washington’s terms, or risk consequences for trading with China. A world of blocs Bessent’s intervention also illustrates the larger transformation taking place in international trade. The old assumption that governments would steadily remove barriers and allow production to follow comparative advantage is giving way to industrial policy, national-security screening, strategic tariffs, subsidies and economic blocs. Canada is particularly exposed to that change because its economy was built around exceptionally deep integration with the United States. Diversification is much easier to announce than to accomplish. Geography, infrastructure, existing supply chains and decades of continental integration cannot be rewritten quickly. Yet the current confrontation with Washington has made diversification less an abstract policy preference than a form of insurance. That is why Bessent’s comments matter here. Canada does not have to choose China over the United States. It does have to preserve the ability to decide for itself what economic relationship it wants with China, Europe and every other major market. The G20 meeting in Asheville will show whether Bessent’s appeal becomes a shared statement, a loose consensus, or simply another American demand that other governments are unwilling to accept. Canada should be watching the wording closely. Because beneath the argument over China’s trade surplus is a larger question that Canada is already being forced to answer: in a world reorganizing itself into economic blocs, how much freedom will middle powers retain to choose their own partners? Sources Reuters — Bessent urges G20 countries to reconsider trade terms with China, Aug. 30, 2026 U.S. Treasury — 2026 G20 Finance Track agenda and Asheville meeting U.S. Treasury — July 2026 report on macroeconomic and foreign-exchange policies of major trading partners U.S. Treasury — Scott Bessent official biography and portrait
Current page · August 31, 2026

WHO OWNS THE MAP?

Trump

WHO OWNS THE MAP? Current page August 31, 2026 Trump Donald Trump can tell the United States government what to call Lake Ontario. He cannot tell Google what to call it. That distinction cuts through most of the slogans, anger and political theatre surrounding the sudden appearance of “Lake America” on Google Maps. Trump’s Aug. 27 executive order directs the U.S. secretary of the interior and the U.S. Board on Geographic Names to make “Lake America” the official name used by the federal government, change the federal Geographic Names Information System and use the new terminology on federal maps, contracts, documents and communications. The order does not order Google, Apple, MapQuest, Microsoft, newspapers, schools, New York State, Canada or the rest of the world to follow. Google followed anyway. MapQuest did not. READ GOOGLE’S STATEMENT → Google says its U.S. label follows its longstanding policy of reflecting names from official government sources such as GNIS. READ MAPQUEST’S STATEMENT → MapQuest’s response to the federal change: “We’re not changing it.” MapQuest proves there was a choice The cleanest way to understand Google’s decision is to start with MapQuest. Both companies were confronted with the same event. The White House issued the same executive order. The U.S. geographic-names database changed. The federal government adopted the same new terminology. MapQuest’s response was four words: “We’re not changing it.” The company then doubled down, saying it would continue calling the lake Lake Ontario and launching a tongue-in-cheek tool that allowed users to make their own labels. The humour generated headlines, but the underlying point was serious: a change in GNIS does not legally command a private mapping service. Google made a different decision. On Aug. 29, Google published a statement saying that because GNIS had formally changed the U.S. name, people using Google Maps in the United States would see “Lake America.” Canadians would continue to see “Lake Ontario.” Users outside both countries would see both names. Google described this as the application of a longstanding policy for bodies of water whose names differ by country. That is a coherent policy. It is also Google’s policy . The key fact: Trump did not compel Google. Google chose GNIS as an authoritative input for the names shown to American users, and chose to render the federal U.S. designation as the sole primary label they see. MapQuest, faced with the same federal change, chose not to. The naming academics are already involved Reuben Rose-Redwood , a University of Victoria geography professor whose research includes the politics of place naming, told Global News that place naming is not a neutral practice and has long been bound up with assertions of authority. Read the interview → Derek H. Alderman , a University of Tennessee geographer and specialist in the politics of memory and place, has argued with Rose-Redwood that renaming is a form of “world-making”: maps do not merely describe political power; they can express it. Read their analysis → Clancy Wilmott , a UC Berkeley geographer, has emphasized the jurisdictional limit: a U.S. president can change how his government refers to a place, but cannot require another country to adopt the change. Read more → Scott Stevens Manning , an Akwesasne Mohawk scholar and associate professor at Syracuse University, has drawn attention to the Indigenous linguistic history embedded in “Ontario.” Read ICT’s report → Editorial image supplied to Canada at War. What the official American system actually controls The U.S. Board on Geographic Names exists to standardize federal geographic nomenclature. Congress reorganized the board in 1947 and directed it, together with the secretary of the interior, to establish uniform usage throughout the federal government . Its decisions become standards for federal publications. That is significant authority. The federal government produces maps, navigational products, scientific databases, emergency information, contracts and vast quantities of public data. Once the name was changed in GNIS, “Lake America” became the operative name across those federal systems. But federal standardization is not the same as universal naming authority. The government’s own geographic-names materials distinguish federal use from the decisions of states, foreign governments and other naming authorities. New York Gov. Kathy Hochul has said New York will continue calling it Lake Ontario. Canada has not renamed it. Ontario has not renamed it. Apple Maps was still displaying Lake Ontario on Sunday. MapQuest said no. Nor is there a world agency that can take the White House order, stamp it “approved,” and compel every country to change its maps. The United Nations Group of Experts on Geographical Names promotes standardization and co-operation, but national naming authorities remain central. Where countries use different names for a shared or disputed feature, multiple names can coexist. Canada and the U.S. already had rules for shared geography This dispute is more unusual because the two countries have a specific bilateral framework for geographic features along their border. In 1989, Canadian and American naming authorities approved the Treatment of Names of Geographical Features Shared by the United States and Canada . It says coordination is mutually beneficial, established names are highly desirable to retain, local usage matters, and name changes for transboundary features should be considered by the appropriate naming authorities in both countries. The Congressional Research Service later summarized ordinary U.S. policy this way: for features crossing the Canadian border, the U.S. Board on Geographic Names is to coordinate proposals with the appropriate Canadian naming authority under the 1989 arrangement. There is an important caveat. The American board’s manual also says names specifically established by an executive order are legally official for federal purposes and are not bound by its ordinary principles and procedures. Trump used that presidential lane. So the serious institutional question is not answered by shouting that the order is either “fake” or globally binding. It is narrower: the president made a federal name official through an exceptional route, while bypassing the normal co-operative naming process designed for shared Canada–U.S. geography. Read Canada at War’s companion report on the 1989 agreement → Google’s defence is reasonable — and still a choice Google has a real operational problem to solve. A global map must display places whose names differ by language, country, history and politics. Some boundaries are disputed. Some seas have competing names. Some cities have exonyms. There is no frictionless universal label. Using official national sources is therefore defensible. It creates a repeatable rule rather than requiring Google executives to adjudicate every geopolitical controversy from scratch. But a consistent rule does not eliminate corporate agency. Google decided which government databases it treats as authoritative. Google decided how to resolve conflicts between them. Google decided that users’ locations should affect which name is primary. And Google decided that an American user looking at an international lake should see only “Lake America” as the principal label rather than, for example, “Lake Ontario / Lake America” or “Lake Ontario — U.S. federal name: Lake America.” We know a dual-name solution is technically possible because Google itself says users outside Canada and the United States will see both names. That makes the U.S.-only rendering a cartographic choice, even if it is the automatic product of a pre-existing corporate rule. From official authority to infrastructure power The distinction between legal authority and practical power is the heart of this story. Kind of power Who holds it What it does Official naming authority Federal, provincial, state and national naming institutions Determines names used by governments within their jurisdictions. International recognition Other governments, international practice and cartographic convention Determines whether a national designation spreads beyond the country that adopted it. Distribution or “map” power Google, Apple, Microsoft, MapQuest, OpenStreetMap and other platforms Determines what names ordinary users actually encounter on phones, search results, cars and embedded maps. Google is not the legal names authority for Lake Ontario. It cannot change the Canada–U.S. boundary. It cannot amend Canadian law. It cannot make Ontario’s naming board recognize a different name. But Google says more than two billion people use Google Maps each month. A label presented repeatedly to an audience of that size can acquire a social reality that once required years of government mapmaking, textbook publishing, journalism and common usage. That is outsized influence even if it is not sovereign authority. Apple has similar structural significance because Apple Maps is built into an enormous global device ecosystem, although Apple does not publish a directly comparable Maps-user figure. Microsoft and other data providers feed geographic labels into software, search and business systems. OpenStreetMap, meanwhile, can store multiple local, official and alternative names, leaving downstream map renderers to decide what to display. The result is a layer of private governance sitting on top of public naming law. Canada saw the consequence immediately The power is not confined to the Google Maps app. Organizations embed Google’s mapping products in their own websites and services. This weekend, the American terminology appeared on some Canadian institutional pages that use Google map data even though the governments responsible for those institutions had not changed the Canadian name. Ontario began reviewing affected sites. That is an extraordinary illustration of infrastructure power: a U.S. president changes a federal database; a private California technology company applies its own rendering policy; and the resulting terminology can surface inside Canadian websites whose own naming authorities reject it. Google Maps screenshot supplied to Canada at War on Aug. 30. Google says U.S. users see “Lake America,” Canadian users see “Lake Ontario,” and users elsewhere see both. Maps have never been neutral None of this means that Google invented political cartography. Governments have used maps to express claims, suppress names, standardize languages and reinforce national narratives for centuries. That is why Rose-Redwood and Alderman’s intervention matters. Political toponymy — the study of place naming as a political and cultural act — starts from the proposition that names carry history and authority. A name can preserve Indigenous language, commemorate conquest, normalize a border or tell citizens which version of history a state considers official. The word “Ontario” itself makes that plain. Scholars and Indigenous language experts trace it to an Iroquoian language tradition predating both modern countries. Manning has cautioned against overly simple claims about exactly which language supplied the word, but the Indigenous origin is not seriously in doubt. Replacing that name for U.S. federal purposes therefore does more than alter letters on a database line. It asserts a new political story about the feature. Google then decides how prominently that story enters everyday life. What Google has — and does not have It would be an exaggeration to say Google now “names” the world. It does not. Governments, local communities, Indigenous nations, language communities, international organizations and ordinary usage all remain part of the process. It would also be an exaggeration to say Google simply had no choice. MapQuest demonstrates otherwise. The more accurate conclusion is that the digital-map era has created a new kind of authority that the traditional geographic-naming system was not built to regulate. Google and Apple are not sovereigns, but their interfaces are where millions of people encounter the work of sovereign naming systems. When governments disagree, the platforms must decide which official source to privilege, whether to show one name or several, and which audience sees which version. Those decisions deserve scrutiny precisely because they are private choices with public consequences. The question after Lake Ontario Trump’s federal designation could disappear under a future administration. The older name could remain dominant in public usage regardless of what GNIS says. Or “Lake America” could persist in American federal terminology for years. The larger issue will remain after this particular fight is forgotten. Who decides what a place is called when governments disagree? Legally, there are naming authorities and jurisdictional rules. Internationally, there is negotiation, convention and sometimes unresolved disagreement. But for the person holding a phone, the immediate answer increasingly comes from a private company. Trump changed a U.S. federal name. Google chose to put that name in front of American users. MapQuest chose not to. That difference is not a footnote to the Lake Ontario story. It is the story. Sources and further reading Google — its Aug. 29 Maps statement MapQuest — “We’re not changing it” White House — Aug. 27 executive order U.S. Board on Geographic Names — Principles, Policies and Procedures Congressional Research Service — geographic naming policy Global News — Google Maps decision and Reuben Rose-Redwood Reuben Rose-Redwood and Derek H. Alderman — analysis of the renaming ICT — Indigenous language history and Scott Stevens Manning Google Maps logo — Wikimedia Commons, PD textlogo MapQuest logo — Wikimedia Commons, PD textlogo
Current page · August 31, 2026

Carney is being handed Europe's parliamentary microphone at exactly the moment Canada is looking for a new strategic centre of gravity.

Mark Carney will attend Ursula von der Leyen

Carney is being handed Europe's parliamentary microphone at exactly the moment Canada is looking for a new strategic centre of gravity. Current page August 31, 2026 Mark Carney will attend Ursula von der Leyen Mark Carney and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen in May 2025. European Commission photo via Wikimedia Commons, licensed CC BY 4.0. © European Union. What is confirmed: Euronews reports that Carney will attend von der Leyen's State of the Union address on Sept. 16 and address the European Parliament on Sept. 17. European Parliament President Roberta Metsola said the visit would be an opportunity to deepen EU-Canada ties. Agence Europe separately describes Carney as the Parliament's guest of honour for the State of the Union proceedings. First, what is actually unprecedented? Foreign leaders have addressed the European Parliament many times. What is unusual here is the combination of events. The EU's State of the Union address is not simply another speech in Strasbourg. Since 2010, the president of the European Commission has used it to take stock of the year and set priorities for the next legislative and political cycle. It is part of the formal relationship between the Commission and Parliament and is followed by debate with members of the European Parliament. European reporting says Carney will be the first non-European head of state or government to attend that annual address in person. That distinction matters. He is not the first foreign leader to speak from the European Parliament's podium. He is being inserted into one of the EU's most important annual institutional rituals — and then given his own parliamentary address the next day. Who has addressed the European Parliament before? The list is distinguished and the setting is normally reserved for moments when Parliament wants to make a political point. Canadian prime minister Justin Trudeau formally addressed the plenary in March 2022, weeks after Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine. His speech was followed by interventions from political group leaders. Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy addressed Parliament remotely in March 2022 and in person at an extraordinary plenary in February 2023. King Abdullah II of Jordan has addressed the Parliament repeatedly — six plenary addresses through 2025, according to Parliament's own record. Colombian President Iván Duque addressed a formal sitting in Strasbourg in 2022. European monarchs and other visiting leaders have also been received in formal sittings over the years. The institution therefore has a well-established tradition of giving foreign leaders the chamber when it wants their message heard directly by Europe's elected representatives. Carney's invitation belongs to that tradition, but with an added layer: his presence is being paired with the State of the Union itself at a time when Canada is openly trying to reduce its dependence on the United States. Why the timing changes everything The invitation was not invented after the Canada-U.S. trade talks collapsed. That is important. It means Brussels was already cultivating Carney and Canada before the latest rupture with Washington. But the collapse of the talks has changed the meaning of the trip. Carney said on Aug. 22 that Canada would begin discussions with the European Union this fall on a much stronger and deeper economic and security partnership. Days later, the public learned that he would not only attend von der Leyen's State of the Union address but speak to Parliament himself. That creates a clear political sequence: Canada announces a European turn; Carney goes to Strasbourg and speaks directly to MEPs; then the EU-Canada summit takes place in Canada on Oct. 29-30. The European Council's own calendar confirms the October summit. In June, European Council President António Costa called the coming summit a major milestone in a partnership that was becoming deeper and broader. Canada and the EU are already working together on digital trade, critical raw materials, defence and energy, and Canada has become the first non-European country to participate in the EU's SAFE defence procurement framework. The parliamentary speech gives Carney something a summit does not A summit is negotiation among executives. A parliamentary address is political persuasion in public. Carney will be speaking to legislators from 27 member states and multiple political families, not merely to von der Leyen and Costa behind closed doors. That matters if Canada wants a relationship that reaches beyond diplomatic communiqués. Any very large expansion of EU-Canada economic integration would eventually encounter Parliament through legislation, budgets, trade measures or consent procedures. The Parliament's trade committee is already politically important to Canada because CETA and future trade arrangements pass through the institution. Building parliamentary support is therefore not decorative diplomacy. The address also gives Carney an audience for his broader middle-power argument: that democracies and other countries that want a rules-based international system need to co-operate more intensively when great powers use market access, security dependence or economic coercion as leverage. Could von der Leyen address Canada's Parliament in October? It is entirely plausible, but there is no public confirmation that she will do so during the Oct. 29-30 summit. There is a strong precedent. Von der Leyen already addressed both houses of Canada's Parliament in the House of Commons chamber on March 7, 2023, during her first official visit to Canada. Then-prime minister Justin Trudeau explicitly linked the event to his own address to the European Parliament the previous year. The parliamentary record shows that she called Canada and the European Union "true friends" and framed their relationship around democracy, international law and support for Ukraine. Because she has already received the rare honour once, a second joint address in October would be politically striking rather than routine. But Parliament has not announced one, and the official EU summit page currently lists only the dates and location in Canada, saying detailed agenda information will be posted closer to the meeting. So Canada at War is treating a second von der Leyen address as a possibility to watch — not as a scheduled event. What would make October truly consequential? The Strasbourg symbolism becomes strategically important only if it produces concrete architecture. The questions for October are therefore much more specific than whether leaders appear friendly. Watch for a formal negotiating mandate or roadmap for a broader Canada-EU economic and security partnership; expansion of defence procurement and industrial co-operation; new critical-minerals and energy agreements; digital-trade commitments; supply-chain measures; and any effort to connect the EU more closely with the Comprehensive and Progressive Agreement for Trans-Pacific Partnership. Also watch the language. If von der Leyen, Costa or the summit declaration begins describing Canada as a preferred strategic partner, a democratic middle-power partner, or part of a European security and industrial ecosystem, that would indicate the relationship is moving beyond conventional free trade. Why this is bigger than a friendly invitation Diplomatic theatre can be empty. This one is worth taking seriously because the theatre is being built around existing machinery. CETA already links the two economies. Canada is inside SAFE. The two sides are working on digital trade, critical minerals, defence, energy and ocean security. The October summit is already on the calendar. The invitation therefore sits on top of a relationship that is accumulating institutions, contracts and negotiating tracks. That does not mean Canada is "joining Europe," and it certainly does not mean Europe can replace the United States economically in the near term. Geography, supply chains and the sheer size of Canada-U.S. trade make that impossible. The more realistic interpretation is diversification of strategic dependence: Canada is trying to ensure that Washington is no longer the only market, security partner or political relationship with enough weight to shape its choices. That is why Sept. 16 and 17 are worth circling. Carney will first sit in the chamber while Europe's executive leader tells Parliament what the Union intends to do next. Then he will stand before the same institution and tell Europe what Canada wants to do with it. For a country in the middle of its sharpest confrontation with Washington in generations, that is not normal protocol. It is a public audition for a different kind of partnership. Sources: European Parliament records on the State of the Union and foreign leaders' formal addresses; Euronews, Aug. 26, 2026; Agence Europe; European Council summit calendar; Prime Minister of Canada readouts; House of Commons Hansard, March 7, 2023. Current page · August 31, 2026

GM’s $1.1-billion Ontario commitment puts pickups, engines and transmissions on Canada’s side of the border.

A tentative GM–Unifor agreement outlines about $1.1 billion in Canadian auto investment, with programs in Oshawa and St. Catharines and a commitment on CAMI.

GM’s $1.1-billion Ontario commitment puts pickups, engines and transmissions on Canada’s side of the border. Current page August 31, 2026 A tentative GM–Unifor agreement outlines about $1.1 billion in Canadian auto investment, with programs in Oshawa and St. Catharines and a commitment on CAMI. Auto industry · Ontario · Canada–U.S. trade GM’s $1.1-billion Ontario commitment puts pickups, engines and transmissions on Canada’s side of the border. The new GMC Sierra line in Oshawa is only one part of a much larger tentative GM–Unifor agreement covering production, investment and job security across Ontario. Published Aug. 29, 2026 · 7:30 p.m. ET · Industry report The headline in the new General Motors–Unifor settlement is not simply a pickup truck line in Oshawa. It is a roughly $1.1-billion commitment to Canadian operations—an investment package built around heavy-duty trucks, V8 engines and a next-generation transmission at a moment when U.S. tariffs have made the future of cross-border auto production a central Canadian political issue. Unifor’s bargaining report says the tentative agreement, covering more than 4,600 workers at GM facilities in Ontario, includes new investments and reconfirmed commitments over the life of the 2026 collective agreement. Members were scheduled to vote on ratification Aug. 29 and 30. Until that process is complete, the commitments remain part of a tentative deal rather than a finished contract. The investment package Oshawa: $207 million. GM plans $144 million to add the next-generation heavy-duty GMC Sierra and recommits $63 million for stamping and related upgrades. St. Catharines: $906 million. This combines a new $215-million transmission program with a previously announced $691-million sixth-generation V8-engine investment. CAMI, Ingersoll: GM pledged not to immediately sell or close the idled assembly plant; its longer-term production future remains unresolved. Oshawa gets the GMC nameplate The $144-million Sierra commitment is meaningful because it gives Oshawa a GMC-badged heavy-duty program alongside its Chevrolet heavy-duty truck work. Unifor says the plant is expected to remain on two shifts during the agreement, subject to demand and company volume forecasts. The union also notes that the current package builds on an earlier $280-million commitment for next-generation Chevrolet Silverado heavy-duty trucks. That does not erase the pressure created by GM’s January decision to reduce Oshawa from three shifts to two. But it gives the plant a clearer production role at a time when the United States has imposed 25% tariffs on Canadian-built vehicles and threatened a further increase. Canada’s position in the stalled trade talks has been that an auto agreement needs genuine protection for Canadian production, not simply tariff relief on paper. The larger sum is in St. Catharines The bulk of the $1.1 billion is tied to St. Catharines Propulsion. GM recommits $691 million for sixth-generation V8-engine production and plans a further $215 million to build a next-generation transmission there. The transmission program is expected to begin in late 2029, with St. Catharines designated the single-source plant and approximately 250 new jobs anticipated at full volume, according to Unifor. That combination matters strategically. Heavy-duty trucks and their powertrains are not marginal Canadian assembly work; they are core North American products. Retaining both vehicle and propulsion programs preserves a more complete industrial base—suppliers, skilled trades, logistics and engineering capacity—than a simple final-assembly commitment would. CAMI remains the unresolved test The deal does not create a new vehicle program for CAMI Assembly in Ingersoll, where BrightDrop electric-van production was halted. Its immediate value is defensive: a pledge not to sell or close the site right away. Reuters reported that defence contracts could be considered if awarded, but no confirmed replacement program has been announced. The distinction matters. A protected facility is not the same thing as a secured production future. GM Canada said when bargaining opened that it had invested $3.3 billion in its Canadian manufacturing plants since 2020. The new tentative agreement makes a substantial addition to that record, but it also demonstrates why the trade fight is so consequential. Auto investment is being negotiated across a North American system whose rules are suddenly less dependable. The central Canadian result is therefore larger than the Sierra headline: GM has put significant product and powertrain capacity in Ontario, while workers have secured commitments that will be tested by ratification, demand and the tariff confrontation still unfolding across the border. What is confirmed and what is not: The investment figures and plant commitments come from Unifor’s bargaining report. The agreement was tentative at publication. Production schedules, job levels and CAMI’s future remain conditional on ratification, demand and GM decisions. Sources Unifor — General Motors bargaining report, August 2026 Unifor — tentative agreements with General Motors, Aug. 22, 2026 General Motors Canada — 2026 contract negotiations Reuters — GM–Unifor investment agreement and tariff context The Globe and Mail — “GM plans Ontario pickup production in deal that would pump $1.1-billion into Canada’s auto sector,” Aug. 29, 2026.
Current page · August 31, 2026

Trump’s 50% Canada tariffs rest on a law never tested in court.

The Section 338 authority behind Trump’s new 50% tariffs on Canadian goods has never previously been used or tested in court.

Trump’s 50% Canada tariffs rest on a law never tested in court. Current page August 31, 2026 The Section 338 authority behind Trump’s new 50% tariffs on Canadian goods has never previously been used or tested in court. United States · Tariffs · Law · Sovereignty Trump’s 50% Canada tariffs rest on a law never tested in court. Section 338 of the Tariff Act of 1930 sat virtually untouched for nearly a century. Now it is the legal foundation for a major escalation against Canada—and lawyers are questioning whether that foundation can survive its first serious court challenge. Published Aug. 29, 2026 · 10:55 a.m. ET · Reporting and analysis President Donald Trump’s latest tariff offensive against Canada has opened a second front beyond the economic confrontation itself: whether the extraordinary legal authority Washington is relying on actually reaches as far as the administration says it does. The new 50 per cent duties were imposed under Section 338 of the Tariff Act of 1930, a Depression-era provision authorizing a president to respond when another country discriminates against American commerce. The White House has invoked it against Canadian treatment of U.S. motor vehicles, dairy and other commerce. What makes the move remarkable is not simply the age of the statute. According to legal experts interviewed by The Associated Press, Section 338 has never previously been used and has never been tested in court. That leaves the central legal questions surrounding Trump’s tariffs largely unanswered. A blank canvas in American trade law Lawyers are examining whether subsequent U.S. trade statutes displaced or constrained the broad presidential discretion apparently contained in the 1930 provision. Modern American trade law normally operates through elaborate investigative and administrative procedures. Section 338 predates much of that architecture. The administration’s proclamations say Canadian measures burden or disadvantage U.S. commerce. But the tariff lists reach beyond the headline disputes over dairy, autos and alcohol, exposing a wider range of Canadian goods to punitive duties. That does not mean the tariffs are unlawful. No court has ruled that they are, and the administration can point to statutory language expressly granting the president tariff authority in response to discrimination. The significance is that the authority is now being exercised at enormous scale without a body of judicial precedent explaining its limits. Why this matters to Canada Ottawa has already chosen retaliation rather than capitulation. Canada says it will match Washington’s new tariffs dollar for dollar and rate for rate, with counter-tariffs taking effect September 8 on products in sectors including steel, dairy, appliances, agricultural equipment, pulp and paper and electronics. Prime Minister Mark Carney has framed the confrontation as a question of sovereignty as well as commerce. In announcing the suspension of negotiations, he said Canada would not accept U.S. demands that compromised its independence or key domestic policies. A successful American legal challenge could therefore change the battlefield. Canadian governments cannot simply sue in U.S. courts whenever they dislike an American trade measure; standing and jurisdiction matter. But American importers, businesses or other parties directly injured by the duties may have stronger routes to litigation. The immediate question is whether such a plaintiff will emerge. The larger question is whether a 96-year-old statute that has never been litigated can sustain one of the most consequential tariff actions in modern Canada–U.S. relations. Until a court answers that question, Section 338 is simultaneously a powerful weapon and an untested one. Sources U.S. presidential proclamation invoking Section 338 — GovInfo Government of Canada — countermeasures and support in response to U.S. tariffs Prime Minister of Canada — remarks on Canada–U.S. trade negotiations Associated Press — legal questions surrounding Section 338 19 U.S.C. § 1338 — statutory text
