Trump confrontation lifts Carney Liberals to 10-point lead: Abacus

A new Abacus Data poll puts the Liberals at 45%, Conservatives at 35%, as Donald Trump rises to second among Canadians’ top issues.

Trump confrontation lifts Carney Liberals to 10-point lead: Abacus Current page August 30, 2026 A new Abacus Data poll puts the Liberals at 45%, Conservatives at 35%, as Donald Trump rises to second among Canadians’ top issues. A new national poll suggests the confrontation with Donald Trump is strengthening Mark Carney’s Liberals politically, with the governing party opening a 10-point lead over the Conservatives as Canadians put the U.S. president back near the top of the national agenda. The Abacus Data survey, released Sunday, puts the Liberals at 45 per cent among committed voters, the Conservatives at 35 per cent, the NDP at nine per cent and the Bloc Québécois at seven per cent. The Green Party is at two per cent and the People’s Party at one per cent. The headline numbers: Liberals 45%, Conservatives 35%. Government approval 56%. Sixty-two per cent say the Liberals are the party best able to deal with Trump and his administration. The Liberal advantage has grown from seven points in Abacus’s previous survey. Liberal support is up three points since Aug. 12, while Conservative support is unchanged at 35 per cent. The timing is politically important. Abacus surveyed 2,022 Canadian adults from Aug. 21 to Aug. 26, a period that covered the collapse of Canada-U.S. trade negotiations and the implementation of new American tariffs. Abacus says most interviews were completed after those developments. The movement is not limited to voting intention. Government approval rose four points to 56 per cent, while disapproval fell to 29 per cent. Carney’s personal numbers also improved: 53 per cent of respondents had a positive impression of the prime minister and 28 per cent a negative one, for a net rating of plus 25. The most revealing result for the Canada-U.S. confrontation may be the change in what voters say they are worried about. Forty-five per cent of Canadians now identify Trump and his administration as one of the three most important issues facing Canada, up seven points in two weeks. Only the cost of living, at 62 per cent, ranks higher. The economy is third at 39 per cent. That concern strongly favours the Liberals. Among respondents who identify Trump as a top issue, 60 per cent say they would vote Liberal and 21 per cent Conservative. Asked which party is best able to deal with Trump and his administration, 62 per cent choose the Liberals, compared with 18 per cent who choose the Conservatives. Those numbers do not prove that every recent Liberal gain was caused by the trade confrontation. Polling captures association, not a laboratory experiment. Cost of living remains the dominant public concern, and federal voting intentions can shift quickly. But the pattern is difficult to ignore. As Trump has become more salient, the Liberals’ advantage has widened, government approval has risen and Carney’s personal standing has improved. That presents Pierre Poilievre and the Conservatives with a strategic problem. A government can normally be expected to take political damage when a major trading relationship deteriorates and economic risks rise. So far, Abacus is finding the opposite: voters who are most worried about the U.S. president are disproportionately consolidating behind the governing party. For Carney, the political benefit carries its own risk. Public support for his handling of Trump creates an expectation that firmness will produce a durable Canadian strategy, not simply better polling. The government still faces the harder work of protecting jobs, diversifying trade and negotiating with Washington without accepting terms it considers damaging to Canadian sovereignty. Abacus notes that its online panel survey is not a probability sample. For comparison, a random probability sample of the same size would have a margin of error of about plus or minus 2.2 percentage points, 19 times out of 20. For now, however, the federal political picture is clear: the more Donald Trump dominates Canada’s public agenda, the more favourable the terrain appears to become for Mark Carney’s Liberals. The next test will be whether that advantage survives a prolonged economic confrontation rather than the initial political rally around it. Source Abacus Data — national federal tracking survey, Aug. 21–26, 2026 Polling analysis. Percentages are reported from Abacus Data’s published results; causal interpretations are treated cautiously because the survey measures public opinion rather than experimental effects.
Current page · August 30, 2026

Capital Doesn’t Have a Personality: What Marx Would Say About Billionaires, Populism, and the Machines That Watch You Back

What Marx

Capital Doesn’t Have a Personality: What Marx Would Say About Billionaires, Populism, and the Machines That Watch You Back Current page August 30, 2026 What Marx Capital Doesn’t Have a Personality: What Marx Would Say About Billionaires, Populism, and the Machines That Watch You Back Marx did not think history was driven by villains. He thought it was driven by a system whose internal logic produces certain kinds of people, certain kinds of behavior, and certain kinds of crises, almost regardless of who’s occupying the roles. That distinction is the single most useful — and most frequently misused — idea in his entire body of work. The character is not the cause The most common misreading of Marx, made by admirers and critics alike, is to treat him as a moralist cataloguing greedy individuals. He wasn’t. His central claim, laid out across Capital and sharpened in the Communist Manifesto , is that capitalism is a system with its own internal compulsion — the endless accumulation of capital — and that the people who rise to the top of it are less the system’s authors than its most successful executors. A capitalist, Marx writes, is “capital personified,” someone who has internalized the system’s single imperative so completely that accumulation stops being a means to some other end and becomes the end itself. He is explicit that he is not primarily interested in blaming individual capitalists for greed, since greed is not a personality quirk here; it is closer to a job requirement, a survival trait selected for by competition. A capitalist who chooses long-term stability, generosity, or restraint over growth gets outcompeted by one who doesn’t. The system doesn’t reward good character. It reward accumulation, and it produces the character that accumulates best. This reframing matters enormously for any attempt to apply Marx to figures who combine extreme personal wealth with extreme political influence today. The Marxist question is not “is this particular billionaire a bad person,” which is a question about individual psychology Marx mostly declined to answer. The Marxist question is: what does this scale of private capital compel its holder to do, structurally, to protect and grow itself — and what does it produce in the wider society as a side effect of that compulsion, regardless of the holder’s private intentions? Accumulation without a natural stopping point Marx’s account of capital’s basic drive gives a specific, mechanical answer to a question that moralizing language usually leaves vague: why does great wealth keep seeking more wealth, past any plausible point of personal need? His formula — money (M) invested to produce more money (M’) in an endlessly repeating cycle he writes as M-C-M’ — describes capital as value that has, in his phrase, become a kind of “automatic subject,” moving through the economy specifically to expand itself, with no resting point built into the process at all. A regular commodity gets produced, sold, and consumed — the transaction ends. Capital, by contrast, doesn’t have an ending point where it’s “enough”; each cycle of profit becomes the seed capital for the next cycle, and the process has no internal signal that says stop. Applied to a landscape of trillion-dollar technology fortunes, this is the part of Marx’s argument that resists being waved away as archaic nineteenth-century economics: it isn’t a claim about factories or steam engines, it’s a claim about the logic of capital itself, and that logic doesn’t change based on whether the product is textiles or targeted advertising. A company or founder sitting on unprecedented wealth, by Marx’s account, is not behaving strangely by continuing to seek more influence, more market share, more political protection for that wealth. They are behaving exactly as the logic of capital predicts, and the scale of wealth involved simply means the compulsion now operates at a civilizational scale rather than a household one. Alienation, but make it a feed Marx’s early writings on alienation ( Entfremdung ) describe workers as separated, under capitalism, from several things at once: from the product of their labor (which belongs to someone else), from the process of their labor (which they don’t control), from their own human potential (reduced to whatever function extracts the most value), and from each other (competing rather than cooperating). The 1844 manuscripts describe this as a kind of self-loss — the worker doesn’t feel like himself at work, and only feels free in his purely animal functions of eating, drinking, and procreating, while feeling like an animal in the one activity, labor, that should be distinctly human. It’s worth being careful about extending this concept, since Marx built it around industrial factory labor specifically. But the underlying structure — a person’s own activity and attention being extracted, packaged, and sold back to systems whose profit logic they don’t control or fully understand — maps unusually well onto the modern attention economy. A user scrolling a feed engineered by someone else’s optimization targets, generating data that gets sold to advertisers, is not factory labor in Marx’s literal sense, but the underlying relationship shares a family resemblance: activity performed by one party, value extracted by another, with the first party often unaware of how much of the transaction is happening at all, and with a growing sense — well documented outside of Marx entirely, by public health researchers studying phone use — of alienation from one’s own time and attention as a byproduct. Ideology and “false consciousness” Marx’s collaborator Engels coined the phrase “false consciousness” to describe a specific phenomenon Marx himself analyzed without always using that exact term: the way a ruling class’s interests get presented, and often genuinely experienced, as universal common sense rather than as the interest of a particular class. In The German Ideology , Marx and Engels write that the ruling ideas of any age are, as a rule, the ideas of its ruling class — not because the ruling class is uniquely dishonest, but because it controls the means of producing and distributing ideas (in their era: the press, the schools, the pulpits) the same way it controls the means of producing goods. The mechanism here doesn’t require conscious conspiracy to function, which is one of the more sophisticated and frequently missed parts of Marx’s argument. A media environment overwhelmingly owned by a small number of extremely wealthy individuals does not need an explicit editorial memo instructing writers to protect billionaire interests in order to produce content that, in aggregate, treats extreme wealth concentration as normal, treats regulation of that wealth as radical, and treats a populist backlash against economic precarity as either a dangerous mob or a marketing opportunity, depending on the outlet. It only needs owners who hire editors who hire writers who have all, independently and often sincerely, absorbed the assumptions of the environment that employs them. Marx’s point is that ideology is most effective exactly when it doesn’t feel like propaganda to the people producing or consuming it — when it feels like simply describing reality. The demagogue as capital’s insurance policy Marx wrote directly about a phenomenon strikingly relevant to any modern populist figure who is himself extraordinarily wealthy: in The Eighteenth Brumaire of Louis Bonaparte , his analysis of Louis Napoleon’s rise to power in France, Marx describes a strongman who won broad popular support, including from struggling farmers and workers, by presenting himself as standing outside and above the corrupt establishment parties — while, once in power, ruling in ways that ultimately protected and stabilized the propertied classes’ interests far more reliably than the fractious parliamentary system he replaced ever had. Marx’s famous, biting phrase for this — that history repeats itself, “the first time as tragedy, the second time as farce” — sits inside a broader argument that a demagogic strongman, however genuinely popular his rhetoric of anti-establishment grievance, tends in practice to function as capital’s most effective protector, because a single strongman is far easier for concentrated wealth to negotiate with, lobby, or capture than a genuinely contested, pluralistic democracy full of competing factions and independent institutions. This is arguably the single most directly transferable piece of Marx’s own writing to a modern configuration in which extraordinary personal wealth and anti-establishment populist rhetoric coexist in the same political movement, rather than opposing each other. Marx would not find that combination paradoxical. His analysis of Bonapartism predicts it as close to the default outcome: a figure who channels real grievances against a distant, incompetent, or corrupt elite while quietly assembling a base of support from a different fraction of the wealthy elite — one that benefits from instability, deregulation, personal loyalty networks, and the weakening of independent courts, legislatures, and press, all of which pluralistic democracy is generally better than any single strongman at maintaining. Technology as the newest means of production — and surveillance Marx spent considerable energy on what he called the “forces of production” — tools, machinery, technology — and argued that whoever controls the dominant forces of production in an era controls, in large part, the shape of that era’s social relations. In the nineteenth century, that meant the factory owner controlling the machine the worker had to operate to survive. Marx would very plausibly extend the same logic to whoever controls the infrastructure of communication, attention, and increasingly automated decision-making in the current era — the platforms determining what billions of people see, the systems determining who gets a loan or a job interview, the infrastructure a modern economy and a modern political campaign alike now depend on. His concept of the “reserve army of labor” — a pool of unemployed or precarious workers whose existence disciplines employed workers into accepting worse terms, because they’re replaceable — has an unusually direct descendant in automation and AI-driven labor displacement, where the threat of replacement by a machine or an algorithm disciplines wages and working conditions even before, or instead of, mass displacement actually occurring. A small number of firms controlling the frontier of that technology, in Marx’s framework, isn’t merely a commercial advantage; it’s control over a lever that shapes the leverage every other worker in the economy has, or doesn’t have, in every negotiation they’ll ever have. Commodity fetishism: when the product hides the relationship One of Marx’s more difficult but directly applicable ideas is “commodity fetishism” — his term for the way a market economy obscures the human relationships and labor embedded in the things it sells, so that a commodity appears to have value in itself, as a kind of independent object with its own intrinsic worth, rather than as the congealed result of specific people’s specific labor, extracted under specific conditions. A shirt on a shelf doesn’t announce the wages, hours, or safety conditions of the person who sewed it; the price tag stands in for that entire relationship and replaces it with a simple, depersonalized number. The digital version of this concept is almost uncomfortably direct. A “free” app or platform presents itself as a product with intrinsic value — convenience, connection, entertainment — while obscuring the actual transaction underneath: a user’s attention, behavioral data, and psychological responses are the real commodity being extracted and sold, to advertisers and data brokers, in a relationship the user rarely sees clearly and never negotiates the terms of. The interface is friendly precisely because a hostile-looking interface would make the underlying extraction legible, and legibility is bad for the business model. Marx’s insight isn’t that this is a new form of dishonesty; it’s that markets are structurally good at hiding the relationships of production and extraction behind a clean, appealing surface, and a sufficiently sophisticated technology platform can do this with a person’s own attention and psychology more thoroughly than any nineteenth-century factory could do with a bolt of cloth. Where this argument has to stop It would be dishonest to present Marx’s framework as a clean, uncomplicated fit for the present moment, and it’s worth being explicit about where it strains. Marx predicted capitalism’s internal contradictions would produce its own revolutionary overthrow by an organized industrial working class; that specific prediction, in the form he made it, has not held up across a century and a half of subsequent history, and serious economists and historians across the political spectrum — not just capitalism’s defenders — regard significant parts of his economic forecasting as wrong. His framework is also frequently invoked by political actors who use “the billionaires” and “the establishment” as targets while building their own concentrated, unaccountable personal power in the process — precisely the Bonapartist pattern Marx himself warned about, which means a movement borrowing Marx’s rhetorical targets is not automatically exempt from Marx’s own sharpest warning. It’s also worth being honest that Marx’s own preferred alternative — centralized state control over the means of production — produced, in every twentieth-century attempt at large-scale implementation, its own extraordinary concentrations of unaccountable power, frequently worse in scope and violence than the capitalist concentrations they replaced. Reading Marx usefully today means taking his diagnostic tools — alienation, ideology, the logic of accumulation, the Bonapartist function of a “populist” strongman — seriously as analysis, without assuming his prescribed cure has a clean track record, or that “anti-billiona
Current page · August 30, 2026

The shadow on the wall

A plain-language essay on Plato

The shadow on the wall Current page August 30, 2026 A plain-language essay on Plato In Book VII of Plato’s Republic , Socrates asks us to imagine prisoners who have spent their lives in a cave. They are restrained so that they can look only at a wall. Behind them, objects pass before a fire. The prisoners see the shadows and hear echoes. Because those shadows are all they have ever known, they take them for reality. The allegory is not a modern theory of propaganda. Plato is asking a larger philosophical question about education, knowledge and the difference between appearance and truth. But the image captures something essential about organized lying. A lie becomes much more powerful when it does not appear as one false sentence. It becomes an environment. Control the field of vision If people can compare a claim with many independent sources, contradictions become visible. If their field of information narrows, a manufactured account can begin to feel complete. The cave matters because the prisoners are not foolish. They are reasoning from the evidence available to them. Their error begins in the limits of what they are allowed to see. Modern information systems can create less literal versions of the same problem. A partisan feed, a tightly controlled state broadcaster or a social group that punishes dissent can make one interpretation seem like the only interpretation. The most effective false world is not the one nobody questions. It is the one that supplies the answers before the questions arise. Shadows can be accurate and still mislead A shadow corresponds to something real, but it is incomplete. That is useful for understanding political manipulation built from selected facts. A video clip can be genuine and still omit what happened before it. A statistic can be correct and still lack the denominator that gives it meaning. A crime can be real and still be used to create a false picture of an entire population. A quotation can be exact and still be framed so that its meaning changes. Propaganda often works by curating reality rather than inventing it from nothing. Leaving the cave is uncomfortable Plato makes the prisoner’s release painful. Light hurts. The world outside is difficult to understand. Familiar shadows are easier. That detail matters. Correcting a false belief is not merely a data problem. Beliefs can be tied to identity, friendships, status and a sense of order. New evidence can feel like an attack on the person who accepts the old story. This is one reason fact-checks sometimes fail. They offer information without addressing the social and emotional cost of changing one’s mind. The person who returns may be mocked In Plato’s story, the person who has seen the outside world returns to the cave and has trouble seeing in the darkness. The other prisoners can take that temporary disorientation as proof that leaving the cave was harmful. People who challenge a closed information system can be treated in the same way: not as participants offering evidence, but as traitors, dupes or members of an enemy camp. Once that happens, disagreement itself becomes evidence of disloyalty. Plato’s difficult political lesson Plato did not solve the political problem by simply celebrating free debate. The Republic contains its own controversial arguments about education, censorship and rule by philosophers. That is precisely why the cave should not be reduced to a slogan. The useful lesson is narrower. Human beings do not encounter reality without mediation. We depend on language, institutions, witnesses, images and other people. Those mediators can illuminate the world or arrange the shadows. The defence is not to assume that every account is false. It is to widen the field of vision: compare sources, seek primary evidence, distinguish what is shown from what is inferred and ask what relevant information has been left outside the frame. A shadow is not necessarily a lie. The lie begins when someone insists that the shadow is all there is. Sources and further reading Plato, Republic , Book VII. Current page · August 30, 2026

The manufacture of desire

A plain-language essay using the Daodejing to examine desire, display and political manipulation.

The manufacture of desire Current page August 30, 2026 A plain-language essay using the Daodejing to examine desire, display and political manipulation. Some political messages do not begin by giving people a false fact. They begin by teaching people what to want. The text known as the Daodejing , traditionally associated with Laozi, is not a handbook about propaganda. It is a layered ancient work whose authorship and dating are subjects of scholarship. But its suspicion of display, competition and manufactured craving offers a useful way to think about manipulation. Create the prize, create the race Several passages warn that public celebration of rare goods, status and cleverness can intensify rivalry. The more a society advertises a prize as the measure of worth, the more people organize themselves around winning it. That mechanism is familiar. A politician can manufacture urgency by defining a symbolic prize: prestige, dominance, recognition, purity, revenge. Once the prize feels necessary, policies that promise it can escape ordinary scrutiny. The manipulation happens before the argument. First create the desire. Then present yourself as the only person capable of satisfying it. A population can be governed not only through fear, but through desires that someone else has taught it to treat as necessities. Display can substitute for substance The Daodejing repeatedly values what is plain, quiet and unforced over ostentation. Applied carefully to modern politics, that contrast points to a simple danger: visible strength can become more important than useful results. A grand announcement may matter more than implementation. A dramatic confrontation may be valued more than a negotiated solution. A leader may be judged by whether an opponent appears humiliated rather than whether citizens are safer or more prosperous. This is fertile ground for deception because appearances are easier to stage than outcomes. Desire narrows attention When people intensely want a result, they become vulnerable to evidence that promises it. The claim “this policy will restore what was taken from you” can gain force from the longing itself. The more emotionally valuable the promised outcome, the harder it can be to ask ordinary questions: How would this work? What would it cost? Who benefits? What evidence would show failure? A disciplined reader therefore watches not only the claim but the desire the claim activates. Non-forcing is not passivity The Daoist idea often translated as wuwei is sometimes rendered too simply as doing nothing. A better starting point is action that is not forced, strained or needlessly controlling. For political thought, the relevant contrast is between government that constantly performs its power and government whose institutions work without theatrical intervention. A leader who needs every issue to prove personal dominance may create conflict in order to display the solution. A system that functions quietly provides fewer opportunities for spectacle. The defence When a message creates a sudden sense that you must possess, defeat, restore or avenge something, ask who defined the desire and who benefits from its urgency. Would the issue matter in the same way without the repeated images, slogans and status comparisons? Is the promised prize a measurable public good, or a feeling of victory? The Daodejing does not give a modern citizen a fact-checking procedure. It offers a more basic suspicion: beware the machinery that first inflames desire and then sells obedience as the path to satisfaction. Not every desire is manufactured. Not every ambition is harmful. The point is to notice when political power depends on keeping people permanently unsatisfied. Sources and further reading Daodejing , especially chapters commonly translated as discussing desire, rare goods, simplicity and non-forcing. Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy — Laozi Chinese Text Project — Dao De Jing ← Return to the Guide to Manipulation and Deceit library
Current page · August 30, 2026

The flattery of the soul

A plain-language essay on Socrates, rhetoric, flattery and the difference between persuasion and truth.

The flattery of the soul Current page August 30, 2026 A plain-language essay on Socrates, rhetoric, flattery and the difference between persuasion and truth. There is a kind of persuasion that tries to discover what is true. There is another kind that tries to discover what an audience wants to hear. In Plato’s Gorgias , the character Socrates treats that distinction as a moral problem. Rhetoric, when detached from knowledge and justice, can become a form of flattery: it gives people the pleasure of feeling persuaded without doing the harder work of understanding. That is why Socrates belongs in a library about lying even though his central concern is not simply the false statement. His concern is the relationship between speech and the health of the person listening. Flattery imitates a real craft Plato has Socrates compare genuine arts with imitations that produce pleasant effects. Medicine aims at health. Cookery can aim merely at taste. Physical training aims at the body’s condition. Cosmetics can create the appearance of that condition. The point is not that food or appearance is evil. The point is that an imitation can borrow the surface of a genuine good while avoiding its discipline. Socrates makes the same charge against manipulative rhetoric. Political speech can imitate the appearance of civic wisdom while concentrating on approval. The speaker learns the audience’s fears, resentments and desires, then feeds them back in a satisfying form. The audience experiences recognition: this person understands us . But recognition is not evidence that the speaker’s explanation is true. Flattery asks, “What will make them agree with me?” Inquiry asks, “What would make this claim true?” The danger is greater when the speaker is skilled A clumsy lie can be checked. A gifted rhetorician can make checking feel unnecessary. The techniques are familiar across centuries: confidence, repetition, vivid examples, ridicule of opponents, emotional contrast and the suggestion that doubters are weak or corrupt. None of those techniques determines whether the underlying claim is true. Socrates’ method moves in the opposite direction. He asks for definitions. He separates one claim from another. He looks for contradictions. He forces the speaker to say what would count as evidence and what principle is being applied. This can feel irritating because it removes the emotional momentum of a speech. That irritation is useful. A claim that survives only while the audience is excited may be a claim that cannot survive examination. Persuasion without knowledge One of the Gorgias ’ central questions is whether a persuasive speaker can convince people about subjects the speaker does not actually understand. The answer is plainly yes. An audience often cannot independently master every technical subject placed before it. The skilled speaker can therefore sound more convincing than the expert. That creates a permanent political vulnerability. Citizens must make judgments about economics, war, public health, law and technology without becoming specialists in all of them. A politician or propagandist does not need to know more than the expert. The propagandist needs to know more about the audience. Socratic questioning offers a defence: ask what the speaker knows, how the speaker knows it, what evidence could change the conclusion and whether the same standard is applied to allies and opponents. Why flattery works Flattery rarely feels like manipulation from the inside. It feels like agreement. A speaker tells us that our anger is justified, our group is innocent, our opponents are contemptible and our preferred solution is obvious. The message may contain truth, falsehood or a mixture of both. Its psychological power comes from relieving us of the need to examine ourselves. Socrates repeatedly makes that examination the price of serious thinking. It is easier to expose a rival’s contradiction than one’s own. It is easier to demand evidence from an enemy than from a friend. The deepest defence against political flattery is therefore not cynicism. It is intellectual symmetry: the willingness to question a pleasing claim as aggressively as an offensive one. The test When a political message feels almost perfectly tailored to what you already want to believe, ask four questions. What exactly is being claimed? What evidence would establish it? What evidence would disprove it? Would I accept the same reasoning from the other side? If the message collapses under those questions, the problem is not that it failed to persuade. The problem is that persuasion was doing work that evidence should have done. That is the Socratic warning. A society can be talked into error not only by people who invent facts, but by people who learn to flatter the soul. Sources and further reading Plato, Gorgias , especially the discussion of rhetoric and flattery. Current page · August 30, 2026

The crisis of names

A plain-language essay on Confucius, the rectification of names and what happens when political language no longer matches reality.

The crisis of names Current page August 30, 2026 A plain-language essay on Confucius, the rectification of names and what happens when political language no longer matches reality. What happens when a government can call failure success, cruelty order or private advantage public service? One answer appears in a famous passage of the Analects . Asked what he would do first if entrusted with government, Confucius answers that he would correct—or rectify—the names. The wording and interpretation vary across translations and centuries of commentary. The underlying problem is clear: when names do not fit realities, speech becomes unreliable, action becomes confused and public standards begin to break down. Names carry duties For Confucius, a title is not merely a label. To be a ruler, parent, child, minister or friend is to stand inside a relationship with obligations. If someone possesses the title while rejecting the obligations, the name becomes hollow. A ruler who treats office as personal property may still be called a ruler, but the word no longer describes the moral function it is supposed to carry. This is why political language matters. Words tell people what kinds of actions are normal, legitimate and expected. When the word survives but its meaning is emptied out, language can conceal disorder instead of describing it. Euphemism is political power Modern institutions constantly rename things. Sometimes this is harmless or useful. Sometimes the new label hides responsibility. A civilian death becomes collateral damage. A firing becomes restructuring. A lie becomes a misstatement. A bribe becomes access. An attack on an institution becomes reform. The test is not whether the new phrase sounds softer. The test is whether it helps us describe what actually happened. If language systematically removes the actor, the victim or the consequence, the public can lose the vocabulary needed to judge conduct. Confusion can be useful Not every misuse of language is accidental. Political actors may benefit when the same word means different things depending on who acts. Violence by one group is terrorism; similar violence by an ally is defence. Spending by opponents is waste; spending by supporters is investment. Information we dislike is propaganda; information that flatters us is common sense. Once definitions become tribal, arguments become difficult to test. People appear to debate facts while actually switching meanings. Rectification is not censorship The Confucian idea is sometimes caricatured as a demand that authorities impose official language. That would miss the value of the concept for a modern reader. The useful principle is accountability between word and thing. If a government calls a policy successful, what measurable condition does “successful” describe? If a politician calls an opponent corrupt, what conduct does “corrupt” identify? If a movement claims to defend freedom, whose freedom and from what constraint? Clear definitions do not settle every dispute. They make disputes inspectable. The discipline of naming A practical defence against political manipulation is to translate abstractions back into actions. When you hear “security,” ask what power is being granted. When you hear “efficiency,” ask who loses a service or job. When you hear “the people,” ask which people are included and which are silently excluded. Then reverse the test. Would you use the same word if the opposing party performed the same act? Confucius’ insight is not that perfect language creates perfect politics. It is that a society loses an important form of control when words no longer constrain the people who use them. If anything can be called anything, power gains freedom and citizens lose a common language for judgment. Sources and further reading Analects 13.3, traditionally associated with the “rectification of names.” Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy — Confucius Chinese Text Project — Analects, Zi Lu ← Return to the Guide to Manipulation and Deceit library
Current page · August 30, 2026

The cold machinery

A plain-language essay on Han Fei, political information, incentives and the machinery rulers use to control officials and claims.

The cold machinery Current page August 30, 2026 A plain-language essay on Han Fei, political information, incentives and the machinery rulers use to control officials and claims. Han Fei begins from an uncomfortable assumption: people in power have incentives to mislead one another. The ancient Chinese thinker associated with the tradition later called Legalism was concerned with a ruler’s information problem. Ministers know things the ruler does not. They can exaggerate success, conceal failure, build private networks and use elegant speech to advance themselves. Han Fei’s proposed solutions are authoritarian and often harsh. They should not be mistaken for a democratic model. But his diagnosis of incentives remains useful for understanding how lies move through institutions. The ruler cannot see everything Large organizations run on reports. Someone says a project is on schedule. Someone reports that a province is calm. Someone claims a policy succeeded. The leader at the top cannot independently observe every fact. That creates an opening between reality and the description of reality. Han Fei worries constantly about this gap. A clever official can use rhetoric, loyalty or personal access to substitute a favourable story for performance. Where power depends on reports, control of the report can become a form of power. Match words to results One important Han Feian theme is often described through the relationship between names or claims and actual performance. An official takes responsibility for a task; the result is compared with what was promised. The attraction is obvious. Do not reward eloquence. Check the outcome. Modern institutions use less brutal versions of the same idea: audits, written mandates, measurable targets, independent inspectors and records that allow later comparison between promise and result. These systems are imperfect, but they reduce the space in which flattering reports can float free of reality. Incentives can produce dishonesty without conspiracy An organization does not need a central order to lie. It may only need incentives that punish bad news. If a manager is promoted for reporting success, subordinates learn what kind of report is welcome. If mistakes bring humiliation while concealment brings time, concealment becomes rational. Each person may shade the truth only slightly. By the time information reaches the top, the picture can be fiction. This is one reason strong leaders can become badly informed. If loyalty is measured by optimism, honest messengers disappear. Han Fei’s dangerous answer Han Fei seeks reliability through law, technique, reward and punishment rather than trust in virtue. The system is deliberately impersonal. Officials should not be able to manipulate the ruler through private affection or moral reputation. The danger is equally obvious. A surveillance-heavy system can produce more lying, not less, because people become terrified of reporting failure. Harsh punishment encourages concealment. A ruler obsessed with control may destroy the independent sources needed to correct the ruler’s own errors. That is where a modern democratic reading must depart from Han Fei. Accountability works best when information can travel upward without fear and when power itself can be checked. The machinery of truth The useful lesson is institutional. Truthfulness is not secured by demanding that everyone be virtuous. Systems need ways to compare claims with evidence. Who verifies the numbers? Can inspectors work independently? Are dissenting reports preserved? Can a whistleblower contradict a superior without career destruction? Does the public see the evidence behind an official claim? Those questions turn truth from a personal preference into an institutional property. Han Fei’s politics is cold because it assumes incentives matter more than good intentions. That can become oppressive. But it also identifies a real weakness in every hierarchy: when the person with the most power hears only what subordinates believe that person wants to hear, the machinery of government can become a machinery of self-deception. Sources and further reading Han Feizi , especially passages on law, administrative technique, rewards and punishments. Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy — Legalism in Chinese Philosophy Chinese Text Project — Han Feizi ← Return to the Guide to Manipulation and Deceit library
Current page · August 30, 2026

Reason against the mob

A plain-language essay on Condorcet, public reasoning, education and the conditions under which collective judgment can improve or fail.

Reason against the mob Current page August 30, 2026 A plain-language essay on Condorcet, public reasoning, education and the conditions under which collective judgment can improve or fail. The Marquis de Condorcet believed human beings could improve their institutions through reason, education and the spread of knowledge. He also tried to describe mathematically when collective decisions become more reliable. The result now associated with his name—the Condorcet jury theorem—is often reduced to the claim that large groups are wise. That is not what it says. Its assumptions are the important part. When more people can help In a simplified version of Condorcet’s argument, imagine a group choosing between two alternatives, one of which is correct. If each voter has a probability greater than one-half of choosing correctly and the judgments are sufficiently independent, adding more voters can increase the probability that the majority reaches the correct answer. That is a powerful defence of collective judgment. No ruler needs to be infallible. Many imperfect people can outperform one decision-maker. But change the assumptions and the result changes. A crowd is not made wise by size alone. Information, competence and independence matter. Manipulation attacks the assumptions Propaganda can be understood as an assault on the conditions that make collective judgment useful. If millions of people receive the same false information, their errors are no longer independent. If a campaign systematically lowers the quality of information, the average voter may not be more likely than chance to identify the correct answer. If social pressure makes people copy their group, adding more people may simply amplify the same error. That is why a false story repeated across a network can be more dangerous than one isolated lie. It creates correlated error. Education is political infrastructure Condorcet argued strongly for public education. The connection to democracy is direct. Citizens cannot exercise judgment if knowledge is monopolized by elites or if large parts of the population are denied the tools needed to evaluate claims. Education does not guarantee agreement. It improves the capacity to ask what evidence supports a statement, how a number was produced and whether an argument follows from its premises. A democratic information system therefore needs more than freedom to speak. It needs institutions that help people become competent judges: schools, libraries, independent research, transparent statistics and journalism that shows its evidence. The mob is created, not discovered Political rhetoric often treats “the crowd” as naturally irrational. Condorcet’s framework suggests a better question: what conditions are shaping the crowd’s judgment? Are people receiving diverse evidence or one synchronized message? Are they deciding independently or signalling loyalty? Are claims testable? Are mistakes corrected? Are institutions increasing competence or rewarding outrage? A public can be made less rational by the environment in which it is asked to decide. Majority rule is not truth Even under favourable conditions, a vote decides what a group will do. It does not transform the winning proposition into a fact. Scientific claims, historical events and legal evidence are not made true by popularity. Democracy requires a distinction between political authority and factual reality. A majority can legitimately choose a policy while being mistaken about the facts used to justify it. That is why democratic systems need mechanisms that survive electoral pressure: courts, auditors, professional statistical agencies, archives and protected research. The defence When a claim is popular, do not ask only how many people believe it. Ask whether those people reached the belief independently and from adequate evidence. When the same assertion appears everywhere, ask whether that represents confirmation or merely repetition from a common source. Condorcet’s optimism is conditional. Collective intelligence is possible. So is collective error. The difference lies not in whether the public is a noble people or a foolish mob, but in the information and institutions through which millions of individual judgments become one political decision. Sources and further reading Condorcet, Essay on the Application of Analysis to the Probability of Majority Decisions (1785). Condorcet, Sketch for a Historical Picture of the Progress of the Human Mind .
