Washington takes a strategic stake in Vancouver-based Trilogy Metals.

The U.S. Department of War has signed definitive agreements for a US$35.6-million strategic investment in Vancouver-based Trilogy Metals.

Washington takes a strategic stake in Vancouver-based Trilogy Metals. Current page August 29, 2026 The U.S. Department of War has signed definitive agreements for a US$35.6-million strategic investment in Vancouver-based Trilogy Metals. Critical minerals · United States · Industrial policy Washington takes a strategic stake in Vancouver-based Trilogy Metals. The U.S. Department of War has signed definitive agreements for a US$35.6-million investment that gives Washington ownership and influence in the Canadian-headquartered mining company. Published Aug. 29, 2026 · Updated Aug. 29, 2026 The U.S. Department of War has signed definitive agreements to invest US$35.6 million in Vancouver-based Trilogy Metals, giving Washington a direct 10 per cent stake in the TSX-listed company and warrants that could lift its economic position to 17.5 per cent. The agreements go beyond a passive investment. The U.S. government will have the right to designate an independent director to Trilogy’s board until October 2028 and appoint a non-voting board observer while it continues to hold at least eight million shares. Trilogy has also agreed not to incur more than US$1 billion in new third-party debt without Department of War approval through the beginning of 2029. The money is intended to support exploration, engineering and infrastructure connected to the Upper Kobuk Mineral Projects in northwest Alaska. Those deposits include copper, cobalt, germanium and other minerals Washington considers strategically important to the American defence industrial base and to efforts to reduce dependence on foreign mineral supply chains. The resources themselves are in the United States, not Canada. That distinction matters: the transaction does not give Washington control over Canadian mineral deposits. But Trilogy is headquartered in Vancouver and publicly traded in Canada, making the structure notable as the United States increasingly uses state capital to secure strategic materials. The arrangement also shows how industrial policy is moving beyond subsidies, tax credits and procurement. Washington is taking an ownership position, obtaining warrants and securing governance rights in a company that controls access to strategically important projects. That gives the U.S. government a direct financial interest in the development of the assets rather than merely supporting a private operator from the sidelines. For Canada, the deal is a reminder that the competition over critical minerals is becoming more state-directed. Ottawa has identified critical minerals as central to economic security, defence supply chains and trade diversification. The Trilogy transaction shows how aggressively the United States is prepared to deploy public money to shape those same supply chains. The definitive agreements were announced Friday and convert a previously proposed investment into a binding transaction. Closing is expected in September, subject to customary conditions. Sources Trilogy Metals — definitive agreements, Aug. 28, 2026 U.S. Department of War — strategic equity investment, Aug. 28, 2026
Current page · August 29, 2026

Canadians back a hard line on U.S. trade—but the economic consent has limits.

An Ipsos poll for Global News finds strong backing for Canada

Canadians back a hard line on U.S. trade—but the economic consent has limits. Current page August 29, 2026 An Ipsos poll for Global News finds strong backing for Canada Public opinion · Canada–U.S. trade · Sovereignty Canadians back a hard line on U.S. trade—but the economic consent has limits. A new Ipsos survey for Global News finds broad backing for Canada’s decision to walk away from an unacceptable deal and answer American tariffs. Its more revealing finding is that the willingness to absorb the price of that stance is unevenly distributed. ipsos Polling source Published Aug. 29, 2026 · News synthesis Canada’s trade confrontation with the United States has acquired something that governments facing economic coercion often need: public permission to resist. An Ipsos survey commissioned by Global News, conducted after the latest talks collapsed, found large majorities support Canada holding its ground and imposing counter-tariffs on U.S. goods. The headline numbers are emphatic. Sixty-three per cent of respondents said Canada was right to stand firm, even if the choice brought higher costs and job losses. Seventy-three per cent supported Ottawa’s dollar-for-dollar response to the latest U.S. tariffs. Those are not narrow margins or a polite preference for a compromise. They suggest that many Canadians see the dispute as more than a technical fight over rates and product lists. That matters because the federal government’s stated rationale is not merely commercial. In its announcement of countermeasures, Ottawa said it suspended negotiations rather than accept terms it believed would damage Canadian workers, strategic sectors and the national interest. Prime Minister Mark Carney has also connected the dispute to the country’s ability to maintain independent decisions on trade, culture and domestic policy. Support for resistance is real The survey was fielded online among 1,001 Canadian adults on Aug. 26 and 27, with quotas and weighting intended to reflect the population. Ipsos put its credibility interval at plus or minus 3.8 percentage points, 19 times out of 20, if all Canadian adults had been surveyed. That is a useful warning against treating any one poll as a referendum. It is also sufficient to establish the central result: this is not a divided country on the immediate question of whether Canada should simply concede. Global News reported that support also extends beyond the tariff list already announced by Finance Canada. Respondents were broadly open to further measures involving energy and critical minerals, tighter border inspections for U.S. imports, a tougher tax approach to large American technology companies, and Canadian-preference rules in public procurement and infrastructure. The figures do not tell Ottawa to deploy every one of those tools. A public appetite for leverage is not a substitute for assessing legality, supply chains, regional exposure, consumer costs or the effect on Canadian workers. Electricity restrictions, for example, would affect connected Canadian and American systems; counter-tariffs can protect some domestic producers while raising costs for importers and households. Public opinion measures consent, not a complete policy design. Still, the result narrows the political space for a deal that appears to trade away core Canadian choices for a temporary easing of tariffs. The point of a negotiation is not simply to end it. It is to reach terms worth living with after the headlines fade. The endurance-economy warning The same survey supplies the caution that should govern the next phase. Younger Canadians were less likely than older respondents to support holding firm or retaliating. Global News reported 48 per cent support for standing firm among Generation Z adults and 57 per cent among millennials, compared with roughly four in five older boomers on the core measures. The gap is not difficult to understand. Younger adults are more likely to be navigating high rents or mortgages, precarious work, debt and the everyday vulnerability of prices rising faster than incomes. A geopolitical posture that is survivable for an established household can be a much more immediate risk for someone already deciding between groceries, rent and an unexpected bill. Fifty-two per cent of respondents said they were personally prepared to accept significant economic pain to help Canada weather the dispute. That is a majority, but it is not an unlimited blank cheque. The number falls to 41 per cent among Generation Z respondents and rises to 67 per cent among boomers. The government cannot responsibly read this as a national endorsement of hardship without a corresponding responsibility to cushion the people who will bear it first. That is why the accompanying policy matters as much as the counter-tariffs. Finance Canada says its package includes $7.5 billion in new and enhanced supports, including regional assistance for small and medium-sized firms, liquidity programs and measures for workers and sectors harmed by U.S. duties. Whether those supports are fast enough, accessible enough and targeted enough will determine whether public solidarity remains durable. A parliamentary test There is another message in the poll that Ottawa should not dismiss. Sixty-two per cent of respondents told Global News they want Parliament recalled before its scheduled late-September return to debate and vote on the response to the United States. That is not a rejection of the government’s trade stance. It is a request for democratic ownership of it. A trade crisis that may alter prices, jobs, investment and Canada’s long-term economic orientation cannot be managed only through ministerial announcements and news conferences. Parliament is where competing regional interests can be put on the record, where support programs can be tested and where opposition parties must state what they would do differently. The poll therefore describes a country that is more resolved than resigned. Canadians appear willing to resist American pressure, but they also expect the costs to be shared fairly and the choices to be debated openly. The political advantage from standing firm will not last on symbolism alone. It will depend on competent protection for exposed workers, credible diversification beyond the U.S. market and an unmistakable refusal to turn sovereignty into a bargaining chip. What this poll can and cannot show: It measures the views of surveyed Canadians during Aug. 26–27. It does not predict the economic consequences of tariffs, prove that every proposed countermeasure is workable, or settle the trade dispute. Sources Global News, Sean Boynton — Ipsos poll on Canada–U.S. trade policy Department of Finance Canada — countermeasures and support for workers and businesses, Aug. 25, 2026 Ipsos Canada — August consumer-confidence update
Current page · August 29, 2026

Norway’s European bargain offers Canada a lesson—not a blueprint.

What Norway’s EEA arrangement says about market access, dairy, fisheries, sovereignty and Canada’s search for a durable European economic relationship.

Norway’s European bargain offers Canada a lesson—not a blueprint. Current page August 29, 2026 What Norway’s EEA arrangement says about market access, dairy, fisheries, sovereignty and Canada’s search for a durable European economic relationship. Canada’s rupture with Washington has made a European question newly urgent. If Canadians want durable alternatives to American economic leverage, what sort of relationship with Europe would actually preserve room for a Canadian state to make Canadian choices? Norway is the useful comparison because it has already lived inside that tension for three decades. It is not an EU member. It has access to the European single market through the European Economic Area, or EEA. Its firms participate in the market of the European Union’s 27 members; Norwegians can live, work, study and establish businesses across that space. Yet Norway has not joined the EU customs union, the common commercial policy, the common agricultural policy, the common fisheries policy, the euro or the EU’s foreign and security policy. That is a serious bargain, not a fairy tale. Norway accepts large amounts of EU-derived economic law to keep its market access. It has institutions—the EFTA Surveillance Authority and EFTA Court—that monitor and enforce the agreement. It can help shape EU rules before they are adopted, but it does not get a vote when the EU makes the final decision. Nor does the EEA guarantee that every new European industrial policy will treat Norway as an insider. This week’s warnings from Norway and Switzerland about “Made in Europe” preferences are proof that access and voice are not the same thing. The Canadian question: broad market access can be compatible with sovereignty only when the areas Canada will not trade away are named in the agreement, defended by institutions, and understood before the pressure arrives. What Norway bought—and what it did not sell The EEA gives Norway the four freedoms at the heart of the single market: movement of goods, services, capital and people. That is why it is often casually described as “the Norway option.” The casual label conceals the constitutional weight of the deal. For the parts of the economy covered by the agreement, Norway continuously incorporates relevant European rules. In return, it gets predictability for companies whose supply chains and customers span Europe. But Norway’s exclusions are not decorative. Agriculture and fisheries sit outside the EEA’s free circulation rules. That matters because they touch food security, rural life, coastlines, political identity and the ownership of natural wealth. Agricultural trade is negotiated separately under Article 19 of the agreement, using preferential access and quotas rather than a simple promise that every product moves tariff-free. Norway also retains its own trade policy with countries outside the EEA, commonly negotiating through EFTA. It retains the krone and the ability to make its own monetary choices. Canada should notice the structure. The lesson is not that an agreement must reproduce Norway’s particular carve-outs. Canada’s economy, geography and federal constitution are different. The lesson is that durable economic integration is built from clear compartments: where common rules apply; who interprets them; what is specifically excluded; and how disputes are settled when politics gets loud. Dairy: protected in both countries, but by different routes Canadian dairy has become a familiar target in U.S. trade arguments because Canada’s supply-management system uses production controls, administered prices and tariff-rate quotas. A quota allows a fixed quantity of an import to enter at a lower or zero rate; imports above that amount face the higher, over-quota tariff. Canada’s parliamentary research service describes TRQs as the mechanism that grants trading partners a minimum level of access while maintaining protection beyond it. CUSMA created additional U.S. dairy access, and the administration of those quotas has repeatedly been the subject of disputes. Norway also protects agriculture, including dairy, but it does so outside the EEA’s central market bargain. Its agricultural relationship with the EU is negotiated under Article 19, with mutual preferences and quotas. The European Commission explicitly says agriculture and fisheries are exempt from free circulation between Norway and the EU. The two systems are not identical—Canada’s supply management and Norway’s farm policy have different mechanics and political histories—but they demonstrate the same basic fact: a state can have deep commercial ties with much larger partners without putting every sensitive food sector into a single free-trade rulebook. That is especially relevant after the U.S. effort to turn market access into leverage over Canada’s broader sovereignty and third-country trade choices. A future Canada–Europe arrangement should not ask dairy farmers to carry the entire symbolic weight of independence. It should state plainly what access exists, how it is administered, how a dispute is arbitrated and what domestic policy remains Canada’s to set. Issue Norway–EU / EEA Canada’s European objective What Canada must protect General market access Deep single-market access for covered sectors; extensive regulatory alignment. Broader, more usable access for Canadian firms, services and investment. Democratic control over the scope of incorporated rules. Dairy and agriculture Outside free circulation; managed through Article 19 preferences and quotas. Predictable access without turning supply management into a permanent bargaining chip. TRQ administration and Canada’s ability to design farm policy. Fisheries and resources Outside the common fisheries policy; Norway retains core national control. Open export routes and investment while retaining resource-policy choices. Provincial jurisdiction, Indigenous rights, environmental review and public interest tests. Rules and enforcement EFTA institutions enforce EEA obligations; Norway has influence but no EU vote. Independent, transparent dispute settlement—not political pressure. A clear parliamentary and provincial role before new obligations bind Canada. Industrial policy Access is not immunity: “Made in Europe” rules can still exclude close partners. Association with European programmes and procurement where possible. No acceptance of rules that quietly turn “Europe” into a closed club. Fish, resources and the hard edge of sovereignty For Norway, the refusal to join the EU’s common fisheries policy is central. Fisheries are not merely exports; they are a question of coastal communities, access to waters and the state’s authority over a resource. Canada’s equivalent concerns are larger and more varied: fisheries, critical minerals, hydroelectricity, oil and gas, forests and the provincial authority that governs much of that terrain. A serious Canadian European strategy therefore cannot be just a tariff schedule. It needs a resources chapter that rejects the American habit of treating Canada’s endowment as a strategic annex of another state. Canada can offer reliable supply, co-investment and transparent rules. It should not offer an arrangement that makes export capacity or public procurement a lever for another government to pull in a political dispute. Norway’s energy position also underlines the point. It sells gas into Europe at a scale that makes it a crucial supplier, but it has not converted that role into automatic membership of every European industrial preference. The current argument over “Made in Europe” programmes shows the friction: an integrated neighbour can pay, align and supply, yet still discover that a bloc’s industrial politics puts insiders first. Canada should go to Europe with its eyes open about this. Partnership is valuable; access to decision-making is more valuable; neither should be confused with deference. The cost of rule-taking There is no honest Norway comparison without the cost. The EEA is often summarized as “access without membership.” In practice, it is access in return for taking on much of the law that governs that access. Norway is represented in expert groups and can contribute during the drafting process, but it does not have a seat at the final EU table. Its domestic politics has long wrestled with that democratic deficit. Canada should not pretend that it could copy that arrangement untouched. Canada is a G7 country, an Atlantic and Pacific power, a CPTPP member and a federation. Its relationship with Europe should be built around an enhanced CETA and sectoral association, not a ritual imitation of a Nordic treaty. But the Norwegian experience does strip away a false choice. The choice is not between complete autonomy and surrender. It is between rules you help shape and can enforce, and arbitrary leverage exercised by the country that happens to be your dominant customer. Washington’s recent approach provides the negative example. A deal that asks Canada to constrain its future relationships with other trading partners, while accepting punitive tariffs and uncertain exemptions, is not a stable integration bargain. It is dependence with a short political fuse. Norway’s arrangement is more constrained than nationalist myth allows, but it is also more legible than that. Its obligations, exceptions and courts are visible. A Canadian European bargain worth pursuing Canada’s goal should be an economic relationship with Europe that has four qualities. First, it should make CETA work in practice: regulatory cooperation, services access, mobility for skilled workers and procurement that do not disappear into administrative barriers. Second, it should seek formal association with European industrial, research, energy and critical-minerals programmes where both sides gain. Third, it should preserve clear Canadian control over agriculture, culture, resources and the federal–provincial division of powers. Fourth, it should create dependable dispute settlement and consultation, so the next political quarrel does not become an excuse to threaten a whole national economy. There will be trade-offs. Europe will protect its own industry, as the current debate makes clear. Canada will have to decide when common standards make sense and when local control matters more. But that is real statecraft: spelling out the trade-off and winning consent for it, rather than discovering after the fact that a larger power has redefined the deal. Norway’s example is not a destination. It is a map of the questions Canada must answer: What access is worth taking common rules for? Which sectors are non-negotiable? Where must Canada have a seat, and where is an independent court enough? How do we make sure an industrial strategy called European does not become a barrier against the very partners Europe needs? Canada does not need to become a star on somebody else’s flag. It needs a set of durable relationships in which mutual access is purchased with rules, not intimidation—and in which Canadian sovereignty is a term of the deal, not the price of getting one. Current page · August 29, 2026

The U.S. trade rupture is accelerating Canada’s new Pacific oil pipeline.

The Canada-U.S. trade rupture is accelerating plans for a new Pacific oil pipeline as Ottawa pushes to reduce dependence on the American market.

The U.S. trade rupture is accelerating Canada’s new Pacific oil pipeline. Current page August 29, 2026 The Canada-U.S. trade rupture is accelerating plans for a new Pacific oil pipeline as Ottawa pushes to reduce dependence on the American market. Canada’s confrontation with the United States is beginning to change the timetable of the country’s energy infrastructure. Mark Maki, chief executive of the federally owned Trans Mountain Corporation, said Friday that the collapse of Canada-U.S. trade talks has increased the urgency of plans for a new oil pipeline to the Pacific coast — a project designed to give Canadian producers substantially greater access to Asian markets. “Events around us have made (the west coast pipeline) much more urgent, and I think that’s actually good. It’s helpful,” Maki told Reuters. The significance goes well beyond another pipeline proposal. Canada remains one of the world’s largest oil producers, but roughly 90 per cent of its oil exports still go to the United States. That concentration has become a strategic vulnerability as Washington escalates tariffs and political pressure against Canada. The federal government and Alberta had already agreed in July to advance a new pipeline capable of carrying about one million barrels a day toward global markets. The proposed corridor would begin in the Bruderheim area of Alberta and largely follow the existing Trans Mountain route to southern British Columbia, avoiding any change to the federal north-coast tanker moratorium. The strategic change: Canada is no longer discussing Pacific energy diversification simply as an economic opportunity. The deteriorating U.S. relationship is turning alternative export capacity into sovereignty infrastructure. A pipeline already running close to full The existing Trans Mountain system illustrates why the question is becoming immediate. The pipeline’s expansion entered service in 2024, tripling nominal capacity to about 890,000 barrels per day. Trans Mountain reported Friday that the system averaged 840,000 barrels per day during the second quarter — about 94 per cent of capacity — compared with 703,000 barrels per day a year earlier. Much of that oil is already moving beyond the United States. The Canadian Press reported that roughly two-thirds of Trans Mountain volumes are destined for Asian markets by tanker, while other shipments serve British Columbia and the U.S. Pacific Northwest. Trans Mountain is simultaneously trying to squeeze considerably more capacity out of the existing system. Its optimization program contemplates adding as much as 300,000 barrels per day. The first stage uses drag-reducing agents to increase throughput by about 90,000 barrels per day. A larger mainline optimization project involving additional pumping capacity and other infrastructure could add approximately another 210,000 barrels per day by the end of 2028, subject to investment decisions and regulatory approvals. That would take the existing system from roughly 890,000 barrels per day to as much as 1.19 million barrels per day before the proposed new west-coast pipeline is counted. Trade diversification becomes national policy Ottawa’s own description of the new pipeline is unusually explicit. In July, the federal government said the project was intended to “strengthen our sovereignty” and diversify Canadian energy exports toward new and growing Asian markets. The proposed ownership structure would bring together Trans Mountain, the Alberta Petroleum Marketing Commission and Pembina Pipeline Corporation, with a meaningful opportunity for Indigenous equity participation. Trans Mountain and Alberta would initially divide most of the ownership between them, while Pembina would hold a private-sector interest. The Major Projects Office is considering whether to list the pipeline as a national-interest project under the Building Canada Act, a designation intended to streamline federal review while maintaining environmental requirements and Indigenous consultation. Maki told Reuters that Trans Mountain needs to move quickly on route planning and consultations so proponents can seek regulatory approval early next year. The project remains a proposal. It has not received final regulatory approval, and the enormous investment required means its eventual economics will depend in part on Canadian production growth and long-term commitments from oil producers. That uncertainty is real. Reuters reported earlier this month that several proposed Canadian pipeline expansions together could add far more capacity than producers currently appear prepared to fill. Large oil-sands producers have remained cautious about committing the capital required for major new production. But the strategic calculation has changed. The price of dependence For decades, geography made the United States Canada’s overwhelmingly natural energy customer. Pipelines ran south, refineries were integrated across the border and the American market offered enormous demand immediately next door. That system was economically efficient when the political relationship was predictable. The current trade confrontation is forcing Canada to put a price on that dependence. The United States has imposed 50-per-cent tariffs on a new range of Canadian goods following the breakdown of negotiations, while Canada has announced dollar-for-dollar retaliation. Washington has also threatened much more damaging action against the deeply integrated Canadian auto sector. Oil itself is not the immediate target of those measures. But the dispute demonstrates how rapidly access to the U.S. market can become political leverage. That is why Pacific export capacity now belongs in the same discussion as defence procurement, critical minerals, domestic manufacturing and expanded trade with Europe and Asia. Each is part of the same strategic problem: how much freedom of action does Canada possess when its largest economic relationship becomes coercive or unpredictable? The answer will not be to abandon the United States. The two economies, particularly their energy systems, remain profoundly interconnected. Maki separately warned Friday that deliberately disrupting Canadian energy exports to punish the United States would be damaging to Canada as well. Diversification is a different strategy. It seeks to give Canada choices. A second Pacific pipeline capable of moving roughly one million additional barrels a day would represent one of the largest physical changes to Canada’s trading geography in decades. Combined with expansions to Trans Mountain, LNG terminals, ports, railways and new trade relationships, it would shift a greater share of Canadian energy toward markets that cannot be reached efficiently today. Canada has talked about that objective for years. The difference in August 2026 is that Washington is giving Ottawa a reason to move faster. Sources Prime Minister of Canada — Canada and Alberta advance west coast pipeline proposal Government of Canada — Strengthening our sovereignty and diversifying exports Reuters — Trans Mountain CEO says U.S. trade rift adds urgency The Canadian Press — Trans Mountain pipeline running almost full
Current page · August 28, 2026

Canada puts $195 million behind a sovereign quantum supply chain.

Ottawa invests $195 million in Xanadu

Canada puts $195 million behind a sovereign quantum supply chain. Current page August 28, 2026 Ottawa invests $195 million in Xanadu Canada at War illustration · Quantum manufacturing and technological sovereignty OTTAWA — Canada is putting $195 million into an $893-million expansion by Toronto-based Xanadu Quantum Technologies, financing new research facilities and advanced manufacturing capacity for one of the technologies Ottawa increasingly treats as a question of national security and sovereignty. The federal investment, announced Friday through the Strategic Response Fund, is expected to create 275 jobs. More important strategically, the project is intended to establish Canadian capabilities for integrating, packaging, testing and assembling the photonic and semiconductor components used in quantum computers. Those are precisely the difficult manufacturing stages that determine whether a country merely produces promising research or controls an industrial supply chain. From laboratory to factory Canada has long possessed an unusually strong quantum research community. Its recurring problem has been familiar across advanced technology: inventions and talent can originate here while manufacturing, scale-up, financing and commercial value migrate elsewhere. The Xanadu investment is an attempt to interrupt that pattern. Innovation, Science and Economic Development Canada says the new facilities will develop manufacturing processes compatible with future fault-tolerant quantum computers. According to Ottawa, such capabilities do not yet exist at industrial scale. Xanadu uses photons — particles of light — as the foundation of its quantum architecture. Its approach is intended to allow modular, networkable systems while avoiding some of the extreme cooling requirements associated with other quantum-computing designs. Ottawa is no longer treating quantum computing simply as a science program. It is treating the ability to manufacture quantum systems as strategic national capacity. A sovereignty investment The government's language is unusually explicit. Friday's announcement links the project to Canada's Defence Industrial Strategy and National Quantum Strategy and describes quantum technology as important to future Canadian prosperity, security and sovereignty. Potential applications reach directly into areas watched by Canada at War: cybersecurity, defence, energy, logistics and advanced industrial systems. A sufficiently capable quantum computer could eventually transform materials discovery and optimization while also threatening existing forms of encryption — one reason governments and militaries are investing heavily in both quantum computing and quantum-resistant cybersecurity. Canada is therefore competing in a field in which technological dependence can become strategic dependence. The industrial race International competition is already intense. Canadian government briefing material describes rising foreign investment as a risk to Canada's early quantum lead and notes that Canadian companies are participating in the U.S. Defense Advanced Research Projects Agency's Quantum Benchmarking Initiative. Ottawa has responded with several layers of support. Xanadu was previously awarded up to $23 million in the first phase of the Canadian Quantum Champions Program. The federal government says that program is explicitly intended to anchor promising quantum companies in Canada and develop a domestic ecosystem extending from research and talent to infrastructure and industrial supply chains. The latest $195-million commitment is much larger because it addresses the next problem: manufacturing. Canada's quantum sector could contribute $17.7 billion to GDP and support more than 157,000 jobs by 2045, according to federal projections. Those forecasts are necessarily uncertain in a technology that has yet to produce a universally useful fault-tolerant quantum computer. The strategic calculation, however, is already being made. The missing link The significance of the Xanadu project is not that Canada has suddenly achieved technological independence. Semiconductor fabrication and specialized components remain globally distributed, and no country operates an entirely self-contained advanced-technology supply chain. The question is instead which pieces Canada must control itself. Packaging, integration, testing and assembly are part of that answer. They create engineering expertise, intellectual property, specialized suppliers and production knowledge that cannot be recreated instantly during a geopolitical or trade disruption. That logic now runs through Canadian industrial policy. Ottawa is investing in domestic critical minerals, defence production, artificial intelligence infrastructure, biomanufacturing and semiconductor capacity while attempting to diversify trade and security relationships beyond overwhelming dependence on the United States. Quantum manufacturing belongs in the same category. Canada's confrontation with Washington has made the argument more immediate. Sovereignty is not only a matter of borders or diplomatic independence. In an economy increasingly organized around strategic technologies, it also depends on whether a country can design, finance and manufacture the systems on which its government, military and industries may eventually rely. The $195-million Xanadu investment is a bet that one of those systems should be built in Canada. Sources Innovation, Science and Economic Development Canada — Aug. 28 announcement ISED — parliamentary briefing on Canada's quantum strategy and industrial competition ← Back to Canada at War
Current page · August 28, 2026

Trump moves to the centre of Quebec’s election

Quebec’s election campaign has opened with Donald Trump and the U.S. trade war reshaping the province’s debate over jobs, Canada and sovereignty.

Trump moves to the centre of Quebec’s election Current page August 28, 2026 Quebec’s election campaign has opened with Donald Trump and the U.S. trade war reshaping the province’s debate over jobs, Canada and sovereignty. QUEBEC CITY — Quebec’s provincial election campaign has barely begun, but Donald Trump is already occupying a remarkable position in it: not as a distant foreign-policy issue, but as an adversary around whom competing visions of Quebec’s future are being organized. Coalition Avenir Québec Leader Christine Fréchette opened the campaign for the Oct. 5 election with an unusually direct declaration: “My opponent is named Donald Trump.” She called the U.S. president the “real adversary” and asked Quebecers for a mandate to guide the province through the economic storm created by the Canada-U.S. trade war. The message reflects immediate economic pressure. U.S. tariffs are hitting Quebec industries, while a National Bank analysis cited by The Canadian Press found Quebec faces the highest effective tariff rate on exports among Canadian provinces. The trade conflict is therefore not an abstraction for the campaign. It is becoming a test of which party can best protect jobs, investment and Quebec’s room to act. Trump complicates the sovereignty question The more consequential effect may be on the province’s sovereignty debate. The Parti Québécois entered the election period leading in public opinion and committed in principle to another referendum on independence. But leader Paul St-Pierre Plamondon said before the campaign that he would not hold that referendum while Trump remains president. On Thursday, he warned voters not to allow the Trump administration’s tariffs to “confiscate” Quebec’s election or determine the province’s political future. The difficulty for the PQ is visible in the polling. Reuters reported Aug. 22 that support for Quebec independence is around 30 per cent, among the lowest levels in decades. Trump’s repeated threats against Canada — including his 51st-state rhetoric — have stirred Canadian patriotism even among some francophone Quebecers who might otherwise be sympathetic to sovereignty. That creates an unusual political inversion. A U.S. president hostile to Canada might have been expected to deepen Quebecers’ doubts about the federation. Instead, the external threat is giving federalists a new argument: in a confrontation with the United States, Quebec may be safer and stronger as part of a country of roughly 41 million people. Quebec Liberal Leader Charles Milliard made precisely that case, arguing that an international trade war demonstrates the advantage of remaining inside Canada. On Friday, he went further, promising to reduce the share of Quebec exports going to the United States by at least 15 per cent through market diversification. A campaign about protection — but protection from whom? Fréchette’s strategy also carries risks. The CAQ has governed for eight years and she inherited a party weakened in the polls after François Legault stepped down. Casting Trump as the central opponent gives her an opportunity to present herself as a crisis leader, but it does not erase voter concerns about health care, housing, affordability or the government’s economic record. St-Pierre Plamondon is trying to prevent the campaign from becoming a referendum on who can best defend Canada. His counterargument is that Quebec sovereignty itself is a form of protection: if Canadians insist national sovereignty must be defended against Washington, sovereigntists can ask why Quebec should not make the same claim for itself. That tension makes the 2026 election different from a conventional federalist-versus-sovereigntist campaign. Trump has inserted a second sovereignty question on top of Quebec’s traditional one. Voters are being asked not only whether Quebec should have more independence from Canada, but how much economic and political independence either Quebec or Canada can realistically maintain from the United States. The trade war is already producing practical responses. Canadian Trade Minister Dominic LeBlanc said Thursday that Washington had dropped objections to Canadian measures supporting French-language and cultural content after those policies had surfaced as trade irritants. In Quebec, where language and culture are core political questions, even trade negotiations can now be read through the lens of national identity. The Oct. 5 vote will still turn on provincial issues. But the opening days have established something extraordinary: a Quebec election is being fought partly around the question of how to withstand pressure from an American president. Whether that strengthens Canadian federalism, revives Quebec nationalism in a new form, or simply pushes economic security ahead of both will be one of the campaign’s defining questions. Sources Associated Press, Aug. 28: Fréchette names Trump the “real adversary” Reuters, Aug. 22: tariffs and Quebec sovereignty sentiment The Canadian Press/National Observer, Aug. 28: trade war and sovereignty debate Reporting and analysis. Election positions and polling are attributed to the cited reporting; interpretation of the campaign’s sovereignty dynamics is analysis.

Detroit turns on Trump’s Canada trade war

Michigan is absorbing the costs of Washington’s escalating trade war with Canada, and opposition is spreading across the Detroit-Windsor economy.

Detroit turns on Trump’s Canada trade war Current page August 28, 2026 Michigan is absorbing the costs of Washington’s escalating trade war with Canada, and opposition is spreading across the Detroit-Windsor economy. DETROIT — The political backlash to Donald Trump’s trade war with Canada is becoming increasingly visible in the American city with perhaps the most to lose from it. Detroit and Windsor do not function economically like two distant cities separated by an international frontier. Their factories, suppliers and workers are parts of a deeply integrated manufacturing system in which components can cross the border repeatedly before a finished vehicle reaches a showroom. The Guardian reported Friday that about US$1 billion in goods crosses the Detroit River each day. Against that backdrop, local political and economic voices are describing the latest escalation in unusually blunt terms. The Detroit Regional Chamber warned after the breakdown in Canada-U.S. negotiations that escalating tariffs would inflict economic pain on both sides of the border, particularly Michigan and the Detroit-Windsor region. Chamber president and CEO Sandy K. Baruah said the two economies are “intertwined, interconnected, and stronger together,” and argued that global competitors stand to benefit from a weakened North American trade bloc. Michigan is paying for the tariff strategy The political problem for the White House is that the argument is no longer theoretical. The Guardian cited a June Epic-MRA survey finding Michigan residents opposed tariffs on Canadian goods by 63 per cent to 31 per cent, with opposition extending to 35 per cent of Republicans. Nearly three quarters of respondents said tariffs were contributing to higher prices. The newspaper also reported estimates that Michigan residents are paying more than US$3,200 annually on average because of tariffs — far more than the national average — reflecting the state’s unusually high exposure to cross-border commerce. Michigan Democrats have seized on that vulnerability. State Senate Majority Leader Winnie Brinks said this week that 50-per-cent tariffs on Canada would raise costs for families and put additional pressure on manufacturers and small businesses. Michigan Democratic Party chair Curtis Hertel warned that the measures could cost jobs and deepen disruption in manufacturing. But the deeper issue is industrial rather than partisan. Reuters reported Wednesday that automakers had entered the latest negotiations expecting relief from existing U.S. auto tariffs. Instead, Trump announced a 50-per-cent tariff on Canadian vehicles, parts and trucks beginning Jan. 1. The industry had reportedly expected a prospective agreement to lower the top-line tariff on Canadian cars and light trucks from 25 per cent to 15 per cent. That reversal matters because the Detroit Three are not simply American companies buying foreign Canadian products. General Motors, Ford and Stellantis operate production networks across the border. Toyota and Honda also build extensively in Canada. American-made parts are embedded in Canadian-built vehicles, just as Canadian inputs are embedded in vehicles assembled in the United States. A political risk in a pivotal state Michigan also matters because the tariff fight is arriving during a U.S. midterm election campaign in which control of Congress could turn on closely contested races in the state. A policy intended to demonstrate economic strength against Canada is therefore creating a conspicuous pocket of domestic opposition in one of America’s most important manufacturing regions. The administration’s central argument is that tariffs can force production and jobs back into the United States. But the Detroit-Windsor experience exposes the difficulty of applying that logic to an industry built through decades of continental integration. A tariff on a Canadian component can become a cost imposed on an American factory that needs it. For Canada, the Detroit reaction is strategically important. Ottawa’s retaliatory measures are designed in part to make the cost of the trade war visible inside the United States. Michigan is evidence that this pressure does not have to create anti-Canadian sentiment. In a region whose prosperity has long depended on Canada, it can instead intensify demands for Washington to restore predictable cross-border trade. The most striking opposition to the Canada trade war may therefore not come from another foreign capital. It may come from across the river in Detroit. Sources The Guardian, Aug. 28: Detroit reaction to the Canada trade war Detroit Regional Chamber, Aug. 22: statement on Canada-U.S. trade relations Reuters, Aug. 26: automakers and the 50-per-cent tariff threat Michigan Advance, Aug. 26: Michigan political reaction Reporting and analysis. Political statements are attributed to their speakers; economic implications are identified as analysis where appropriate.
