Canada enters the tariff shock with an $8.8-billion external surplus

Canada swung to an $8.8-billion current-account surplus in the second quarter, its largest since 2005, as goods exports and energy revenues surged.

Canada enters the tariff shock with an $8.8-billion external surplus Current page August 28, 2026 Canada swung to an $8.8-billion current-account surplus in the second quarter, its largest since 2005, as goods exports and energy revenues surged. Canada earned more from its transactions with the rest of the world than it spent in the second quarter of 2026, producing an $8.8-billion current-account surplus and reversing an $8.3-billion deficit only three months earlier. Statistics Canada says it was the country’s first current-account surplus since the second quarter of 2022 and the largest since the final quarter of 2005. In the context of Canada’s renewed confrontation with the United States, the result matters because the current account is one of the broadest measures of a country’s economic relationship with the outside world. The improvement was driven overwhelmingly by merchandise trade. Canada’s goods balance swung from a $6.4-billion deficit in the first quarter to a $12.2-billion surplus in the second—the largest goods surplus since 2008. Energy did the heavy lifting Goods exports jumped 13.1 per cent to a record $232.1 billion. Energy exports rose 27.4 per cent, with crude oil and bitumen exports reaching a record $44.8 billion and total energy-product exports reaching $60.6 billion. That strength reflects both Canada’s enormous resource base and unusually favourable international conditions. Statistics Canada attributes part of the rise in prices to international production and supply uncertainty associated with the conflict in Iran. It therefore would be a mistake to read the $8.8-billion surplus as evidence that Canada has already solved its structural trade vulnerabilities. A large part of the improvement came from a commodity-price windfall. But it is equally mistaken to dismiss what the figures say about Canadian economic capacity. Canada was able to increase exports dramatically at the same time as imports reached their own record. Motor vehicles and parts exports increased 19.3 per cent as Canadian auto production rose, although they remained below the quarterly averages recorded in 2023 and 2024. A stronger starting point The timing is politically and economically important. These numbers largely describe Canada immediately before the latest escalation in the U.S. tariff confrontation. They are not a measurement of the damage—or resilience—that will follow the newest tariffs and counter-tariffs. Instead, they establish the starting point. Canada entered that confrontation with a current-account surplus, a record level of goods exports and a substantial goods-trade surplus. The figures also arrive alongside stronger-than-expected second-quarter GDP data, reinforcing the picture of an economy that had regained momentum before the newest tariff shock. Foreign money was also moving into Canada The financial account contains another striking number. Foreign investors increased their holdings of Canadian securities by $100.6 billion during the quarter. Foreign purchases of Canadian debt securities reached a record $110.2 billion, including a record $80.8 billion of government bonds. Foreign direct investment in Canada totalled $25.9 billion, up from $18.8 billion in the first quarter. More than half of that investment was directed toward manufacturing and finance and insurance. The principal sources were the United States, United Kingdom and Netherlands. At the same time, Canadian direct investment abroad slowed sharply, and nearly three-quarters of that outward investment went to countries other than the United States—another small but notable indication of the increasingly diversified geography of Canadian capital. The sovereignty question For Canada, an external surplus is not sovereignty by itself. The country remains deeply dependent on the United States as a customer, particularly for energy. The second-quarter figures demonstrate both sides of the Canadian problem: enormous capacity to sell resources and manufactured products abroad, but continued exposure to a concentrated continental trading relationship. The strategic question is whether Canada can turn temporary external strength into durable diversification—more pipelines and LNG capacity to non-U.S. markets, more Pacific and Atlantic port capacity, deeper European and Asian trade, more domestic processing and manufacturing, and greater Canadian ownership of the intellectual property and industrial capacity behind its exports. The second-quarter surplus does not answer that question. It does show that Canada is not beginning the latest stage of the trade confrontation from the position of external weakness that the first-quarter deficit suggested. Primary source: Statistics Canada, Canada’s balance of international payments, second quarter 2026 . Independent corroboration: Reuters .
Current page · August 28, 2026

World Watch: The trade war is widening into an industrial-bloc fight

Canada’s tariff battle now sits inside a wider contest over Chinese manufacturing, European industrial policy and protected markets.

World Watch: The trade war is widening into an industrial-bloc fight Current page August 28, 2026 Canada’s tariff battle now sits inside a wider contest over Chinese manufacturing, European industrial policy and protected markets. Global developments reviewed Aug. 28, 2026. This report excludes the widely covered GDP-growth story. The Canada–United States rupture is no longer an isolated North American dispute. A fresh review of developments around the world points to a broader shift: governments and industrial lobbies are increasingly treating trade not as a set of rules for lowering barriers, but as a tool for protecting domestic capacity, forcing concessions and reorganizing supply chains by political bloc. For Canada, that makes the immediate tariff fight more consequential. It is taking place while Europe debates stronger defences against Chinese industrial competition, while its closest non-member economic partners worry about being shut out of European procurement and subsidy rules, and while Washington adds symbolic pressure to an already damaged relationship with Ottawa. Canada–U.S.: a tariff fight becomes a sovereignty fight Reuters reported Aug. 22 that the United States imposed 50-per-cent tariffs on some Canadian goods after negotiations failed. The U.S. Trade Representative said the measures applied to nearly US$20 billion of imports from Canada—about 5.2 per cent of U.S. imports from Canada in 2025—and, unlike earlier measures, did not retain the usual USMCA exemption for covered goods. The immediate commercial effects are real, especially for exposed manufacturers. But the political conflict continued to move beyond trade on Aug. 27, when President Donald Trump ordered U.S. federal agencies to use the name “Lake America” for Lake Ontario. The order cannot change the name in Canada, nor does it alter international geography. Its significance is political: it turns an already hostile tariff conflict into a public assertion of unilateral American power over a shared border and a shared lake. That does not itself change a customs rate. It does make a negotiated return to ordinary relations harder, because it reinforces the Canadian view that the dispute is not only about market access. Germany presses the EU to toughen up on China The other major development is in Europe. Reuters reported Aug. 28 that German industry groups are pressing Chancellor Friedrich Merz for a stronger policy response to Chinese imports and subsidies. Germany’s trade deficit with China reached €89.3 billion in 2025, according to the Reuters report, and the pressure is particularly acute in automobiles and manufacturing equipment. Business groups are asking for a “level playing field,” including faster use of EU trade-defence tools and potentially tariffs or local-content rules. Merz has asked cabinet ministers for proposals and has left open a common EU response if October discussions with China do not produce progress. This is not yet an EU decision to impose new tariffs. It is a warning that the European market Canada hopes to deepen ties with may become more protective of European production. For Ottawa, that raises a practical question for the October Canada–EU summit: can a deeper partnership secure reciprocal procurement, minerals and defence-industrial access before “Made in Europe” rules harden into barriers for partners? Europe’s industrial turn is straining its closest partners The Financial Times reported Aug. 28 that Norway, Switzerland, Iceland and Liechtenstein are increasingly concerned that new EU subsidies, import restrictions and local-content requirements will exclude them despite their deep integration with the European market. Norway’s concern has immediate relevance to Canada. Oslo is debating whether its existing relationship offers enough influence as EU decisions increasingly shape steel, energy and industrial policy. The country has been affected by EU steel-import restrictions, while Switzerland has warned that exclusionary rules can weaken, rather than strengthen, European supply chains. Canada is not in the European Economic Area and will have less automatic access than those countries. The lesson is not that closer Canada–EU ties are unwise; it is that political warmth and market access are different things. Any October package needs concrete, enforceable arrangements—especially on defence procurement, critical minerals, energy and standards—rather than broad language about partnership. What this means for Canada The world is not dividing neatly into free-trading democracies on one side and protectionist states on the other. The United States is using tariffs against a treaty ally. Europe is considering stronger industrial defences against China while its neighbours fear exclusion. China remains central to the competition over manufacturing capacity and supply chains. Canada’s task is therefore not simply to replace the U.S. market. It is to secure durable access to several markets without accepting political dependence on any one of them. That means treating trade agreements as security arrangements, insisting on enforceable reciprocity, and moving quickly while the rules of the next industrial order are still being written. Sources Reuters, Aug. 22: U.S. tariffs on Canadian goods Reuters, Aug. 28: German industry presses for tougher China policy Reuters, Aug. 27: U.S. order on Lake Ontario naming Financial Times, Aug. 28: EU industrial policy and Norway/Switzerland Reporting and analysis. The tariff and policy developments are reported facts; the implications for Canada are analysis.
Current page · August 28, 2026

The legal record of Trump’s Cabinet

A fact-checked guide separates charges, lawsuits, investigations and settlements—and records their actual outcomes.

The legal record of Trump’s Cabinet Current page August 28, 2026 A fact-checked guide separates charges, lawsuits, investigations and settlements—and records their actual outcomes. A fact-checked guide to the publicly documented legal matters involving the current Trump Cabinet, updated Aug. 27, 2026. The phrase “Trump’s Cabinet has legal problems” is too loose to be useful. A criminal charge, a civil complaint, an administrative investigation, a settlement by a government agency and an allegation never tested in court are not the same thing. They do not carry the same evidentiary weight, and they do not produce the same outcome. This report separates them. It examines the current officials listed on the White House’s Cabinet page as of Aug. 27, 2026. The list has changed since the administration began: Todd Blanche is attorney general; Jay Clayton is director of national intelligence; Markwayne Mullin is secretary of homeland security; and Keith Sonderling is acting labour secretary. The president and vice-president are not included in the roster used here, because this is a record of Cabinet and Cabinet-level officials rather than a retelling of Donald Trump’s separate and unusually extensive personal legal history. The standard is deliberately conservative. An entry appears only where a public record shows one of the following: a criminal charge, conviction or plea; a civil lawsuit naming the official personally, with its present result; a formal government or regulatory investigation, with its stated outcome; or a disclosed settlement that names the official or, in the case of a government office-holder, a settlement by the government that the official led or was sued in an official capacity. That distinction matters. A settlement is not automatically an admission of wrongdoing. A civil lawsuit is an allegation until a court finds otherwise. And an investigation closed without charges is not an exoneration by a court — but neither is it a criminal case. The result is more limited than an opposition-research file, and more useful. It identifies what is documented, says what happened to it, and does not turn proximity to a controversy into an accusation. At a glance Official Documented matter Outcome as of Aug. 27, 2026 Robert F. Kennedy Jr. 1983 heroin-possession prosecution Pleaded guilty; probation and court-ordered treatment. Pete Hegseth 2017 police investigation after sexual-assault allegation; later private settlement No criminal charge filed; Hegseth denied wrongdoing and said the encounter was consensual. Reported settlement was $50,000. Howard Lutnick Civil shareholder litigation over a Newmark bonus; older FINRA-recorded civil disclosures Newmark bonus case settled with the $50-million award returned; no criminal charge. FINRA entries record civil settlements, not criminal findings. Linda McMahon Pending civil litigation alleging she and WWE failed to protect minors from abuse by a WWE employee Civil allegations only; no criminal charge against McMahon. She disputes liability; no merits judgment or personal settlement has been reported. Kristi Noem Civil and access-to-records litigation naming her as governor or involving her administration State settlements were paid by South Dakota; they are not personal admissions or criminal cases against Noem. Kelly Loeffler 2020 federal inquiry into stock trades made during the early COVID-19 market collapse Justice Department closed the inquiry; no charge was filed. For the other current Cabinet officials, this review did not identify a publicly documented personal criminal charge, conviction, named civil settlement or completed regulatory enforcement action meeting the standard above. That is not a claim that no complaint has ever been made against any of them; it is a statement about the verified record located for this report. Robert F. Kennedy Jr.: a criminal conviction, followed by treatment Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. has the clearest criminal record of any present Cabinet official identified in this review. In 1983, Kennedy was arrested in South Dakota for heroin possession. He pleaded guilty to a felony charge. A judge imposed two years’ probation rather than jail and required treatment; accounts of the case report that the probation term was later reduced to one year after he entered treatment. Kennedy has not disputed the underlying history. He has spoken publicly for years about his addiction and recovery, saying he stopped using drugs after the arrest and treatment. That public account does not erase the conviction, but it is relevant context: this was a 43-year-old drug-possession case, not a current allegation or an unresolved prosecution. There is no basis for describing the case as merely an accusation. It ended in a guilty plea and a criminal sentence. There is equally no basis for inflating it into a claim of current misconduct. The documented outcome was a conviction, probation and treatment. Pete Hegseth: no charge after police investigation; a private settlement followed Secretary of War Pete Hegseth was investigated by police in Monterey, California, after a woman alleged that he sexually assaulted her in 2017. Hegseth has denied the allegation and said the encounter was consensual. The Monterey County district attorney did not file criminal charges, stating that the evidence did not meet the standard required to prove a case beyond a reasonable doubt. That is the criminal outcome: no charge and no criminal conviction. The matter did not end there. During his confirmation process, Hegseth disclosed that he had made a settlement payment to the woman. In a written response to Sen. Elizabeth Warren, he put the amount at $50,000. His lawyer has said the settlement was reached after the woman raised the prospect of civil litigation; the public record does not show a civil trial or a court finding that Hegseth committed an assault. The careful description is therefore important. A woman made a sexual-assault allegation; police investigated; prosecutors declined to charge; Hegseth denied the allegation; and he later made a reported $50,000 settlement. The settlement is a fact. It is not, by itself, a legal admission of liability. Howard Lutnick: civil disputes and a shareholder settlement, not criminal charges Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick’s record is chiefly one of business litigation, not criminal prosecution. The most significant recent case concerned a $50-million compensation award from Newmark Group, the commercial-real-estate company where Lutnick was executive chairman. Shareholders sued in Delaware, challenging the bonus. In 2025, the parties settled; reporting on the settlement and a subsequent Delaware fee ruling described the full $50 million as returned to the company. The shareholder lawyers received a separate fee award. The settlement resolved a corporate-governance dispute. It did not produce a criminal charge, a criminal finding or an admission by Lutnick that he committed a crime. For readers, the meaningful fact is that the bonus was challenged in court and was given up through a settlement. Lutnick’s FINRA BrokerCheck report also records historic civil-litigation disclosures from his securities-industry career. One entry reports an $80,000 settlement; another says remaining claims were settled without a payment by Lutnick. BrokerCheck disclosures are regulatory-record summaries, not a substitute for the underlying pleadings, and they do not establish criminal liability. They are included here because they are official, publicly available disclosures and because the request is for settlements as well as charges. Lutnick has also been associated with major lawsuits brought by Cantor Fitzgerald and other firms he led. Those corporate cases are not included as personal legal matters unless he was individually named or a public record identifies his personal settlement. A chief executive’s company being sued is not evidence that the executive was personally charged with wrongdoing. Linda McMahon: a pending civil case, not a criminal charge Education Secretary Linda McMahon and her husband, former WWE executive Vince McMahon, were named with WWE in civil litigation brought by former WWE “ring boys.” The plaintiffs allege that the company and its leaders failed to protect minors from sexual abuse by a former employee during the 1980s and 1990s. The allegations are serious, but the legal status must be described precisely. This is a civil lawsuit, not a criminal prosecution of Linda McMahon. The plaintiffs seek damages; the case has faced procedural delays and litigation over where and how claims should proceed. There has been no merits verdict holding Linda McMahon liable, and this review found no reported personal settlement by her of those claims. McMahon and WWE have disputed the claims. That response belongs in any fair account, as does the difference between Linda McMahon’s position and separate allegations or investigations involving Vince McMahon. This article does not assign his alleged conduct, his personal settlements or any regulatory action involving him to her. That is a basic but necessary discipline. Shared ownership of a company and marriage to another executive do not dissolve the legal distinction between defendants, claims and outcomes. Kristi Noem: settlements by South Dakota, not personal criminal cases Kristi Noem is no longer the current secretary of homeland security, but the events below occurred during her Cabinet tenure and her prior governorship. They are included because the subject is the current administration’s Cabinet record. Several lawsuits involved actions by the State of South Dakota while Noem was governor. They should not be described as criminal cases against Noem, nor as personal settlements by her. In 2024, South Dakota settled litigation brought by the Transformation Project, a transgender advocacy organization, after the state cancelled the organization’s grant-funded contract. The state agreed to pay $300,000 and issue an apology. The settlement involved the state and its health department. It did not include a judicial finding that Noem personally committed a legal wrong, and it was not a criminal matter. That same year, South Dakota paid $42,000 to settle an open-records lawsuit related to documents sought concerning the governor’s border-deployment programme. Earlier reporting also described a $200,000 settlement with a former state employee who alleged that she had been forced to retire. Each is relevant to the legal record of an administration Noem headed. None is a conviction, criminal charge or personal payment by Noem. Noem has also faced ethics complaints and reporting concerning travel, promotion of a dental practice and an undisclosed payment to a personal company. Those are not listed here as established legal violations because, on the sources reviewed, they are allegations, complaints or reporting rather than a completed charge with an adjudicated outcome. Reporting a complaint as if it were a conviction would be false. Kelly Loeffler: a federal inquiry closed without charges Small Business Administration Administrator Kelly Loeffler was among several U.S. senators whose stock trades drew scrutiny early in the COVID-19 pandemic. The Justice Department investigated trades made on Loeffler’s behalf after a private Senate briefing and before the market crash. In May 2020, the department closed its inquiry. No criminal charge was filed against Loeffler, and no SEC enforcement action against her emerged from the inquiry. Loeffler said the closure cleared her of wrongdoing. The more exact formulation is narrower: prosecutors closed the investigation without bringing a case. A decision not to charge is not a judicial declaration of innocence; it is nevertheless materially different from a criminal charge or conviction. Other current Cabinet officials: no verified personal case meeting this report’s standard located The White House’s current roster includes Scott Bessent, Todd Blanche, Doug Burgum, Jay Clayton, Doug Collins, Sean Duffy, Jamieson Greer, Markwayne Mullin, John Ratcliffe, Brooke Rollins, Marco Rubio, Keith Sonderling, Scott Turner, Russ Vought, Chris Wright and Lee Zeldin, in addition to the officials addressed above. For those officials, this review did not locate a public record of a personal criminal charge, criminal conviction, named civil settlement or concluded personal regulatory enforcement action that meets the standard used in this article. That wording is intentional. It is not a sweeping personal endorsement, and it does not imply that none has ever been criticized, sued in an official capacity or associated with a company that faced litigation. Several have been named as defendants in policy litigation because of their offices. Cabinet secretaries are routinely sued over executive actions, regulations or agency decisions. Such suits test the legality of government action. They do not, without more, establish personal misconduct by the office-holder. Similarly, some officials have worked for companies or organizations that have faced litigation. Business litigation against a corporation is not a criminal charge against every executive, board member or lawyer connected with it. A serious legal record has to keep those categories separate. Why the distinctions matter Public discussion of political figures often collapses four questions into one: 1. Was there an allegation? 2. Was there an investigation or a filed lawsuit? 3. Was there a charge, judgment, plea or settlement? 4. What did the final record actually establish? The answer to one is not automatically the answer to the others. Kennedy’s 1983 case ended in a criminal conviction. Hegseth’s 2017 matter produced no criminal charge but did lead to a reported settlement. Loeffler was investigated but not charged. McMahon faces a pending civil claim, not a criminal prosecution. Noem’s state-government settlements should not be inaccurately turned into personal admissions. Lutnick’s business disputes are civil matters with their own documented resolutions. Those are no
Current page · August 28, 2026

What China is saying about Canada’s trade war with the United States

Beijing’s official response is restrained, but Chinese media is turning the Canada-U.S. trade fight into an argument about coercion, alliances and strategic autonomy.

What China is saying about Canada’s trade war with the United States Current page August 28, 2026 Beijing’s official response is restrained, but Chinese media is turning the Canada-U.S. trade fight into an argument about coercion, alliances and strategic autonomy. Canada at War illustration. China News republished and discussed the White House’s eagle-and-goose social-media image on Aug. 27. In the space of two days, Chinese coverage of the Canada–U.S. trade confrontation has moved through three distinct registers: an officially neutral call for equal dialogue; state-media reporting that dwells on Canadian resistance; and a louder layer of commentary arguing that Washington is damaging its own alliances. The distinction matters. Beijing has not declared Canada a partner, and nothing in the official record supports saying that it has. But China plainly sees political and strategic value in the story. The official line: equal footing The clearest government statement came at the Chinese Foreign Ministry’s Aug. 26 briefing. Asked by Bloomberg about Canada’s retaliatory tariffs, spokesperson Lin Jian gave a single-sentence answer: economic and trade concerns, he said, should be settled through “dialogue and consultation on an equal footing.” The ministry’s official English transcript uses precisely that language. “China believes that economic and trade concerns should be addressed through dialogue and consultation on an equal footing.” — Lin Jian, Chinese Foreign Ministry, Aug. 26, 2026 It is formulaic diplomacy, deliberately so. Lin did not praise Ottawa, condemn Washington by name, endorse counter-tariffs or suggest a new Canada–China arrangement. Yet China chose to answer a question about Canada rather than deflect it. And “equal footing” is a pointed principle in a dispute Canada describes as involving both tariffs and the freedom to make sovereign economic choices. Beijing can restate its long-running opposition to economic coercion without formally inserting itself into a fight between two other countries. State media amplifies the Canadian answer Chinese outlets have been much less restrained. Xinhua’s Aug. 25 dispatch collected the strongest Canadian political responses: former prime minister Jean Chrétien’s proposal that Canada consider export taxes on energy, oil, gas and potash, and Ontario Premier Doug Ford’s warning that electricity and critical-mineral exports could become leverage if the conflict worsens. These are Canadian politicians’ statements, not Chinese policy proposals. But their prominence in Xinhua matters because they reinforce a simple message to Chinese readers: even the United States’ most integrated ally is prepared to impose costs. China News, a state-affiliated outlet, then turned the White House’s eagle-and-Canadian-goose social-media post into a story in its own right. Its Aug. 27 article documented the post’s claim that Trump was ending Canadian “free-riding,” then featured online mockery of its imagery. One counter-meme described a docile bird attacked by a more aggressive one, only for the goose to stand up and the hungry eagle to leave confused. Another reinterpreted the animals through U.S. midterm politics. That is not an official Chinese statement, and the comments themselves were not necessarily Chinese. What China News did was select and amplify an international online response that matched the outlet’s framing. The effect is to turn a piece of White House theatre into evidence of arrogance meeting resistance. It is a familiar information strategy: formal diplomacy stays measured while media reporting gives a sharper emotional form to the same underlying argument. The memes, and why China News highlighted them China News did more than report that the White House used a bald eagle and a Canadian goose. It preserved screenshots of the replies and gave the memes their own political interpretation. That editorial choice is important: the outlet was not merely relaying a social-media quarrel, but documenting how the attempt at symbolic dominance was reversed by commenters. “The goose fought back”: the screenshot used by China News, credited there to an online commenter. China News source . The first counter-image recasts the eagle-and-goose scene as an attack by a more aggressive bird on a more docile one. The key reversal is its ending: the goose refuses to submit, while the eagle leaves confused and still hungry. It is not a sophisticated trade argument. It is a compact moral story in which resistance, not accommodation, changes the balance of power. China News presents it as an answer to the White House’s claim that Canada had been free-riding. A second screenshot, in which the animals are recast as the U.S. midterms and MAGA. Credit: online commenter, as reproduced by China News. China News source . The second meme is more revealing because it removes Canada from the central symbolism altogether. China News says the commenter cast the eagle as the U.S. midterm elections and the goose as Trump’s MAGA movement, suggesting that domestic politics could eventually constrain the administration. This is an example of the trade fight being folded into an American argument about political accountability. China News’s decision to include it connects international trade conflict to perceived vulnerability inside the United States. The third screenshot shows a bear pinning the eagle while the goose watches. Credit: online commenter, as reproduced by China News. China News source . The bear-and-eagle version is the most overtly geopolitical. China News describes it as a joke about the escalating Canada–U.S. conflict, captioned “Bring it on.” The outlet does not identify the bear, and neither should we: readers may infer Russia, China, or a generic rival. Its significance lies in the image’s hierarchy. The White House picture depicts the United States as dominant over Canada; the reply imagines American power constrained by another actor, while Canada watches. China News gives that reversal prominence because it serves the wider media narrative that unilateral U.S. pressure produces resistance and exposes limits. The bigger narrative: Washington is pushing partners away The most ambitious claim is emerging in commentary. A Sohu article, published on a platform that carries user-generated material and therefore should not be read as official Chinese policy, argued that U.S. terms had sought to restrict Canada’s ability to make independent agreements with other countries, especially China. It cast the collapse as an act of self-sabotage: Washington wants Canada to help reduce reliance on Chinese critical minerals, the argument goes, yet tariff pressure may make Canada more willing to diversify toward China. That conclusion is a forecast, not a fact. Canada remains a NATO and Five Eyes member with substantial and unresolved disputes with Beijing on security, foreign interference, human rights and trade. Canada’s diversification strategy is not synonymous with replacing one dependency with another. Still, the Sohu argument exposes what Chinese commentators hope this moment will prove: American economic power does not automatically produce loyalty, and attempts to police a partner’s options can make alternatives more attractive. Global Times made a related point from the opposite direction on Aug. 27, criticizing U.S. media for treating any Canada–U.S. dispute as a story about whether China is “winning.” That response is revealing. Beijing wants credit for identifying what it says is a pattern of unilateral U.S. pressure, but it also wants to resist the idea that every rupture in the Western alliance system is a Chinese plot. In its preferred telling, Washington creates the friction itself. What Canadians should take from it Chinese coverage is useful evidence of international perception, not an instruction manual for Canada. Its sympathy is self-interested: a Canadian refusal to yield strengthens Beijing’s broader case that U.S. tariff coercion has costs beyond China. Nor does media attention amount to a trade offer, a policy shift or a geopolitical realignment. But the reaction does show that Canada’s fight is being watched far beyond North America. A government that sets out to punish a close ally may discover that every act of resistance becomes an example for other partners and competitors alike. China’s official statement is careful because it needs to preserve room to manoeuvre. Its media is less cautious: it sees Canada’s stand as proof that sovereignty, bargaining leverage and diversification are now part of the same argument. Sources Chinese Foreign Ministry: Lin Jian’s Aug. 26 briefing · China News: White House eagle-and-goose post · Xinhua: Canadian politicians sharpen tone over U.S. tariffs · Sohu platform commentary · Global Times: Why U.S. media keeps bringing China into the trade war . This article distinguishes formal Chinese government statements from state-media reporting and platform commentary. Translations are by the cited outlets or rendered into English for readability.
Current page · August 28, 2026

Macao Daily sees a turning point in Canada’s trade war with the United States

An extensive English summary and analysis of the Aug. 28 Macao Daily editorial on the Canada-U.S. trade war.

Macao Daily sees a turning point in Canada’s trade war with the United States Current page August 28, 2026 An extensive English summary and analysis of the Aug. 28 Macao Daily editorial on the Canada-U.S. trade war. “The U.S.–Canada trade war continues to heat up” is the blunt title of a Macao Daily editorial published Aug. 28. The piece is not a neutral dispatch. It is a political argument, written in the vivid, accusatory language common to Chinese-language commentary on the rupture. But it is also valuable evidence of how a newspaper in China’s wider media sphere is interpreting Canada’s confrontation with Washington: not as a routine tariff quarrel, but as a lesson in the limits of accommodation. The editorial begins with the immediate measure: Canada’s announced retaliation against roughly US$20 billion in American goods, with tariffs reaching 50 per cent. It calls the move fast and forceful, then works backwards through the negotiations to explain why, in its view, Ottawa had reached that point. Its central argument: yielding did not end the pressure Macao Daily’s essential story is that Canada spent more than a year attempting to preserve a workable North American trade arrangement, only to find that every concession invited a further demand. The editorial describes a United States that bargained from strength, raised its asks during negotiations and treated tariff threats not as a last resort but as a tool for extracting economic and political advantages. That account is the editorial’s interpretation, not a substitute for a published negotiating record. No complete authenticated term sheet from the failed talks has emerged publicly. The paper’s language should therefore be read as argument: it says Canada made repeated compromises in hopes of protecting the continental trade relationship, and then discovered that compromise had not produced a stable deal. The editorial’s thesis: Canada did not turn confrontational because it rejected negotiation; it turned confrontational because it concluded that yielding could not secure a respectful or durable settlement. It places the escalation on a timeline beginning in July, when it says Washington used tariff threats against a broad list of Canadian products while allowing short delays for talks to continue. The paper’s account then has the negotiations collapsing late on Aug. 21, Canada recalling its delegation and the 50-per-cent U.S. tariff taking effect the following day. It notes that Ottawa initially announced reciprocal action with a period before implementation, a detail the editorial treats as evidence that Canada had not immediately abandoned the possibility of a negotiated exit. From tariffs to industrial pressure The sharpest part of the editorial is its reading of the political purpose behind the dispute. It characterizes the U.S. position as using access to the American market to pressure Canadian firms into moving production south. In the newspaper’s terms, that is not ordinary trade competition: it is an attempt to weaken Canadian manufacturing capacity while demanding market access on American terms. That framing aligns with a concern now widely present in Canadian debate. The danger in an asymmetric continental economy is not only the tariff paid at the border. It is the investment decision made years earlier, when a company concludes it must locate future production inside the United States to avoid political risk. The editorial does not offer independent evidence that this was the explicit aim of every U.S. demand. Its contribution is to articulate how the threat is being understood abroad: tariffs can operate as industrial-location policy, especially when paired with public invitations for Canadian factories to relocate. Why the product list matters Macao Daily pays unusual attention to the design of Canada’s retaliation. It says the package covers more than 700 American products in three rate bands and highlights items such as dairy products, appliances, agricultural equipment and pulp. The editorial argues that the list was selected with political geography in mind, concentrating pressure on agricultural and manufacturing states, including Ohio, Michigan and Pennsylvania. The larger point is straightforward. Counter-tariffs are not merely a revenue measure. A government can choose products whose producers, workers and local politicians are positioned to demand a change in policy from Washington. The Macao editorial sees Canada trying to turn its economic leverage into U.S. domestic political pressure ahead of the midterm elections. Whether the package succeeds will depend on supply chains, substitution possibilities and the willingness of affected American industries to lobby — none of which the editorial can settle. But its reading correctly identifies the political logic that often shapes a targeted retaliation list. Three futures, in the paper’s view The editorial lays out three possible paths. The first is direct escalation: a tariff spiral in which both economies suffer. The second is a conditional compromise, in which a deal becomes possible but only if there is genuine room for a settlement. The third is a long-term restructuring, with Canada accelerating diversification and the United States losing some of its ability to set the terms of the North American economic relationship. That third possibility is where the piece reaches beyond the next round of tariffs. It presents the crisis as a test of whether Canada can reduce its exposure without pretending that the U.S. market can be replaced overnight. Diversification in this sense means more export capacity, more trade arrangements and more domestic industrial resilience — not an abrupt departure from a deeply integrated continental economy. The conclusion is both sympathetic to Canada and cautionary. Trade wars, it says, have no winners. Yet it argues that for Canada, continued concession would leave industries at a permanent disadvantage; for the wider world, the episode tests whether countries can maintain a rules-based trading system and a principled view of sovereignty when faced with pressure from a much larger economy. What this tells us — and what it does not Macao Daily is offering a clear political narrative, not a detached Canadian assessment. Its vocabulary is sharper than Ottawa’s, and some assertions about the negotiations cannot be independently verified without the missing deal text. That limitation should remain visible. Still, the editorial is notable for the way it connects several elements that are often discussed separately in Canada: tariff retaliation, the location of manufacturing, U.S. electoral politics, export diversification and sovereignty. Its central warning is that a country can preserve commercial access in the short term yet lose bargaining power and industrial capacity in the long term. That is why it treats the present confrontation as something bigger than a disagreement over duties. The question, in the paper’s telling, is whether Canada can insist that partnership cannot mean permanent vulnerability. Source and method This is an original English summary and analysis of the Macao Daily editorial, “The U.S.–Canada trade war continues to heat up,” Aug. 28, 2026 . It is not a translation. Statements about the editorial’s account and arguments are attributed to the paper; unresolved claims about the negotiations are identified as such.

Canada opens a new Pacific export hub — infrastructure for the pivot away from dependence.

PHOTO: CANXPORT and Port of Prince Rupert imagery is available through the official port material linked below. Official project source; no unlicensed news photograph reproduced. Canada's effort to diversify trade away from overwhelming dependence on the United States has…

Canada opens a new Pacific export hub — infrastructure for the pivot away from dependence. Current page August 28, 2026 PHOTO: CANXPORT and Port of Prince Rupert imagery is available through the official port material linked below. Official project source; no unlicensed news photograph reproduced. Canada's effort to diversify trade away from overwhelming dependence on the United States has… PHOTO: CANXPORT and Port of Prince Rupert imagery is available through the official port material linked below. Official project source; no unlicensed news photograph reproduced. Canada's effort to diversify trade away from overwhelming dependence on the United States has acquired another piece of physical infrastructure. The CANXPORT logistics facility has officially opened at the Port of Prince Rupert in northern British Columbia. Formerly known as the Ridley Island Export Logistics Project, the terminal connects rail shipments with marine containers and is designed to handle at least 400,000 containers annually, with potential capacity of 750,000. Ottawa contributed nearly C$50 million through the National Trade Corridors Fund. The facility includes three inbound and three outbound rail tracks dedicated to export transloading and is designed for products including forestry goods, agricultural commodities and plastic resins. Trade diversification requires ports The strategic significance lies in geography. Prince Rupert is North America's closest major port to Asia. In 2025 it handled 26.3 million tonnes of cargo, up 14 per cent from 2024, making it Canada's third-busiest container port and fourth-busiest port authority by tonnage. Canada can sign trade agreements across Europe and the Indo-Pacific, but those agreements cannot materially reduce U.S. dependence unless Canadian producers can move substantially larger volumes efficiently to overseas customers. Railways, transload facilities, terminals and berths are therefore as much a part of trade policy as tariff schedules. CANXPORT is especially useful because it converts bulk Canadian products arriving by rail into marine containers for export. That creates another route to customers that does not depend on the north-south continental supply chain. The Port of Prince Rupert says about C$3 billion in expansion projects are underway and could double the volume of goods moving through the port by 2030. CANXPORT sits alongside other new logistics and energy-export infrastructure. For Canada at War, this is what economic diversification looks like when it becomes concrete: not another promise to find new customers, but rails, cranes, containers and a Pacific port capable of moving Canadian production toward them. Sources Transport Canada · Prince Rupert Port Authority

$2.7 billion flows into B.C.'s gas backbone as Canada builds export options.

PHOTO: Enbridge Westcoast system/project imagery is available with the company's official announcement linked below. Official corporate source; no wire-service image reproduced. Private capital is making a C$2.7-billion bet on the infrastructure carrying Western Canadian natural…

$2.7 billion flows into B.C.'s gas backbone as Canada builds export options. Current page August 28, 2026 PHOTO: Enbridge Westcoast system/project imagery is available with the company's official announcement linked below. Official corporate source; no wire-service image reproduced. Private capital is making a C$2.7-billion bet on the infrastructure carrying Western Canadian natural… PHOTO: Enbridge Westcoast system/project imagery is available with the company's official announcement linked below. Official corporate source; no wire-service image reproduced. Private capital is making a C$2.7-billion bet on the infrastructure carrying Western Canadian natural gas toward domestic customers, the United States and increasingly the Pacific export market. Enbridge announced a joint venture led by KKR, with Apollo funds participating, to finance the already sanctioned Aspen Point and Sunrise expansion programs on the Westcoast natural gas pipeline system in British Columbia. The investors will ultimately receive an indirect cumulative 29-per-cent interest in the aggregate Westcoast system after Sunrise enters service. Enbridge retains majority ownership, operational control and responsibility for construction. Aspen Point is expected to enter service in 2026 and Sunrise in late 2028. Both projects have regulatory approval and are backed by long-term take-or-pay contracts. A Pacific-facing energy system The Westcoast network stretches more than 2,900 kilometres from northeastern British Columbia south to the Canada-U.S. border. It currently can move as much as 3.6 billion cubic feet of gas per day; Enbridge says capacity should reach about 3.9 bcf/d after Sunrise enters service. The system remains deeply integrated with the United States, serving the Pacific Northwest. But its role is changing as B.C. LNG projects create another outlet for Western Canadian gas. Apollo explicitly cited international LNG demand when explaining the investment. For Canada's diversification strategy, that distinction matters. Reducing dependence on one customer does not require severing continental infrastructure. It requires adding credible alternative markets and the transportation capacity to reach them. The transaction also demonstrates that Canada's trade-diversification buildout is attracting enormous pools of private capital. KKR and Apollo are not making a symbolic geopolitical gesture; they are investing against contracted infrastructure cash flows. That is precisely why the deal is significant. Source Enbridge official announcement
Current page · August 28, 2026

Sudbury reaches new nickel ore — and Canada gains another piece of strategic mineral security.

Glencore

Sudbury reaches new nickel ore — and Canada gains another piece of strategic mineral security. Current page August 28, 2026 Glencore PHOTO: Onaping Depth/Craig Mine project imagery from the Government of Canada and Glencore announcement. Official project source linked below; publication avoids unlicensed wire-service photography. Canada's critical-minerals strategy moved from plans and permitting to something much more tangible Thursday: miners reached the ore. Glencore Canada marked completion of the shaft and first access to the ore body at its Onaping Depth project at Craig Mine near Sudbury. The high-grade nickel and copper deposit lies almost 2,600 metres underground. Production is expected to begin later in 2026 and the project is designed to extend nickel production in the Sudbury Basin beyond 2040. The scale matters. Glencore says nearly $2 billion has been invested since construction began in 2019. The federal government says the project generated roughly 7,000 jobs during development, while Sudbury Integrated Nickel Operations already employs more than 1,000 people. Nickel is now sovereignty infrastructure Nickel is used in stainless steel and batteries and is increasingly treated by Western governments as a strategic input for advanced manufacturing and defence supply chains. Canada's policy challenge is therefore no longer simply to possess mineral deposits. It is to get mines operating, retain processing capacity and become a dependable supplier to Canadian industry and allied economies. That is why Ottawa described the milestone explicitly in the language of sovereign critical-minerals production. The project gives Canada a long-lived source of high-grade nickel in one of the country's oldest mining districts at a moment when governments are trying to reduce exposure to concentrated overseas supply chains. There is also a technological dimension. Onaping Depth is being developed around a fully battery-electric underground fleet, from drilling and extraction equipment to service vehicles. Ottawa previously committed up to $11 million through its industrial decarbonization program. The importance for Canada at War is straightforward: unlike a proposed mine or an exploration announcement, Onaping Depth has now physically reached the resource. Canada is adding productive capacity in a mineral that sits at the intersection of batteries, industrial manufacturing and national-security supply chains. Sources Natural Resources Canada · Glencore Canada release
Current page · August 28, 2026

Canada draws an auto-industry red line.

Ambassador Mark Wiseman says Canada cannot accept a U.S. trade agreement that fails to preserve a robust Canadian auto assembly and parts industry, as Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick offers a sharply different account of the failed talks.

Canada draws an auto-industry red line. Current page August 28, 2026 Ambassador Mark Wiseman says Canada cannot accept a U.S. trade agreement that fails to preserve a robust Canadian auto assembly and parts industry, as Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick offers a sharply different account of the failed talks. WASHINGTON — Canada has put a concrete industrial condition on any return to the negotiating table with the United States: an agreement must leave Canada with a viable automobile assembly and parts industry. Canada's ambassador to Washington, Mark Wiseman, told Reuters on Thursday that Ottawa cannot accept a trade bargain that fails that test. The statement turns a broader political argument about sovereignty into something measurable. Canada is not merely seeking a lower tariff number. It is insisting that the final structure of any agreement must permit cars, trucks and parts to continue being made in Canada. “We need to have those capabilities in Canada. We need to have those jobs in Canada.” — Mark Wiseman Wiseman said preservation of a robust assembly and parts industry is critical because automobile manufacturing remains a major component of Canada's industrial base, particularly in Ontario and Quebec. The intervention came less than a week after negotiations collapsed and only days after U.S. President Donald Trump threatened to raise tariffs on Canadian cars, trucks and automotive parts to 50 per cent on Jan. 1, 2027. That threat has transformed the automobile dispute from one negotiating file among many into a test of whether the deeply integrated North American auto industry can survive a deliberate attempt to move production across the border. Two accounts of how the deal died Wiseman's comments were accompanied by a strikingly different account from U.S. Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick. Lutnick told reporters that Canada had been positioned to receive what he called the best trade deal in the world. But he said Ottawa did not raise tariff relief for medium- and heavy-duty vehicles until the final hours of negotiations. According to Lutnick, Washington regarded those vehicles as a separate category. He suggested the late Canadian demand was inserted in a way that caused the negotiations to end. The Canadian account is substantially different. Wiseman has said previously that as negotiators examined the detailed written language, Canada found repeated differences between what it believed had been agreed and what appeared in the documents. Medium- and heavy-duty vehicles were one example, he said, rather than the single cause of the collapse. That distinction matters. In the American version, Canada added a new demand at the end of an otherwise exceptionally favourable negotiation. In the Canadian version, Ottawa discovered that the fine print would not protect industries it believed were covered by the political understanding. Neither government has published a complete authenticated draft of the failed agreement, making it impossible for outsiders to independently resolve every discrepancy between the two accounts. The argument is now about industrial capacity The most consequential part of Wiseman's statement is what it says about Canada's negotiating objective. For decades, Canadian governments generally treated access to the U.S. market as the central objective of continental trade policy. The current dispute is forcing Ottawa to consider a different question: access on what terms, and with how much domestic productive capacity left when the agreement is finished? A tariff reduction that allowed Canadian vehicles into the United States but created powerful incentives for automakers to move future assembly south could technically improve market access while weakening Canada's industrial base. Wiseman's language indicates Ottawa will judge a deal by the latter measure as well. That puts jobs, factories, supplier networks, engineering expertise and future investment at the centre of the negotiation. Why the auto sector is different Automobile manufacturing is unusually difficult to separate neatly along a national border. Vehicles assembled in Canada contain U.S.-made components. American factories use Canadian parts. Components can cross the border several times before a completed vehicle reaches a dealership. Reuters reported this week that Canadian-built vehicles accounted for about six per cent of U.S. auto sales in 2025. The exposure, however, is concentrated among some of the world's largest manufacturers. General Motors builds a portion of Chevrolet Silverado production in Canada. Stellantis builds the Chrysler Pacifica in Canada. Toyota and Honda have enormous Canadian manufacturing operations. Ford is preparing large-truck production at Oakville. That is why a 50-per-cent tariff would not simply punish a collection of Canadian-owned exporters. It would hit production decisions made by American, Japanese and European multinational automakers whose North American operations were constructed around decades of continental integration. Industry officials have warned that tariffs on Canadian parts would also raise costs at U.S. assembly plants. A looming 50-per-cent wall The immediate pressure comes from Trump's announcement that tariffs on Canadian vehicles, trucks and auto parts will rise to 50 per cent at the beginning of 2027 if the dispute is not resolved. Before negotiations collapsed, automakers had expected an agreement to reduce the headline tariff on Canadian cars and light trucks from 25 per cent to 15 per cent, according to Reuters. Instead, the threatened rate doubled. The contrast with U.S. arrangements involving Asian and European producers is particularly significant. Reuters reported that imports from those markets face 15-per-cent rates under separate agreements, while Canada — whose factories are physically integrated with American production — could face 50 per cent. Automobile executives have consequently questioned the economic logic of treating Canada more harshly than less-integrated overseas suppliers. Canada is not closing the door Wiseman was careful not to describe Canada's position as a refusal to negotiate. He said Ottawa will continue talking while discussions generate positive momentum and expressed optimism that an agreement can ultimately be reached. His description of the relationship was pragmatic: neither country is leaving the neighbourhood. But he also said Canada has a plan and resolve. Asked whether Ottawa could consider measures beyond the retaliation already announced, Wiseman said Canada would preserve all options. That language stops well short of announcing another escalation. It nevertheless signals that Ottawa does not regard the current retaliation as necessarily the end of its response. January changes the investment calculation now The 50-per-cent automobile tariff is scheduled for January, but investment decisions are being made before then. Honda has already warned that uncertainty surrounding the future of the Canada-U.S.-Mexico trade agreement could affect its thinking about a needed eighth North American assembly plant. That is the deeper danger for Canada. A factory does not have to close tomorrow for industrial capacity to migrate. If the next assembly plant, battery facility, component operation or model allocation repeatedly goes to the United States because executives cannot predict whether Canadian production will retain access to the American market, the industrial geography of North America can change gradually and then become extremely difficult to reverse. Ottawa's new red line therefore concerns future factories as much as existing ones. The meaning of a deal has changed The confrontation is exposing two very different definitions of a successful trade agreement. Washington's public argument emphasizes the concessions and tariff reductions it was prepared to offer Canada. The Trump administration has repeatedly argued that companies can avoid tariffs by manufacturing in the United States. Canada's emerging position is that an agreement that accelerates the relocation of Canadian production to the United States is not acceptable merely because some tariff rates are lower than they otherwise would have been. That is a fundamental difference. The Canadian government's test is increasingly not simply whether a deal permits trade. It is whether Canada emerges from the deal still possessing the industrial capacity to produce. For the automobile industry, Wiseman has now said so explicitly. Sources Reuters, Aug. 27: Wiseman interview and Lutnick remarks. Reuters, Aug. 26: automaker reaction and investment implications. Reuters, Aug. 24: threatened 50-per-cent Canadian vehicle and parts tariff. Global Affairs Canada: official biography of Ambassador Mark Wiseman and Canada-U.S. negotiating updates. Reuters report (syndicated copy) · Reuters auto-industry report · Global Affairs Canada — Mark Wiseman
Current page · August 27, 2026

The legal record of Trump’s Cabinet: charges, lawsuits, investigations and settlements

A careful public-record guide to documented legal matters involving current Trump Cabinet officials, their status and outcomes.

The legal record of Trump’s Cabinet: charges, lawsuits, investigations and settlements Current page August 27, 2026 A careful public-record guide to documented legal matters involving current Trump Cabinet officials, their status and outcomes. The phrase “Trump’s Cabinet has legal problems” is too loose to be useful. A criminal charge, a civil complaint, an administrative investigation, a settlement by a government agency and an allegation never tested in court are not the same thing. They do not carry the same evidentiary weight, and they do not produce the same outcome. This report separates them. It examines the current officials listed on the White House’s Cabinet page as of Aug. 27, 2026. The list has changed since the administration began: Todd Blanche is attorney general; Jay Clayton is director of national intelligence; Markwayne Mullin is secretary of homeland security; and Keith Sonderling is acting labour secretary. The president and vice-president are not included in the roster used here, because this is a record of Cabinet and Cabinet-level officials rather than a retelling of Donald Trump’s separate and unusually extensive personal legal history. The standard is deliberately conservative. An entry appears only where a public record shows one of the following: a criminal charge, conviction or plea; a civil lawsuit naming the official personally, with its present result; a formal government or regulatory investigation, with its stated outcome; or a disclosed settlement that names the official or, in the case of a government office-holder, a settlement by the government that the official led or was sued in an official capacity. That distinction matters. A settlement is not automatically an admission of wrongdoing. A civil lawsuit is an allegation until a court finds otherwise. And an investigation closed without charges is not an exoneration by a court — but neither is it a criminal case. The result is more limited than an opposition-research file, and more useful. It identifies what is documented, says what happened to it, and does not turn proximity to a controversy into an accusation. At a glance | Official | Documented matter | Outcome as of Aug. 27, 2026 | |---|---|---| | Robert F. Kennedy Jr. | 1983 heroin-possession prosecution | Pleaded guilty; probation and court-ordered treatment. | | Pete Hegseth | 2017 police investigation after sexual-assault allegation; later private settlement | No criminal charge filed; Hegseth denied wrongdoing and said the encounter was consensual. Reported settlement was $50,000. | | Howard Lutnick | Civil shareholder litigation over a Newmark bonus; older FINRA-recorded civil disclosures | Newmark bonus case settled with the $50-million award returned; no criminal charge. FINRA entries record civil settlements, not criminal findings. | | Linda McMahon | Pending civil litigation alleging she and WWE failed to protect minors from abuse by a WWE employee | Civil allegations only; no criminal charge against McMahon. She disputes liability; no merits judgment or personal settlement has been reported. | | Kristi Noem | Civil and access-to-records litigation naming her as governor or involving her administration | State settlements were paid by South Dakota; they are not personal admissions or criminal cases against Noem. | | Kelly Loeffler | 2020 federal inquiry into stock trades made during the early COVID-19 market collapse | Justice Department closed the inquiry; no charge was filed. | For the other current Cabinet officials, this review did not identify a publicly documented personal criminal charge, conviction, named civil settlement or completed regulatory enforcement action meeting the standard above. That is not a claim that no complaint has ever been made against any of them; it is a statement about the verified record located for this report. Robert F. Kennedy Jr.: a criminal conviction, followed by treatment Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. has the clearest criminal record of any present Cabinet official identified in this review. In 1983, Kennedy was arrested in South Dakota for heroin possession. He pleaded guilty to a felony charge. A judge imposed two years’ probation rather than jail and required treatment; accounts of the case report that the probation term was later reduced to one year after he entered treatment. Kennedy has not disputed the underlying history. He has spoken publicly for years about his addiction and recovery, saying he stopped using drugs after the arrest and treatment. That public account does not erase the conviction, but it is relevant context: this was a 43-year-old drug-possession case, not a current allegation or an unresolved prosecution. There is no basis for describing the case as merely an accusation. It ended in a guilty plea and a criminal sentence. There is equally no basis for inflating it into a claim of current misconduct. The documented outcome was a conviction, probation and treatment. Pete Hegseth: no charge after police investigation; a private settlement followed Secretary of War Pete Hegseth was investigated by police in Monterey, California, after a woman alleged that he sexually assaulted her in 2017. Hegseth has denied the allegation and said the encounter was consensual. The Monterey County district attorney did not file criminal charges, stating that the evidence did not meet the standard required to prove a case beyond a reasonable doubt. That is the criminal outcome: no charge and no criminal conviction. The matter did not end there. During his confirmation process, Hegseth disclosed that he had made a settlement payment to the woman. In a written response to Sen. Elizabeth Warren, he put the amount at $50,000. His lawyer has said the settlement was reached after the woman raised the prospect of civil litigation; the public record does not show a civil trial or a court finding that Hegseth committed an assault. The careful description is therefore important. A woman made a sexual-assault allegation; police investigated; prosecutors declined to charge; Hegseth denied the allegation; and he later made a reported $50,000 settlement. The settlement is a fact. It is not, by itself, a legal admission of liability. Howard Lutnick: civil disputes and a shareholder settlement, not criminal charges Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick’s record is chiefly one of business litigation, not criminal prosecution. The most significant recent case concerned a $50-million compensation award from Newmark Group, the commercial-real-estate company where Lutnick was executive chairman. Shareholders sued in Delaware, challenging the bonus. In 2025, the parties settled; reporting on the settlement and a subsequent Delaware fee ruling described the full $50 million as returned to the company. The shareholder lawyers received a separate fee award. The settlement resolved a corporate-governance dispute. It did not produce a criminal charge, a criminal finding or an admission by Lutnick that he committed a crime. For readers, the meaningful fact is that the bonus was challenged in court and was given up through a settlement. Lutnick’s FINRA BrokerCheck report also records historic civil-litigation disclosures from his securities-industry career. One entry reports an $80,000 settlement; another says remaining claims were settled without a payment by Lutnick. BrokerCheck disclosures are regulatory-record summaries, not a substitute for the underlying pleadings, and they do not establish criminal liability. They are included here because they are official, publicly available disclosures and because the request is for settlements as well as charges. Lutnick has also been associated with major lawsuits brought by Cantor Fitzgerald and other firms he led. Those corporate cases are not included as personal legal matters unless he was individually named or a public record identifies his personal settlement. A chief executive’s company being sued is not evidence that the executive was personally charged with wrongdoing. Linda McMahon: a pending civil case, not a criminal charge Education Secretary Linda McMahon and her husband, former WWE executive Vince McMahon, were named with WWE in civil litigation brought by former WWE “ring boys.” The plaintiffs allege that the company and its leaders failed to protect minors from sexual abuse by a former employee during the 1980s and 1990s. The allegations are serious, but the legal status must be described precisely. This is a civil lawsuit, not a criminal prosecution of Linda McMahon. The plaintiffs seek damages; the case has faced procedural delays and litigation over where and how claims should proceed. There has been no merits verdict holding Linda McMahon liable, and this review found no reported personal settlement by her of those claims. McMahon and WWE have disputed the claims. That response belongs in any fair account, as does the difference between Linda McMahon’s position and separate allegations or investigations involving Vince McMahon. This article does not assign his alleged conduct, his personal settlements or any regulatory action involving him to her. That is a basic but necessary discipline. Shared ownership of a company and marriage to another executive do not dissolve the legal distinction between defendants, claims and outcomes. Kristi Noem: settlements by South Dakota, not personal criminal cases Kristi Noem is no longer the current secretary of homeland security, but the events below occurred during her Cabinet tenure and her prior governorship. They are included because the subject is the current administration’s Cabinet record. Several lawsuits involved actions by the State of South Dakota while Noem was governor. They should not be described as criminal cases against Noem, nor as personal settlements by her. In 2024, South Dakota settled litigation brought by the Transformation Project, a transgender advocacy organization, after the state cancelled the organization’s grant-funded contract. The state agreed to pay $300,000 and issue an apology. The settlement involved the state and its health department. It did not include a judicial finding that Noem personally committed a legal wrong, and it was not a criminal matter. That same year, South Dakota paid $42,000 to settle an open-records lawsuit related to documents sought concerning the governor’s border-deployment programme. Earlier reporting also described a $200,000 settlement with a former state employee who alleged that she had been forced to retire. Each is relevant to the legal record of an administration Noem headed. None is a conviction, criminal charge or personal payment by Noem. Noem has also faced ethics complaints and reporting concerning travel, promotion of a dental practice and an undisclosed payment to a personal company. Those are not listed here as established legal violations because, on the sources reviewed, they are allegations, complaints or reporting rather than a completed charge with an adjudicated outcome. Reporting a complaint as if it were a conviction would be false. Kelly Loeffler: a federal inquiry closed without charges Small Business Administration Administrator Kelly Loeffler was among several U.S. senators whose stock trades drew scrutiny early in the COVID-19 pandemic. The Justice Department investigated trades made on Loeffler’s behalf after a private Senate briefing and before the market crash. In May 2020, the department closed its inquiry. No criminal charge was filed against Loeffler, and no SEC enforcement action against her emerged from the inquiry. Loeffler said the closure cleared her of wrongdoing. The more exact formulation is narrower: prosecutors closed the investigation without bringing a case. A decision not to charge is not a judicial declaration of innocence; it is nevertheless materially different from a criminal charge or conviction. Other current Cabinet officials: no verified personal case meeting this report’s standard located The White House’s current roster includes Scott Bessent, Todd Blanche, Doug Burgum, Jay Clayton, Doug Collins, Sean Duffy, Jamieson Greer, Markwayne Mullin, John Ratcliffe, Brooke Rollins, Marco Rubio, Keith Sonderling, Scott Turner, Russ Vought, Chris Wright and Lee Zeldin, in addition to the officials addressed above. For those officials, this review did not locate a public record of a personal criminal charge, criminal conviction, named civil settlement or concluded personal regulatory enforcement action that meets the standard used in this article. That wording is intentional. It is not a sweeping personal endorsement, and it does not imply that none has ever been criticized, sued in an official capacity or associated with a company that faced litigation. Several have been named as defendants in policy litigation because of their offices. Cabinet secretaries are routinely sued over executive actions, regulations or agency decisions. Such suits test the legality of government action. They do not, without more, establish personal misconduct by the office-holder. Similarly, some officials have worked for companies or organizations that have faced litigation. Business litigation against a corporation is not a criminal charge against every executive, board member or lawyer connected with it. A serious legal record has to keep those categories separate. Why the distinctions matter Public discussion of political figures often collapses four questions into one: 1. Was there an allegation? 2. Was there an investigation or a filed lawsuit? 3. Was there a charge, judgment, plea or settlement? 4. What did the final record actually establish? The answer to one is not automatically the answer to the others. Kennedy’s 1983 case ended in a criminal conviction. Hegseth’s 2017 matter produced no criminal charge but did lead to a reported settlement. Loeffler was investigated but not charged. McMahon faces a pending civil claim, not a criminal prosecution. Noem’s state-government settlements should not be inaccurately turned into personal admissions. Lutnick’s business disputes are civil matters with their own documented resolutions. Those are not technicalities. They are the difference between re
Current page · August 27, 2026

The real bedrock of Trumpism is class warfare — and Canada is already in its path

Project 2025 offers a governing theory of concentrated power with consequences for Canadian institutions, social trust and sovereignty.

The real bedrock of Trumpism is class warfare — and Canada is already in its path Current page August 27, 2026 Project 2025 offers a governing theory of concentrated power with consequences for Canadian institutions, social trust and sovereignty. Donald Trump’s politics are often described as populism, nationalism, authoritarianism or a culture war. Each label captures something real. But none gets to the centre of it as clearly as class warfare. Not class warfare in the old, straightforward sense of workers against owners. Trumpism’s achievement is to turn a conflict that is fundamentally about wealth and power into a permanent cultural battle among working- and middle-class people themselves. The promise is that ordinary Americans will be restored to dignity and power. The practical political method is to direct their anger away from concentrated corporate power, extreme wealth, deregulation, weakened labour rights and a shrinking public sphere — and toward migrants, racial minorities, public servants, universities, teachers, journalists, LGBTQ+ people, environmentalists and any institution that can be called “elite.” That does not mean every Trump supporter is wealthy, cynical or motivated by hostility. Many are responding to real injuries: wages that failed to keep pace with costs, hollowed-out towns, declining faith in government, unaffordable housing and a sense that powerful institutions no longer listen. Those grievances are real. The question is what politics does with them. Trumpism offers recognition and belonging, but its governing programme directs the resulting power upward. It seeks lower taxes and fewer constraints for the wealthy and large corporations, while making the state more punitive toward people with less security and fewer political resources. Its culture war is not a sideshow. It is the mechanism that makes this arrangement durable. The document is Project 2025 — and it is 922 pages The document is Project 2025 , formally called Mandate for Leadership: The Conservative Promise . It is not 2,925 pages; the main volume is 922 pages . Produced under the leadership of the Heritage Foundation and released in 2023, Project 2025 was prepared as a transition blueprint for the next conservative U.S. administration. Heritage described it as a plan to reshape federal agencies, and it was paired with a personnel database, training programme and a transition playbook. Its significance is that it is unusually concrete. It does not merely argue for conservative values. It goes department by department through the U.S. government, setting out how power can be centralized in the White House, how civil servants can be replaced or controlled, how regulatory agencies can be redirected, and how public institutions can be forced into a more ideological role. Donald Trump publicly distanced himself from Project 2025 during the 2024 campaign, and it was not formally his campaign platform. But the distinction should not obscure the overlap. Project 2025 and Trump’s own Agenda47 shared central themes: a more powerful presidency; the removal of officials regarded as disloyal; reduced regulatory power; a hard line on immigration; attacks on diversity, equity and inclusion programmes; a remaking of education; fossil-fuel expansion; and a more openly political use of government. The important point is not whether every page becomes law. It is that the project provides a governing theory: democratic institutions should not be independent counterweights to political power. They should be made answerable to a leader and an ideological movement. A politics that turns public institutions into enemies In a healthy democracy, institutions are imperfect but necessary mediators. Courts, civil services, public broadcasters, universities, professional regulators, independent agencies and public schools can restrain arbitrary power. They can also protect citizens who lack money, connections or media influence. Trumpism teaches people to regard many of those institutions as enemies. Sometimes that critique begins from a legitimate observation. Institutions can be complacent, bureaucratic, exclusionary or unaccountable. But Trumpism does not generally seek to democratize them. It seeks to subordinate them. The civil service becomes a “deep state.” Independent journalism becomes “the enemy of the people.” Universities become ideological factories. Public-health officials become conspirators. Judges who rule against the executive become corrupt. Election administrators become suspect if their count produces the wrong result. Once that worldview takes hold, the public servant is no longer a fellow citizen performing a professional duty. The teacher, librarian, scientist, reporter or regulator becomes a political combatant. Public life loses the shared ground on which people can disagree without treating one another as enemies.
