That is why the cultural element of Trumpism matters so much. A movement that wants to weaken independent institutions needs the public to distrust them first. The class war hidden inside the culture war The cultural targets change as needed. One week the focus is immigration; another, crime; another, transgender rights; another, a university protest; another, a school library. But the deeper pattern is remarkably consistent. Social anxiety is transformed into anger at visible, less powerful targets. The person worried about rent is encouraged to blame a newcomer. The person angry about a hospital wait is encouraged to blame a “woke” bureaucracy. The worker who feels disrespected is invited to focus on a symbolic cultural dispute rather than on the employer, private-equity owner or tax structure that may be shaping their material life. This is not to say that immigration, public safety, education or gender policy are imaginary issues. They are real matters of public debate. The question is whether they are being debated honestly, or being used to consolidate a political coalition whose economic programme serves the most powerful. A politics that promises to fight “elites” while weakening organized labour, cutting regulation, reducing taxes for the wealthy and attacking public institutions is not an uprising against the ruling class. It is a reorganization of power within that class, with public anger used as fuel. The genius of Trumpism is its emotional clarity. It tells people who is to blame. The danger is that it often points downward or sideways, not upward. Why this matters to Canada Canada is not the United States. Its constitutional structure, parliamentary system, political culture and public institutions differ. Canadians should not lazily assume that every American political development crosses the border unchanged. But Canada is unusually exposed to American political weather. The countries share a continent, an economy, media platforms, cultural products, business networks and an increasingly integrated information environment. When American politics becomes more punitive, conspiratorial and hostile to independent institutions, Canada does not remain untouched. The first impact is economic and institutional pressure. A U.S. administration that treats trade as leverage rather than as a rules-based relationship can use tariffs, threats and sectoral exemptions to press Canada into policy concessions. That pressure is not only about automobiles, steel, aluminum, dairy or energy. It can reach into procurement, digital policy, environmental regulation, border administration, defence, critical minerals and the broad question of whether Canada retains room to make its own decisions. A smaller country beside a far larger and more aggressive neighbour can be pushed toward a familiar response: accommodate, deregulate, lower standards, weaken public protections and call it competitiveness. That is not inevitable. But it is the logic of dependence. The second impact is political imitation. Canada already has its own versions of the American culture war: disputes over education, gender, immigration, public broadcasting, climate policy, Indigenous rights, urban crime and trust in government. These debates are real and often difficult. The risk comes when they are imported in American language and American proportions, stripped of Canadian context and used to define fellow citizens as enemies. Canada’s political traditions have long rested on a more modest premise than American revolutionary individualism: that a large, diverse northern country requires institutions capable of holding people together. Public health care, the CBC, public schools, universities, bilingualism, multicultural citizenship, the Charter, independent elections administration and a professional public service are all imperfect. But they are part of the civic infrastructure that allows a country as regionally divided as Canada to function. If Canadians come to see all public institutions as corrupt by definition, reform becomes harder and demolition becomes easier. The third impact is cultural. American political media is designed for intensity. Its algorithms reward outrage, certainty, humiliation and conflict. Canada’s smaller media ecosystem is economically vulnerable and increasingly dependent on the same platforms. The result is pressure to turn every disagreement into an American-style identity war. That changes more than politics. It changes how people treat neighbours, teachers, service workers, immigrants, public employees and even family members. It teaches that compromise is betrayal and that social trust is naïveté. For Canada, social trust is not sentimental. It is strategic. A country facing economic coercion, supply-chain shocks, defence pressures and a transforming global order needs citizens who can cooperate across region, language, class and background. It needs public institutions strong enough to act, but accountable enough to retain legitimacy. It needs a media culture that can expose failures without convincing the public that nothing is worth defending. The Canadian test The right response is not to pretend Canadian institutions have earned blind trust. They have not. Governments fail. Corporations capture regulators. Public systems can be slow and remote. Media organizations can be narrow, concentrated and timid. Universities can be insulated from ordinary life. But the answer to institutional failure is democratic repair — better transparency, stronger labour power, genuine affordability, anti-monopoly enforcement, accountable public services and independent journalism — not a politics of permanent demolition. Canada’s danger is not that it will become the United States overnight. It is that it will absorb the habits of Trumpism gradually: contempt for expertise, suspicion of every neutral institution, permanent cultural antagonism and the belief that power should belong to the leader who can hurt the right people. Project 2025 matters because it gives those habits a governing architecture. Its lesson is that class warfare does not always arrive carrying a banner for the rich. Sometimes it arrives wrapped in a flag, speaking the language of national renewal, promising to punish cultural enemies and asking ordinary people to surrender the very institutions that give them any protection from concentrated power. For Canada, the task is not simply to resist American pressure at the border. It is to recognize the same political logic when it appears inside our own culture — and to defend a country where citizenship is stronger than resentment, public institutions serve the public, and national sovereignty means more than choosing which powerful interests get to rule. Sources Project 2025, Mandate for Leadership: The Conservative Promise (full 922-page document) Heritage Foundation announcement of Project 2025’s policy guide Associated Press comparison of Project 2025 and Trump’s Agenda47 PBS NewsHour fact-check on Project 2025 and Trump’s second-term proposals Method: This analysis is based on the linked public records and reporting. It distinguishes allegation, investigation, charge, settlement and legal outcome.
Current page · August 27, 2026

The brain gain: Canada is recruiting the scientific talent America is losing

Canada is spending more than $541 million in the first phase of a $1.7-billion global talent strategy, recruiting 64 senior researchers — 48 of them from U.S. institutions — into strategic Canadian research programs.

The brain gain: Canada is recruiting the scientific talent America is losing Current page August 27, 2026 Canada is spending more than $541 million in the first phase of a $1.7-billion global talent strategy, recruiting 64 senior researchers — 48 of them from U.S. institutions — into strategic Canadian research programs. Canada has spent decades worrying about the brain drain: highly trained Canadians leaving for larger laboratories, richer universities and better-funded technology companies in the United States. On Thursday, Ottawa unveiled evidence that the flow can run the other way. The federal government announced more than $541 million for the first intake of the Eddie Goldenberg Research Chairs of Canada and Canada Impact+ Emerging Leaders programs. The centrepiece is $504 million over eight years for 64 senior research chairs recruited from 13 countries. Independent reporting by the Financial Times and Bloomberg establishes the striking detail behind the headline number: 48 of those 64 researchers are currently based at U.S. institutions. They are not being recruited merely to fill faculty vacancies. The federal program is explicitly built around fields Canada considers strategically important to its prosperity, resilience and sovereignty. Ottawa is buying time, teams and institutional capacity in fields ranging from artificial intelligence and advanced materials to biotechnology, climate resilience, energy systems, health technologies and public policy. The numbers: $504 million for 64 senior chairs over eight years; more than $37 million for 63 emerging leaders; $1 billion over 12 years for up to 100 senior chairs; $400 million for supporting research infrastructure; and $1.7 billion across the complete Global Impact+ initiative, designed to attract and support more than 1,000 international and expatriate Canadian researchers. The timing is impossible to separate from the political and scientific upheaval south of the border. The Financial Times reports that several of the recruited academics work in areas where U.S. support has been cut and describes the Canadian initiative as drawing elite scholars from an American university system facing political pressure and declining support under the Trump administration. Canada is not simply waiting to see whether researchers become unhappy. It has constructed a government-funded mechanism capable of moving quickly when they do. From brain drain to state strategy The Global Impact+ Research Talent Initiative was announced in December 2025. At $1.7 billion over 12 years, Ottawa described it from the outset as one of the largest programs of its kind internationally and emphasized speed and flexibility as competitive advantages. The idea was to give Canadian universities enough money and certainty to approach researchers who would otherwise be difficult to recruit. The senior-chair stream alone provides $1 billion over 12 years. Individual chairs receive either $8 million or $4 million over eight years, with the possibility of a four-year extension at half the original award value. Crucially, the program is designed to support not just a professor’s salary but the research operation around that person: teams, partnerships and the infrastructure required to build a durable program in Canada. Another $400 million over six years is being made available through a research infrastructure fund. The Emerging Leaders stream provides $120 million over 12 years to attract international early-career researchers. Ottawa has already announced 659 training awards for international and returning Canadian doctoral and postdoctoral researchers from 72 countries. That architecture matters. Recruiting a famous scientist without the laboratory, graduate students, equipment, technicians and long-term funding needed to do the work simply relocates a résumé. Canada is attempting to relocate research ecosystems. Harvard, MIT, Carnegie Mellon, Cornell — and now Canada The institutional names demonstrate the scale of the opportunity. The first cohort includes researchers moving from some of the most formidable universities and laboratories in the United States. The University of British Columbia is receiving nine of the 64 chairs. Among them is Peter Caravan, a Canadian citizen and UBC alumnus currently at Harvard Medical School and Massachusetts General Hospital, whose work focuses on molecular imaging and targeted therapies. Computational protein-design specialist Brian Kuhlman is moving from the University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill. Kathryn Whitehead, a major figure in lipid nanoparticle drug delivery, is coming from Carnegie Mellon University. UBC’s recruits will work on areas including medical diagnostics and treatments, protein design, drug delivery, freshwater ecosystems, wildfire preparedness and the effects of digital technology and artificial intelligence on democratic societies. McMaster University is receiving $43 million and five researchers from American universities. Their work spans radiopharmaceuticals, advanced materials and technologies, musculoskeletal health and social innovation. McMaster says each of its five senior chairholders will receive $8 million over eight years. McGill University is recruiting six senior researchers with $52 million in federal support. Its cohort includes specialists in RNA biology, engineering, digital public policy, sustainable chemistry and climate resilience. Chloé Arson is moving from Cornell University to work on geomechanics, AI-enabled subsurface exploration, geothermal energy, geologic hydrogen production and underground energy storage. The University of Toronto is bringing in six senior researchers across health, technology, economics and astrophysics. The group includes Sara Seager, the internationally prominent astrophysicist and planetary scientist associated with MIT, as well as experts in computer science, biostatistics, chemical biology, economics and medical imaging. These are not all identical forms of economic activity, and basic research does not automatically become a Canadian company or factory. But clusters of internationally competitive researchers attract graduate students, private partnerships, laboratories, patents, startups and additional researchers. Scientific capacity compounds. Why this belongs in a sovereignty strategy Industry Minister Mélanie Joly made the government’s framing unusually explicit Thursday. She said Canada is expanding research capacity in fields vital to both prosperity and sovereignty and contrasted that approach with parts of the world that are turning away from science. The word sovereignty is important. In the current Canadian debate it usually invokes tariffs, borders, defence procurement or dependence on the United States. But technological sovereignty begins much earlier in the chain. A country that cannot develop advanced materials, design medicines, understand artificial intelligence, build energy systems or train specialists becomes dependent on countries that can. Canada’s traditional vulnerability is not a shortage of educated people. It is the difficulty of retaining them and turning Canadian research into Canadian commercial scale. The enormous American market, higher salaries, venture capital and globally dominant universities have long pulled Canadian talent south. The new program cannot erase those structural advantages. But it can exploit an unusual moment in which the American system is creating disadvantages of its own. Academic freedom, predictable grant funding, immigration policy, political stability and confidence that a research program will survive changes in government all influence where scientists build careers. If American researchers begin to believe those conditions are more reliable elsewhere, Canada has an opportunity that may not last indefinitely. The geopolitical inversion That makes Thursday’s announcement especially striking against the backdrop of the Canada-U.S. confrontation. Washington’s economic strategy is explicitly intended to draw investment and production into the United States. Tariffs are being used to make foreign production more expensive and American production more attractive. Yet in scientific research, Canada is now demonstrating the opposite dynamic: instability in the United States is helping another country attract scarce high-value human capital. The contrast with the auto sector is revealing. Honda has warned that uncertainty over the future of North American trade rules could affect its plans for another assembly plant. Scientific researchers are making a different kind of location decision, but the underlying variable is similar: institutions invest where they believe the operating environment will remain stable enough to justify long-term commitments. A laboratory can take years to build. A research team can take longer. A scientist moving a career, family and graduate program across an international border is making a bet about where the next decade of work can be done. Forty-eight U.S.-based senior researchers have now made that bet on Canada. Canada is not only recruiting Americans The story should not be reduced to a raid on the United States. Sixteen of the first 64 senior researchers are coming from 12 other countries, including the United Kingdom, Germany, China and Japan, according to Bloomberg. The federal government says the entire cohort represents 13 countries. That wider reach fits the broader direction of Canadian policy. Ottawa is trying to diversify trade, defence relationships, industrial partnerships and capital beyond an overwhelming dependence on one neighbour. Talent can be diversified in exactly the same way. A research system connected deeply to Europe, Asia and the United States gives Canada more resilience than one dependent on a single pipeline. International researchers also bring their existing collaborations with them. A scientist moving from Britain or Germany does not sever every European relationship; a researcher arriving from Japan does not lose every Japanese collaborator. Canadian institutions become nodes in those networks. The next test: keeping the value in Canada The program’s success cannot ultimately be measured by the number of professors who cross the border. The harder question is what Canada does with them after they arrive. Canada has repeatedly generated excellent research and then watched intellectual property, companies and talent migrate elsewhere during commercialization. If the new chairs create breakthroughs that are patented abroad, financed abroad and manufactured abroad, the sovereignty benefit will be limited. The federal program attempts to address that problem by requiring connections with industry, government and society and by emphasizing research that can be applied or commercialized. But the surrounding ecosystem matters: venture capital, procurement, intellectual-property policy, domestic customers, scale-up financing and Canadian industrial capacity will determine how much of the downstream value remains here. This is therefore best understood as one part of a much larger industrial strategy. Recruiting a world-leading specialist in advanced materials is more valuable if Canadian battery, semiconductor, aerospace and defence companies can use the resulting knowledge. Recruiting a biotechnology pioneer is more strategically valuable if Canadian firms can manufacture and commercialize the resulting therapies. Building AI expertise matters more if Canadian companies and public institutions become sophisticated buyers and developers rather than merely suppliers of talent to foreign technology giants. A window that may not stay open Canada has tried variations of international talent recruitment before. What distinguishes the present moment is the combination of money, speed, geopolitical disruption and explicit strategic intent. The government is not hiding the opportunity. Its December announcement described a “historic opportunity” to recruit global talent. Thursday’s first results suggest Canadian universities have been able to convert that opportunity into actual appointments faster than might have been expected. There are reasons for caution. Researchers can move again. Governments can cut funding. Universities can fail to integrate large new programs. Canada’s chronic commercialization problem remains unresolved. And a successful American political or institutional correction could quickly restore the United States’ extraordinary gravitational pull. But scientific leadership is cumulative. A prominent researcher attracts students. Students become researchers and founders. Laboratories attract grants and corporate partners. Discoveries create intellectual property. Successful clusters draw more talent. If Canada can establish enough of those cycles now, some of the advantage can persist even after the geopolitical moment changes. The larger Canadian response Canada’s confrontation with the United States is often discussed as though the country has only two choices: obtain favourable access to the American market or suffer the consequences of losing it. The research initiative illustrates a third response. Canada can use the disruption itself to accumulate assets. Those assets can be physical — factories, mines, ports, pipelines, data centres and defence plants. They can be institutional — European procurement access, trade agreements and new security relationships. And they can be human: scientists, engineers, physicians, economists and research teams whose knowledge will shape technologies that do not yet exist. The first wave of the Global Impact+ strategy is therefore more than an education announcement. It is an industrial-policy announcement, a talent-policy announcement and, in the government’s own language, a sovereignty announcement. For generations, Canadians have watched some of their brightest people head south because the United States offered opportunities Canada could not match. At least for this moment, Canada has found a way to reverse part of that equation. And the most remarkable number in Thursday’s announcement is not $541 million. It is 48. Sources and methodology: Government of Canada, Aug. 27, 2026 announcement of the first Eddie Goldenberg Research Chairs and Canada
Current page · August 27, 2026

Norway’s European bargain is not the American bargain Washington tried to force on Canada

Norway’s European bargain is not the American bargain Washington tried to force on Canada Canada at War · Analysis · Published 27 August 2026 · 1:40 p.m. ET Norway’s European bargain is not the American bargain Washington tried to force on Canada Analysis · about 2,000 words The…

Norway’s European bargain is not the American bargain Washington tried to force on Canada Current page August 27, 2026 Norway’s European bargain is not the American bargain Washington tried to force on Canada Canada at War · Analysis · Published 27 August 2026 · 1:40 p.m. ET Norway’s European bargain is not the American bargain Washington tried to force on Canada Analysis · about 2,000 words The… Norway’s European bargain is not the American bargain Washington tried to force on Canada Canada at War · Analysis · Published 27 August 2026 · 1:40 p.m. ET Norway’s European bargain is not the American bargain Washington tried to force on Canada Analysis · about 2,000 words The comparison with Norway is useful precisely because it stops a false choice. Canada does not have to choose between complete economic separation from its largest partner and accepting any demand attached to a tariff threat. Norway is one of the most integrated non-members of the European Union. It applies a large body of single-market law, accepts common competition and state-aid rules, participates in procurement disciplines and has regulatory obligations that Canada does not have under CUSMA. But Norway has also preserved the power to protect the parts of its economy that it regards as politically and geographically essential — above all agriculture and fisheries. That distinction matters in the Canada–United States dispute. Washington’s recent approach has not been a single normal trade negotiation. It has mixed legal CUSMA disputes with unilateral pressure: a 10 per cent Section 301 tariff on Canadian goods in the 2026 action; 50 per cent tariffs on steel and aluminum; 50 per cent Section 338 tariffs on specified Canadian imports; and the continuing possibility of separate measures aimed at autos or at goods that do not qualify under CUSMA rules of origin. Alongside those tariffs are demands and complaints about Canadian dairy quota administration, provincial alcohol systems, digital-services taxation, cultural and digital regulation, procurement and industrial policy. Some of those matters are legitimate subjects for negotiation. That is what trade agreements are for. The problem is the format: broad punitive tariffs, including on goods covered by an existing North American agreement, are used to make unrelated Canadian policy choices negotiable under deadline pressure. Norway’s relationship with Europe is demanding, but it has a different constitutional logic. It is a reciprocal rules-based arrangement with known carve-outs, joint institutions and dispute procedures. It does not make Norway’s farm policy, resource policy or democratic choices automatically available as a price for keeping its industrial exports out of a tariff war. The central comparison Issue U.S. pressure or existing Canada–U.S. rule Norway’s arrangement with Europe What the comparison shows Industrial goods CUSMA normally provides duty-free trade for qualifying goods, but 2026 Section 301 action set a 10% tariff on Canadian goods unless exempted; Section 338 measures covered selected goods at 50%. EEA-origin industrial goods are generally tariff-free inside the EEA, subject to origin rules. Both arrangements integrate industry; Norway’s access is more rule-bound once conditions are met. Steel and aluminum U.S. Section 232 tariffs were raised from 25% to 50%; derivative products can carry 50% on non-U.S. metal content. No equivalent Europe-wide punishment tariff is built into Norway’s EEA access. Norway still applies EEA product and competition rules. Sectoral national-security tariffs can override normal access in the U.S. relationship. Autos and origin CUSMA requires North American content, labour-value rules and North American steel/aluminum purchasing for preferential treatment; non-qualifying goods have faced added U.S. duties. Norway is part of the EU single market for industrial goods, but must incorporate relevant EEA rules. Norway accepts deeper regulation in return for predictability; Canada has a treaty but faces unilateral override risk. Dairy and basic agriculture Canada retained supply management but granted U.S. dairy TRQs, ended classes 6 and 7, removed tariffs on whey and margarine, and faces repeated U.S. challenges to quota allocation. Agriculture is explicitly outside EEA free circulation. EU–Norway farm access is negotiated separately under Article 19, with product-specific quotas and concessions. Norway proves that intimate regional trade does not require surrendering dairy protection. Dairy protection level Inside Canadian TRQs imports receive preferential access; above quota, Canada keeps high over-quota tariffs. Norway also uses high tariffs on sensitive farm goods including dairy, while granting low- or zero-tariff import quotas under WTO, EEA and EFTA arrangements. The two countries use the same basic policy instrument: managed access, not unrestricted dairy trade. Alcohol U.S. complaints have treated provincial alcohol distribution and U.S. product access as part of wider bargaining. Norway retains a state alcohol retail monopoly and domestic alcohol policy within the European legal framework. Europe has not required Norway to dismantle its distinctive alcohol model as a condition of market access. Digital taxation and regulation The U.S. objected to Canada’s digital-services tax; Canada repealed it in 2025 as talks resumed. U.S. complaints also extend to digital and cultural regulation. Norway adopts much EU internal-market and digital legislation through the EEA process, but does so through its own constitutional machinery and EEA institutions. Norway accepts constraints, but through standing obligations rather than tariff blackmail. Procurement and state aid U.S. concerns include market access, procurement and subsidies; tariff relief has been discussed alongside concessions in unrelated areas. EEA includes common procurement and state-aid disciplines, overseen for EFTA states by the EFTA Surveillance Authority. Norway’s limits are institutional and reciprocal, not discretionary demands made in a crisis. Fisheries and resources Canada–U.S. trade has repeatedly intertwined resources, energy and strategic supply chains with tariff leverage. Fisheries, like agriculture, are outside EEA free circulation; Norway keeps a separate negotiating position. Carve-outs can protect sectors central to national identity and regional economies. Dispute settlement Canada can use CUSMA panels and other legal routes, but the U.S. has imposed measures while disputes continue. EEA has the EFTA Surveillance Authority and EFTA Court on the EFTA side, designed to maintain homogeneous rules. Institutions do not eliminate conflict, but they reduce the ability of one side to rewrite terms by threat. Norway’s dairy answer: protection, but negotiated access Norway is not a free-market exception to Europe. Its agriculture is expensive to sustain because of climate, terrain, distance and a political determination to maintain farming across the country. The Norwegian government states plainly that high tariffs limit imports of goods it considers sensitive; it specifically describes low- or zero-tariff quotas created through WTO, EEA and EFTA agreements. Dairy is among the protected sectors. The European Commission makes the legal position equally clear. Norway benefits from the EU single market through the EEA, but agriculture and fisheries are exempt from free circulation. Agricultural liberalization is handled separately under Article 19 of the EEA Agreement. The current Article 19 arrangement entered into force in 2018. It provides preferential access and negotiated quotas; it does not abolish Norway’s farm border or force it to adopt the EU’s Common Agricultural Policy. This is the first lesson for Canada. A country can accept extremely close economic integration while maintaining a defended agricultural settlement. Norway pays a price for this. Food is often expensive. Farmers receive substantial support. European exporters complain about barriers. But those are political choices made through Norwegian institutions, not evidence that Norway has somehow failed the European project. Canada’s supply-management system works through a similar architecture: controlled domestic production, tariff-rate quotas for imports and very high tariffs above quota. CUSMA did not abolish it. It added access for U.S. dairy products and changed elements of Canadian milk policy. The continuing fight has been about how Canada allocates quota access — including the previous reservation of pools for processors — not about a treaty clause ordering Canada to eliminate supply management altogether. A panel found Canada offside on one element in 2021; that is what a rules-based dispute process is supposed to do. It decides the particular obligation, rather than turning a disagreement into a demand for an entire domestic system. That is why it is misleading when U.S. political rhetoric treats Canadian dairy tariffs as proof that all Canadian trade is unfair. Norway maintains its own highly protected dairy and farm market even while selling industrial goods into Europe with deep single-market access. The relevant question is not whether Canada’s over-quota dairy tariffs are high — they are. The question is whether its treaty partners received the negotiated quota access they bargained for, and whether disputes are resolved by the agreement rather than by punishing unrelated workers in steel, autos or forestry. The tariff asymmetry The American tariff package is particularly revealing. The U.S. has applied several different legal instruments at once. The 2026 Section 301 action imposed 10 per cent on Canadian goods, subject to exemptions. Separate Section 232 measures put steel and aluminum at 50 per cent, with different treatment for some derivatives. A White House July fact sheet described Section 338 proclamations imposing 50 per cent on different specified Canadian products, including consumer goods, regardless of USMCA origin. Earlier reciprocal-tariff policy also described a 25 per cent additional duty on non-USMCA qualifying Canadian goods, with 10 per cent for certain Canadian energy resources and potash. These rates should not be collapsed into a single number. They have different legal bases, product coverage, exemptions and effective dates. But their political effect is cumulative: a Canadian business cannot safely assume that meeting CUSMA origin rules will protect it from every U.S. tariff action. That uncertainty is itself a form of leverage. Norway’s EEA bargain contains obligations Canada may not want. It imports a large share of EU law without a Norwegian vote in the European Parliament or Council. Its practical sovereignty is constrained by market rules and the need to maintain legal homogeneity. Critics call this “rule-taking,” and they have a real point. Norway is not a model of untouched national autonomy. But the contrast remains substantial. The EEA makes the constraints legible. Norway knows the areas where the single market applies; it knows agriculture and fisheries are outside the basic free-circulation rule; and it has EFTA institutions that police compliance. The United States’ current approach has made market access contingent not only on treaty compliance but on the political mood in Washington and the next list of unilateral tariff actions. What Canada should take from Norway — and what it should not Canada should not try to copy the EEA wholesale. Norway’s geography, energy wealth, relationship with the EU and long institutional history are different. Nor should Canada pretend that a deal with Europe would be costless: closer access may require more alignment on product standards, competition, procurement, state aid, climate-related rules and dispute institutions. The usable lesson is more modest and more important. A serious trade partnership distinguishes between broad mutual market access and a short list of explicitly negotiated sovereign choices. Norway’s agricultural and fisheries carve-outs do not make it anti-European. They are part of the bargain that makes deep integration politically sustainable. Canada should seek the same maturity in any future framework: broad access for industry and services; transparent, reciprocal rules; concrete strategic-procurement and investment cooperation; and clearly protected space for supply management, culture, provincial constitutional responsibilities and resource policy. The immediate Canada–U.S. task is also narrower than the rhetoric. Canada should insist that every American complaint be separated: dairy quotas, alcohol distribution, digital tax, autos, steel, aluminum, procurement and cultural regulation. For each, Ottawa should identify the treaty text, the claimed breach, the available panel process, the specific tariff threat and the specific concession requested. A country cannot defend sovereignty by refusing to negotiate; it defends sovereignty by refusing to let one tariff threat convert every domestic institution into a bargaining chip. Norway’s example shows that there is no contradiction between trading closely with a larger bloc and retaining a protected dairy sector, a distinct alcohol system and politically defined control over sensitive industries. The real choice is between negotiated interdependence and coercive dependence. Canada’s next trade framework — with Europe, the United States or both — should be judged by whether it makes that difference visible in law. Source note Key factual sources: Norwegian Government, “Import duties for agricultural products”; European Commission, EEA agriculture and trade pages; EFTA’s description of agricultural treatment in EFTA agreements; Global Affairs Canada CUSMA and trade briefing material; USTR/White House tariff notices and fact sheets. Tariff instruments must be checked again against the relevant customs notices before publication because product lists and exemptions can change.
Current page · August 27, 2026

China’s Reaction to the Canada–U.S. Trade-Talk Collapse

A 2,000-word report on Chinese reporting and commentary after Canada–U.S. trade talks collapsed in August 2026.

China’s Reaction to the Canada–U.S. Trade-Talk Collapse Current page August 27, 2026 A 2,000-word report on Chinese reporting and commentary after Canada–U.S. trade talks collapsed in August 2026. Reporting and analysis · 2,000 words · Updated 26 August 2026 The collapse of Canada–U.S. trade talks has become far more than a North American business story in Chinese media. Within days, Chinese coverage moved from straightforward accounts of the negotiating breakdown to a layered argument about sovereignty, American power and the value of resistance. Canada is being presented not as a new Chinese ally, and not as a country without differences with Beijing, but as a striking example: even a Five Eyes and NATO partner can conclude that compliance with Washington does not guarantee security. The basic Chinese account The facts of the rupture have been laid out most systematically by Xinhua , China’s official news agency. Its August 22 explanatory report says Prime Minister Mark Carney halted the talks, recalled Canada’s negotiating team and announced equivalent counter-tariffs, scheduled to begin September 8, after the United States imposed 50-per-cent tariffs on about US$20 billion in Canadian goods. It gives readers a clear account of Canada’s argument: the late U.S. demands were unfair, economically illogical and, above all, an intrusion on Canadian sovereignty. Xinhua highlighted the three objections Carney set out publicly: Washington sought limits on Canada’s ability to conclude other trade arrangements; it sought constraints touching Canada’s protection of language, culture and sovereignty; and it proposed auto-sector terms Canada regarded as profoundly unfair. This was a significant editorial choice. A short dispatch could have described an argument over tariffs. By putting sovereignty beside the commercial dispute, China’s national news agency made the case legible in the terms most resonant in Chinese political language. The same account emphasized Canada’s rare internal unity: Conservative leader Pierre Poilievre, provincial premiers and the federal government were all reported as rejecting a bad deal. It also reported U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer’s position that there were no new talks planned. These details do not make the report an editorial. But together they establish a frame: Canada did not simply fail to secure an exemption; it decided the price of a deal had become unacceptable. From reporting to argument The more openly interpretive coverage arrived quickly. A Sina Finance analysis said the breakdown exposed the limits of Trump’s aggressive trade strategy. It described a negotiating model in which access to the U.S. market is used to obtain concessions, market opening and sometimes commitments to invest in America. The argument is not merely that this is tough bargaining. It is that bargaining structured in this way is unequal from the outset: a partner is asked to yield in order to avoid a barrier Washington created unilaterally. That distinction matters. Chinese coverage is not neutral about the dispute, and readers should not mistake it for an independent Canadian account. It serves an established Beijing critique of U.S. unilateralism. Still, it is useful evidence of how the story is being narrated to a very large Chinese audience. Canada’s refusal is treated as a test case for whether American leverage works automatically when aimed at a close ally with a deeply integrated economy. The most vivid formulation came from a Global Times editorial , later republished widely: “加拿大发起‘中国式反击’，这是一记重锤” — “Canada launches a ‘China-style counterattack’: this is a heavy blow.” The correction is important: this is a Global Times editorial, not a 163.com editorial. Global Times is a party-affiliated nationalist newspaper, and its language should be read as an authoritative advocacy voice, not as a neutral wire service or a direct statement of policy. Its claim is that “China-style” does not mean something uniquely Chinese. It lists three elements: reciprocal response, defence of sovereignty and recourse to rules. The editorial argues that Canada’s action shows alliance status confers no exemption from tariff pressure. “Today Canada, tomorrow the European Union, the day after Japan and South Korea,” it says. That is the strategic message: American trade coercion, in this telling, is not targeted at rivals alone and can create a broader demand for strategic autonomy among U.S. partners. The China factor is no longer subtext China is explicit in the coverage for two reasons. First, one of Carney’s stated objections was a proposed restriction on Canada’s freedom to reach trade agreements with other countries. Chinese outlets naturally read that through the current Canada–China relationship. Canada and China signed a Canada–China economic co-operation roadmap in January, covering trade dialogue and co-operation across eight areas. In that context, Chinese commentary sees an American attempt to police Canada’s future choices, whether China was named in the room or not. Second, Greer’s reported comparison of the People’s Republic of China and Canada as the only countries to retaliate has been embraced in Chinese reporting. In Washington it was intended as criticism. In the Chinese narrative it becomes evidence that a close American partner has reached the same practical conclusion China did: unilateral pressure is met by a reciprocal answer. The point is not that Canada has joined China geopolitically. Canada remains in Five Eyes and NATO and has deep disagreements with Beijing. The point is that the same U.S. instrument can produce similar defensive conduct in radically different countries. This helps explain the strength of the rhetoric. The Global Times editorial says Canada’s countermeasure revises the story of who is protecting rules and who is disrupting them. That is a propagandistic conclusion, but it reveals the strategic reward China sees in the episode. A successful Canadian refusal would not only advance Canada’s interests; it would make the claim that resistance is futile less believable to other U.S. partners. Expert commentary: admiration mixed with calculation A more analytical version appears in a commentary by Sun Xingjie, published by ZAKER and syndicated by Sina under the headline “Canada begins copying China’s homework.” Sun calls Carney one of the few Western leaders willing to confront Trump directly. He argues that China’s earlier, measured countermeasures offered a usable model and that Canadian reciprocity proves a U.S. trade war does not distinguish reliably between allies and competitors. 如果加拿大能够迫使美国作出让步，其他的盟国大概率也会效仿，这对于美国的权威将是重大的冲击。 “If Canada can force the United States to make concessions, other allies will probably follow; that would be a major blow to U.S. authority.” That is not disinterested admiration. It is alliance analysis. Sun describes Canada as America’s largely undefended military ally and major trading partner, then argues that trade conflict between them signals a change in the international order. Whether one accepts that conclusion or not, it is a concise expression of what Chinese strategic commentators are looking for: fissures in the assumption that U.S. alliances automatically create economic alignment. Other Chinese reporting has taken a more practical angle. A Yangcheng Evening News analysis asked what cards Canada actually holds. It pointed to Canadian public exhaustion with threats and humiliating rhetoric, the political salience of the “51st state” language, and the limited scope of the immediate tariff target compared with Canada’s overall U.S.-bound exports. That is a more concrete explanation for Carney’s room to resist than a simple tale of national courage. Sovereignty is the emotional centre The common thread is 主权 — sovereignty. Chinese writers use it to connect trade terms to Canada’s linguistic and cultural protections, its freedom to negotiate with third countries and its basic standing as an independent state. On Chinese platforms, a common phrase for perceived external overreach is “干涉内政” , interference in internal affairs. It carries heavy historical and political weight. Applying it to Canada makes an immediate emotional bridge: if even a prosperous Western democracy can be pressed in this manner, the problem is presented as the behaviour of the stronger state, not the nature of its target. This is why some commentary reaches beyond economics. Sun wrote that the trade war had risen to the level of a struggle for national independence. Global Times calls Canada’s response a move toward strategic autonomy. These are analytical and political claims, not settled facts. Canada has not abandoned its alliance with the United States, and no serious reading should confuse trade retaliation with a rupture in defence or intelligence co-operation. Yet the language is meaningful because it asks Chinese readers to see Canada through a historical “resistance to bullying” narrative central to Chinese public discourse. Official Beijing: conspicuously restrained There is an important limit to the story. As of August 26, this review found no fresh, specific Foreign Ministry or Ministry of Commerce statement addressing the collapse itself. That absence should not be inflated into a coded announcement. Chinese official silence can simply reflect a decision not to insert the government directly into a fast-moving bilateral dispute involving two other countries. The context, however, is favourable to engagement. In January, China’s Commerce Ministry described the new roadmap as a broad economic co-operation framework. And the Canada China Business Council’s 2025–26 survey found 82 per cent of respondents saw Ottawa’s recalibrated China approach as positive for business; 86 per cent ranked China a top or number-one priority market; and 68 per cent planned to expand operations there. Those figures are a survey of interested businesses, not a forecast or a national consensus. They do show why Chinese commercial reporting sees a Canada–U.S. rupture as creating more room for Canada–China trade. What China is really saying China’s media reaction is both sympathetic and self-interested. Xinhua has presented the Canadian case in careful, sovereignty-centred detail. More political outlets have elevated it into proof that reciprocal resistance is legitimate, strategically effective and increasingly necessary even for U.S. allies. The official government has not publicly claimed Canada as a partner in a shared confrontation. The stronger claim comes from commentators: Canada has discovered that preserving choice, dignity and leverage may require saying no to Washington. That is the message China wants other countries to hear. Primary reporting and reading This report distinguishes factual reporting from editorial argument. Chinese-language quotations are translated for readability; links lead to the original publications. Xinhua: Why the Canada–U.S. trade talks collapsed Global Times editorial: Canada’s “China-style counterattack” Sina Finance: limits of an aggressive U.S. trade strategy ZAKER: Sun Xingjie’s commentary China Ministry of Commerce: Canada–China economic co-operation roadmap Canada China Business Council survey In Short Chinese discourse about Canada standing up to the U.S. is overwhelmingly sympathetic, often admiring of Carney’s toughness, and frequently self-referential — using Canada’s situation to validate China’s own past choices. The dominant storyline is that Canada finally learned what China already knew: conceding to Trump’s demands only invites more demands, and dignity can sometimes require walking away from the table.

The next Canada-EU relationship is already being built.

Prime Minister Mark Carney says Canada will begin discussions with the European Union this fall on a much stronger and deeper security and economic partnership. But several components of that relationship are already operational. Canada is the first non-European country admitted…

The next Canada-EU relationship is already being built. Current page August 27, 2026 Prime Minister Mark Carney says Canada will begin discussions with the European Union this fall on a much stronger and deeper security and economic partnership. But several components of that relationship are already operational. Canada is the first non-European country admitted… Prime Minister Mark Carney says Canada will begin discussions with the European Union this fall on a much stronger and deeper security and economic partnership. But several components of that relationship are already operational. Canada is the first non-European country admitted to the EU’s Security Action for Europe, or SAFE, defence-procurement instrument. The Council of the EU formally concluded the participation agreement in June, giving Canadian companies and Canadian-origin products a route into qualifying common European defence procurement. The relationship has already produced a concrete procurement: Montréal-based Marconi Technologies is to build Canadian-made tactical radios for Poland’s Cyber Command, drawing on nearly 100 Canadian suppliers. Canada has also launched negotiations to purchase Italian M-346 advanced jet trainers, while France, Germany, Italy and South Korea have signalled critical-mineral stockpiling partnerships with Canada. These are important precedents because they move Canada-Europe relations beyond conventional tariff policy. CETA opened markets. The next phase is increasingly about whether Canadian companies can participate in European industrial strategy — procurement, defence production, technology, critical minerals and strategic supply chains — and whether European companies receive comparable opportunities in Canada. The sovereignty logic Carney’s diversification strategy does not assume Europe can replace the United States. It aims to create enough alternative economic depth that Canada has choices. SAFE illustrates the principle: Canadian defence firms gain another major market and Europe gains a trusted non-European supplier base. The same logic could extend to energy, AI, nuclear technology, research, strategic infrastructure and procurement. The more deeply Canada is connected to several large markets, the less any single market can dictate the terms of Canadian economic policy. That is why the coming Canada-EU discussions deserve to be watched for language that goes beyond “free trade.” The significant question will be mutual access to industrial policy: whether Canadian firms can increasingly qualify for European subsidies, procurement and strategic programmes and whether European firms receive reciprocal treatment in Canada. The Norway model shows how far European economic integration can theoretically go without EU membership. Canada is nowhere near that model today. But SAFE proves that bespoke forms of insider access can be negotiated. The architecture of a deeper relationship is no longer entirely hypothetical. Sources: Council of the European Union SAFE decision, June 15, 2026; Prime Minister of Canada statements on G7 defence and critical-mineral partnerships and Aug. 22 trade-policy remarks.

U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer frames the episode differently. He says Washington had concerns and discussed them, but showed substantial flexibility and never regarded the issue as remotely important enough to sacrifice a good agreement. The disagreement over what happened remains. The more important forward-looking sentence came from LeBlanc: Ottawa is inviting further U.S. clarifications on other positions that could create the possibility of a mutually beneficial agreement respecting Canadian sovereignty. That is not a restart of negotiations. Greer says there is currently no open communication channel. But it is the clearest public indication yet of what a path back might look like: Washington retreats or clarifies disputed positions, and Ottawa decides whether enough has changed to return. Current page August 27, 2026 Mexican President Claudia Sheinbaum has revealed that when she spoke with Mark Carney shortly before the Canada-U.S. negotiations collapsed, the Canadian side appeared headed toward an agreement. Sheinbaum described the subsequent breakdown not as a rupture but as a desencuentro — a disagreement or falling-out — and said Mexico remains optimistic it can reach its own arrangement with Washington. Economy Minister Marcelo Ebrard is in the United States pursuing that objective. The contrast is becoming one of the most important subplots in North American trade. Mexico faces 50% U.S. tariffs on steel and aluminum and 25% tariffs on vehicles, yet is emphasizing negotiation and accommodation. Canada has suspended talks and announced matching retaliation after deciding the American package was not in its national interest. That does not mean Mexico is capitulating or Canada is permanently confrontational. Both governments are trying to protect deeply integrated economies with enormous U.S. exposure. But the two USMCA partners are providing a real-time test of different negotiating strategies toward the same administration. The outcome matters beyond tariffs. If Washington increasingly deals with Canada and Mexico separately rather than treating USMCA as an integrated three-country framework, the political foundations of North American economic integration could begin changing even before the formal review process settles the agreement’s future. Current page · August 27, 2026

Britain may be the hinge in Carney’s Atlantic Bridge.

The UK joins Canada under CPTPP on September 1, creating a practical bridge between Canada

Britain may be the hinge in Carney’s Atlantic Bridge. Current page August 27, 2026 The UK joins Canada under CPTPP on September 1, creating a practical bridge between Canada Five days from now, a quiet change in the architecture of Canadian trade takes effect. Britain’s accession to the Comprehensive and Progressive Agreement for Trans-Pacific Partnership, or CPTPP, enters into force with Canada on September 1. The United Kingdom has already been operating under CPTPP with most members since late 2024 and with Mexico since June. Canada completed ratification in July. From September 1, Canadian and British businesses will be able to choose between the existing bilateral Canada-UK Trade Continuity Agreement and CPTPP rules, depending on which arrangement works better for a particular transaction. That choice matters. The existing bilateral agreement already eliminates tariffs on 99 per cent of UK tariff lines, so this is not principally a story about another dramatic round of tariff cuts. CPTPP adds a second legal route into the British market and creates opportunities the bilateral agreement does not provide. What Canadians actually gain Global Affairs Canada says Britain’s CPTPP commitments add duty-free quota volumes for some Canadian meat products, immediate duty-free and quota-free access for sweetcorn, and preferential treatment for poultry and eggs. The rules-of-origin choice can also make it easier for Canadian businesses to construct supply chains across CPTPP countries and still qualify for preferential treatment. Services and mobility may prove at least as important. Canadian business visitors can stay in Britain for up to 90 days in a 12-month period under relevant commitments; investors can receive stays of up to a year to establish or manage investments; spouses of intra-company transferees receive work access; and some highly skilled Canadian professionals can remain for 12 months rather than six. Financial-services firms gain additional cross-border rights in areas including portfolio-management advice and electronic payments. Canadian suppliers also gain guaranteed procurement access across levels of British government under CPTPP commitments layered on top of existing WTO and bilateral arrangements. None of that by itself revolutionizes a Canada-UK relationship that was already unusually open. Its strategic importance comes from the network around it. The first European member of a Pacific bloc The CPTPP was conceived as a Pacific trade architecture. Its membership includes Canada, Japan, Australia, New Zealand, Mexico, Vietnam, Malaysia, Singapore, Brunei, Chile and Peru, with Costa Rica moving through accession. Britain is geographically the anomaly — and strategically the bridge. Prime Minister Mark Carney has explicitly said Canada is championing a connection between the CPTPP and the European Union. In his June “Atlantic Bridge” speech at Trinity College Dublin, Carney said such a link could create a trading bloc of 1.5 billion people. His February discussion with British Prime Minister Keir Starmer also specifically covered ongoing discussions between the EU and CPTPP. This is the bigger idea: Canada is not simply trying to replace lost American commerce with a collection of bilateral deals. Carney is trying to connect existing blocs so middle powers collectively have an economic scale that none possesses alone. Britain occupies a unique position in that project. It is now inside CPTPP but remains economically, geographically and institutionally tied to Europe. The UK is outside the EU customs union and Single Market, yet its largest trading relationships remain European and the Starmer government has sought closer practical co-operation with Brussels. Britain can therefore function as a demonstration that European economies can participate in the Pacific trade architecture without becoming Pacific countries. What an EU-CPTPP bridge might mean There is no signed EU-CPTPP mega-agreement, and Carney’s “bridge” should not be mistaken for one. The concept could develop incrementally: regulatory co-operation, compatible rules of origin, digital-trade standards, critical-mineral supply chains, energy security, investment, procurement and sectoral agreements that allow firms to operate across both networks with fewer barriers. The strategic potential is enormous. CPTPP members include major advanced economies and important emerging Asian markets. The EU is one of the world’s largest economic blocs. Linking them would create a rules-based economic counterweight at a moment when Washington is increasingly willing to condition access to the American market on political and industrial concessions. Canada is unusually well positioned to push the idea because it already has preferential trade agreements spanning both systems. CETA gives Canadian firms extensive access to the EU. CPTPP connects Canada across the Pacific. The UK now sits with Canada inside CPTPP while maintaining its own dense European connections. Rather than building an entirely new structure from nothing, Ottawa can try to make existing structures interoperate. Defence is already showing how this can work Trade is only one side of Carney’s strategy. Canada became the first non-European country formally admitted to participation in the EU’s SAFE defence-procurement instrument. Canadian companies and Canadian-origin products can participate in qualifying common procurement. Canada has already announced a Polish Cyber Command procurement of Canadian-built tactical radios through SAFE. That precedent is important. Europe has demonstrated that it is willing to open a strategic industrial instrument beyond the continent when political trust and mutual benefit justify it. Canada is simultaneously working with Britain on critical minerals, digital trade, sovereign AI infrastructure and proposals for new defence-financing mechanisms. The emerging model is therefore broader than “free trade.” It links markets, industrial policy, defence production, critical minerals, technology and security. That is precisely the terrain on which the Canada-U.S. confrontation is now being fought. Why September 1 matters The entry into force of UK-CPTPP relations with Canada is not a response invented after the latest breakdown with Washington. The accession process predates the current crisis. But timing changes strategic meaning. A trade architecture constructed over years is becoming operational just as Canada is urgently searching for ways to make economic diversification real. Canada will remain deeply tied to the United States. Neither Britain, Europe nor the Pacific can duplicate the advantage of a continental neighbour with enormous purchasing power. Carney’s objective is different: create enough alternative routes for Canadian capital, exports, technology and strategic industries that dependence on the United States no longer gives Washington overwhelming leverage. Britain could be central to that project precisely because it belongs completely to neither of the two worlds Carney wants to connect. It is European but outside the EU. It is Atlantic but now institutionally embedded in the Pacific trade pact. Canada is Atlantic, Pacific and Arctic at once. If Carney’s proposed bridge ever becomes a serious economic architecture, September 1 may eventually look less like an obscure treaty implementation date and more like one of the pieces that made the bridge possible. Primary sources: Government of Canada summary of the UK’s CPTPP accession; UK Department for Business and Trade CPTPP guidance; Prime Minister Carney’s June 13 “Atlantic Bridge” speech; PMO readout of the Carney-Starmer discussion; Council of the EU decision concluding Canada’s SAFE participation.
Current page · August 27, 2026

The American officials exercising U.S. authority inside Canada

American officials do not merely influence Canada from Washington. Under formal bilateral arrangements, U.S. Customs and Border Protection officers work physically inside Canada, screening travellers and goods bound for the United States before they leave Canadian territory.…

The American officials exercising U.S. authority inside Canada Current page August 27, 2026 American officials do not merely influence Canada from Washington. Under formal bilateral arrangements, U.S. Customs and Border Protection officers work physically inside Canada, screening travellers and goods bound for the United States before they leave Canadian territory.… American officials do not merely influence Canada from Washington. Under formal bilateral arrangements, U.S. Customs and Border Protection officers work physically inside Canada, screening travellers and goods bound for the United States before they leave Canadian territory. Preclearance began in Canada in the 1950s and has expanded substantially. U.S. officers operate at major Canadian airports, and Canada opened new U.S. CBP preclearance facilities at Billy Bishop Toronto City Airport and Vancouver’s Pacific Central rail station in 2026. The system has obvious practical advantages: travellers arrive in the United States already cleared, connections become easier and border processing can be faster. But the legal powers are real. Canadian legislation permits U.S. preclearance officers, within designated areas and subject to Canadian law, to conduct U.S. immigration and customs examinations. Federal Justice Department material describes powers involving examination, search and detention of documents stored on personal digital devices under prescribed thresholds. U.S. officers can detain a person in circumstances requiring Canadian police intervention, although they do not have Canadian peace-officer status and cannot themselves make a Canadian arrest. The arrangement is reciprocal. Canadian officers can perform Canadian preclearance functions in the United States, and Canada launched its first land preclearance operation there in 2025. Canadian government documents repeatedly stress that U.S. officers in Canada remain subject to Canadian law, including the Charter of Rights and Freedoms, the Canadian Bill of Rights and the Canadian Human Rights Act. The sovereignty question is therefore not whether these officials are secretly occupying Canadian territory. They are here because Canada agreed that they could be here. The question is whether agreements designed during an era of exceptional bilateral trust should be reviewed when the political assumptions underlying that trust have changed. That distinction matters. A serious sovereignty review should not turn lawful co-operation into conspiracy. It should ask whether the authorities granted remain proportionate, whether Canadian oversight is sufficient, whether data collected in Canada is adequately protected, whether reciprocal Canadian powers in the United States genuinely balance the arrangement and whether Canada retains practical freedom to modify or terminate systems that no longer serve its interests. The broader Canada-U.S. relationship contains many such layers: integrated border enforcement, policing co-operation, intelligence sharing and defence structures. Most were built because integration produced enormous benefits. But integration also creates dependence, and dependence becomes politically important when one partner begins openly using economic leverage against the other. Sovereignty Watch will examine those arrangements one by one — distinguishing legitimate bilateral co-operation from genuine vulnerabilities, and asking a simple question: in each case, does Canada still possess the effective ability to make and enforce its own choices? Sources: Public Safety Canada, Justice Canada, Transport Canada and Canada Border Services Agency material on the Preclearance Act and Canada-U.S. preclearance arrangements.
